The NDC 3.0 RD-2025 was developed under a participatory, multi-sectoral, transparent, inclusive and interactive with all relevant actors in the Dominican territory.

Presentation

The Dominican Republic presents the updated Nationally Determined Contribution 3.0 (NDC 3.0) RD - 2025) at a crucial moment for national climate action . Our country stands out for its integration progressive inclusion of the threat of climate change in key national planning instruments, including the National Development Strategy 2010-2030 , placing climate action as a structural pillar of sustainable development.

In the last decade, the Dominican Republic has faced hydrometeorological phenomena that affect increasingly impacting the population, ecosystems, and strategic economic sectors. The increase in Hurricanes, floods, droughts, and extreme events highlight the country's differentiated vulnerability. particularly due to its status as a small island developing state. This reality has reinforced the need for an instrument that coherently and clearly articulates national efforts in Mitigation, adaptation, financing, transparency, just transition, and protection of human rights. The NDC 3.0 RD - 2025 arises, as well as a comprehensive response to the mandate of the Paris Agreement and the commitments that the nation assumed under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change.

This document is based on the experience accumulated since the 2015 iNDC and the NDC 2.0 presented in 2020, incorporating lessons learned, advances in scientific analysis and a broad consultative process multisectoral. This update acknowledges the progress made in climate governance, institutional framework, data and planning, but it also identifies the persistent gaps that hindered the full implementation of the previous NDC.

The NDC 3.0 RD - 2025 expresses the country's willingness to contribute in a proportionate, fair, and responsible manner to global effort to limit the planet's temperature. Our contribution reaffirms the commitment to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 28% by 2030 and by 32% by 2035 compared to the scenario trend-based, while integrating a robust adaptation component based on needs and vulnerabilities of the territory. This ambition encompasses mitigation and adaptation actions ranging from the transformation of the energy system, sustainable mobility and waste management, to the protection of ecosystems, the water security, public health, resilient urban planning and coastal management.

Furthermore, the NDC highlights the importance of safeguarding fundamental human rights, with the integration of differential approaches that address the specific needs of women, people with Disability, early childhood and youth, rural and coastal communities. Mainstreaming these These approaches not only aim to highlight differentiated vulnerabilities, but also to recognize the key role of these groups as agents of change in building national resilience.

The process of updating NDC 3.0 RD - 2025 involved a group of key stakeholders, including public institutions, the private sector, academia, community organizations, and civil society, in order to to guarantee the representativeness and legitimacy of its content. This collaborative process reinforces the character national scope of the document and the importance of cooperation for its implementation.

Thus, through this NDC, the Dominican Republic reaffirms climate action as a national priority, and renews its commitment to continue strengthening it through political will, inter-institutional coordination, capacity building and mobilization of national and international financing.

Prologue

The Dominican Republic reaffirms its commitment to climate action by submitting its Contribution Nationally Determined Contribution 3.0 (NDC 3.0 RD-2025), an instrument that consolidates national efforts to to address the challenges associated with climate change and move towards a sustainable development model, resilient and low-emission. This document is based on the mandate of the Paris Agreement and on legislation current national legislation and the growing need to strengthen technical, institutional and the country's strategies to adapt to and mitigate the impacts of climate change, whose frequency and intensity have increased significantly in recent decades.

The submission of this NDC constitutes a decisive step in the country's climate trajectory, continuing the work carried out in the First, Second Nationally Determined Contribution, as well as in the recent Publication of the Biennial Transparency Report (BTR 1). As highlighted in climate reports national communications, including the First, Second, Third and Fourth National Communications, the Dominican Republic It faces vulnerabilities that manifest themselves in economic losses, damage to infrastructure, and pressure. on water resources and the degradation of ecosystems that sustain life and livelihoods. These Trends have reinforced the need to adopt more robust, integrated and linked climate planning to the development processes.

The NDC 3.0 RD-2025 emerges as a response to this challenge, articulating a coherent set of goals, policies and measures aimed at reducing emissions, strengthening resilience, promoting climate investment and consolidating transparency mechanisms that allow for rigorous monitoring of progress. In accordance with the international commitments undertaken, this update integrates cross-cutting considerations on human rights, gender equality, youth, early childhood, people with disabilities and justice climate, recognizing that the impacts of climate change are not evenly distributed, and that Certain population groups require differentiated attention. It also incorporates structural elements such as integration with the National Climate Transparency System, strengthening of the National System of Public investment and progress towards a national carbon market, ensuring that climate action is deployment within a solid, transparent and efficient institutional framework.

With NDC 3.0 RD-2025, the Dominican Republic expresses its willingness to continue collaborating with the community international, under the principle of common but differentiated responsibilities, in the global search for limiting global warming. This document is a testament to the country's commitment to a a safer and more sustainable future, reaffirming that climate action is not only an environmental imperative, but It is also a way to strengthen community resilience, economic competitiveness, and well-being. of present and future generations.

Preamble

The Dominican Republic's Nationally Determined Contribution 3.0 2025 (NDC 3.0 RD-2025) is developed in a global context in the face of the growing climate emergency. Sixth Assessment Report of the IPCC (AR6) and the first Global Stocktake of the Paris Agreement in 2023, underline that this decade is crucial to keeping global warming below 1.5°C compared to pre-industrial levels.

In this context, the Dominican Republic presents its NDC 3.0 RD-2025 as a manifestation of political will, technical coherence and national commitment to address the latent challenges posed by climate change.

Over the past two decades, the country has faced an increase in the frequency and intensity of events extreme weather events, primarily hurricanes, droughts, floods, and coastal erosion, have severely affected sectors key to the economy. The high dependence on climate-sensitive activities, such as tourism, agriculture, and Imported energy highlights the need to strengthen the country's structural and social resilience.

This vulnerability is accentuated by the country's island status, which exposes it disproportionately to extreme weather events, rising sea levels, and emerging phenomena such as the proliferation of Sargassum, which has repeatedly affected beaches and coastal ecosystems. Likewise, the Republic The Dominican Republic has been among the countries most affected by climate events, according to the According to the Germanwatch Institute's Global Climate Risk Index, in its 2026 report analyzing the period 1995–2004, the country ranks 37th out of 173 nations assessed. This position reflects its high exposure. structural and its limited capacity to respond to extreme weather events23.

NDC 2.0 (2020) represented a significant step forward by raising the mitigation target from 25% (conditional) to 27%. (mixed) and by consolidating a robust adaptation component. However, the process for its implementation It revealed institutional, technical, and financial challenges:

  • Human resource limitations in the institutions responsible for quantifying and reporting emissions results under MRV schemes.

  • Limited budgetary and territorial integration of adaptation measures.

  • Gaps in inter-institutional coordination and availability of sectoral data.

  • Challenges in obtaining and managing the necessary financing for the implementation of planned actions.

    NDC 3.0 RD-2025 responds to these lessons learned through a series of structural and methodological improvements, Among the most notable are:

  • The design of the NDC Subsystem within the National Climate Transparency System (SNTC).

  • Integrating climate action into the National Public Investment System (SNIP) and the Plan Climate Action Investment (PIAC-RD).

  • The update of National Greenhouse Gas Inventory (2022).

Furthermore, NDC 3.0 RD-2025 recognizes that the adverse effects of climate change are increasingly severe in the country, they require a response that effectively integrates respect, protection, and promotion of human rights.

The country has identified the following risks as priorities: hurricanes, prolonged droughts, Urban flooding and coastal erosion. To manage these issues, NDC 3.0 RD-2025 integrates measures from the Plan National Adaptation to Climate Change Plan (PNACC-RD), PIAC-RD and the National Land Use Plan Territorial, including:

  • Strengthening sanitation infrastructure in tourist areas;

  • the promotion of resilient urban regeneration processes;

  • integrated river basin management; and

  • The restoration of marine and coastal ecosystems.

The document emphasizes that the actions taken must contribute to reducing emissions and strengthening the resilience, generating green jobs and protecting biodiversity, consolidating climate action as a a pillar of national and regional well-being. Resilience is addressed not only through infrastructure, but also also from social protection, public health and food security policies, recognizing that the Climate action must be inclusive, transformative, and people-centered.

1. Strategic Introduction

The Dominican Republic reaffirms, with the Nationally Determined Contribution 3.0 2025 (NDC 3.0 RD-2025), its political commitment at the highest level to climate action, consistent with the Constitution, the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), Agreement from Paris, the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and the National Development Strategy 2030 (Law No. 1-12).

This update reinforces the country's view that climate action constitutes a policy of State and an engine of sustainable development, integrating mitigation, adaptation and the elements cross-cutting themes within a common framework for planning, investment, and monitoring. This approach seeks to guarantee benefits for all of society, with special attention to sectors and people in vulnerable situations of climate vulnerability.

The Dominican Republic, in its NDC 3.0 RD-2025, raises its climate ambition by committing to reduce its greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions by 28% by 2030, equivalent to 14,732.1 kt CO₂ eq, and by 32% by 2035, equivalent to 18,349.32 kt CO₂ eq, compared to the business-as-usual (BAU) scenario, using the base year 2022 as a reference.

Mitigation actions cover five sectors: Energy, Industrial Processes, and Product Use (IPPU), Agriculture, Forestry and Other Land Uses (UTCUTS) and Waste, prioritizing measures with a high impact on emissions reduction and socio-economic co-benefits, which represents progress significant toward its national goal. Achieving these mitigation objectives will require a Total estimated investment of USD 11.468 billion. Therefore, of the 28% reduction commitment, a 8.5% is unconditional, financed with national resources and other sources managed by the country, and 19.5% is conditional on external financing, technology transfer, creation and capacity building and additional international cooperation. The set of measures to Mitigation is distributed across 26 policies, actions and measures (PAMs) in the five sectors priority.

It also establishes measures for adaptation in the sectors most vulnerable to adverse effects. generated by climate change, which represent more than 35% of the national GDP, including primarily to the tourism, agriculture, water and energy sectors.

National climate action is taking place in an increasingly complex economic and geographical context. Challenging. The Dominican Republic has maintained sustained economic growth of around of 5% annually during the last decade and has established itself as one of the most dynamic economies of the Caribbean4. However, its status as a Small Island Developing State (SIDS) , places it among the most vulnerable countries in the world to extreme hydrometeorological phenomena, that recurrently affects infrastructure, ecosystems, food security and livelihoods life. As indicated above, NDC 3.0 RD-2025 incorporates a robust set of measures adaptation, with an estimated investment amount exceeding USD 12,251 million , distributed across 41 measures and 155 specific goals in seven priority sectors.

The Central Bank of the Dominican Republic (2024) and the Ministry of Finance and Economy (MHE) have indicated that the impacts of climate change could represent average losses equivalent to 0.9% of GDP annual, particularly in transport and housing infrastructure. In scenarios of phenomena In extreme weather events, these losses could reach up to 10% of the value of these infrastructures.

Therefore, NDC 3.0 RD-2025 is conceived under a framework that links mitigation and adaptation goals with its means of implementation, financing mechanisms and expected benefits.

This logical framework is articulated within the National System of Planning and Public Investment (SNIP) and is It aligns with the Dominican Republic's National Climate Change Adaptation Plan (PNACC-RD), the Plan of Investment for Climate Action (PIAC-RD) and the Climate Finance Strategy, ensuring the coherence between sectoral, territorial and fiscal planning.

The mitigation Climate change constitutes a central axis of NDC 3.0 RD-2025, oriented towards a transformation structural in the following sectors:

  • Energy and transport: accelerate the transition to renewable energy, modernize urban mobility and reduce dependence on fossil fuels.

  • Agriculture and LULUCF: promoting agroforestry systems, reducing deforestation and strengthening resilient food security.

  • Waste and circular economy: implementing Law No. 225-20 for the comprehensive management and co-processing of solid waste, with an emphasis on recycling and energy recovery. As well as Law No. 98-25 where the Extended Producer Responsibility scheme is expanded, promoting the circular economy.

  • Industrial Processes: promoting decarbonization and emissions reduction in industrial sectors.

The adaptation It acquires priority relevance, as it constitutes a fundamental pillar for the sustainability of national development. The prioritized measures are concentrated in the sectors water, agriculture, health, infrastructure, biodiversity, forests, tourism and ecosystems tero-marines with emphasis on:

  • Integrated management of river basins and water security in vulnerable regions.

  • Restoration of marine-coastal ecosystems and forests to strengthen natural protection against hurricanes, erosion and rising sea levels.

  • Climate-informed territorial planning, in accordance with the Law on Territorial Planning and Use of Soil (Law No. 368-22), to identify priority areas, critical infrastructure and ecosystems strategic.

  • Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) and Nature-based Solutions (NbS) to reduce vulnerabilities, increase resilience and create sustainable productive opportunities.

  • Strengthening the public health system by integrating climate variables into surveillance epidemiology and health risk management.

  • Tourism and coasts: promoting resilient infrastructure and restoration of marine-coastal ecosystems.

The goals of this NDC 3.0 strengthen a people-centered approach, with the aim of ensuring that Climate action must be more effective, fair, and implementable. This builds on the iNDC (2015) and NDC 2.0 (2020), the country reinforces the gender approach, youth, and rights as cross-cutting elements human rights, just transition, and Climate Empowerment Action (CEA). Furthermore, the following is strengthened: inclusion of early childhood, childhood and adolescence, as well as people with disabilities, recognizing the differentiated contributions and impacts of climate change on these groups.

Furthermore, NDC 3.0 integrates just transition as a cross-cutting element for planned management and includes sectoral transformations associated with mitigation and adaptation, through capacity building, new technologies, training for green jobs, active policies of labor market and social protection mechanisms, with attention to gender gaps and inequalities territorial. In addition, it incorporates disaster risk management as a cross-cutting lens. to reduce vulnerabilities, prevent the generation of new risks and strengthen the resilience of infrastructure, human settlements, ecosystems and essential services, articulating climate action with land use planning and public planning.

In this way, NDC 3.0 RD-2025 is not limited to emissions reduction commitments, but redefines the Climate action as a strategy for comprehensive development and social cohesion. The combination of these two The axes, mitigation and adaptation, as well as the cross-cutting elements, reinforce the coherence between the action climate and the country's economic and social development goals, ensuring that every climate measure generate tangible collateral benefits in health, employment, innovation and community well-being.

Finally, NDC 3.0 RD-2025 reaffirms that, despite emitting less than 0.1% of global GHGs, the The Dominican Republic responsibly assumes an active and supportive role in climate action5 The country links its climate strategy to the well-being of its population, the competitiveness of its economy, and to sustainable use of its resources and conservation of its ecosystems, moving towards a development model low carbon, resilient and fair.

Country Vision

The Dominican Republic views climate action as a fundamental pillar of its development model sustainable. Its strategic vision is based on the principles of coherence, integration, and alignment. with international frameworks such as the Paris Agreement and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and with national planning, investment and governance instruments.

The country aims to consolidate a resilient, low-emission, and socially inclusive economy, where the Mitigation, adaptation, and climate finance policies are implemented in a coordinated manner, guaranteeing measurable, sustainable and equitable results.

This vision recognizes that the transition to a climate-friendly economy requires simultaneously structural transformations in the productive sectors, institutional strengthening for the climate governance and access to predictable and affordable financial resources, enabling scaling up climate ambition without compromising social justice.

Guiding Principles of the Country Vision:

  1. Coherence and integrated planning: The NDC 3.0 RD-2025 is linked to the National Investment System Public (SNIP), ensuring that climate goals are integrated into sectoral plans, territorial and budgetary, and that climate action be part of the public investment cycle.

  2. Territorial and climate integration: In compliance with the Territorial Planning Law (No. 368-22), the identification of priority areas, critical infrastructures and ecosystems is promoted strategic, optimizing resources and minimizing adverse impacts on the territory and the population.

  3. International alignment and financing: The strategy is aligned with the SDGs, the Strategy National Climate Finance, green bond instruments, Payment for Climate schemes Environmental Services (PES), and the Integrated National Financing Frameworks (INFF), consolidating the link between financial sustainability and climate ambition.

  4. Risk management and resilience: Recognizing its status as a SIDS, the Dominican Republic promotes the strengthening of capacities in Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E), as well as the improvement of Climate governance and the training of national technical talent, as a basis for resilience structural and social.

2.1. Priority Sectors

The selection of priority sectors in NDC 3.0 RD-2025 was based on a comprehensive technical analysis which considered not only the current contribution of each sector to GHG emissions, but also its capacity to generate structural transformations aligned with national commitments and international. By focusing action on these sectors, NDC 3.0 RD2025 seeks to consolidate a an ambitious, effective, and coherent approach that maximizes the impact of mitigation measures and adaptation, and strengthen climate resilience in the country.

In that sense, the prioritization process starts from those defined in NDC 2.0, which They were updated and supplemented to reflect current dynamics in the country, as well as the advances in national and international climate policy. This process was based on an approach participatory (see Annex 5: Participatory Process ) which involved the public sector, private sector, and civil society, guaranteeing legitimacy and representativeness.

The following outlines the prioritization process, highlighting the arti- correlation between the cross-cutting themes that underpin national climate action. Likewise, in the Table 2 specifies the scope of the sectors and thematic areas that make up NDC 3.0 RD-2025.

Focus on reducing climate disaster risks, aimed at decreasing losses and damages.

Just Transition

Integrating just transition into governance, skills development and training for green jobs, promotion of active labor market policies and encouragement of green entrepreneurship and productive transformation.

3. National Context

3.1. Geographic and socioeconomic context

The Dominican Republic is a Caribbean island state located east of the island of Hispaniola, with an approximate area of 48,195.90 km² and a coastline of more than 1,527 km for the main coast and another 139 km for cays and islets, bordered by the Atlantic Ocean to the north and The Caribbean Sea to the south. It is bordered to the east by the Mona Passage and shares a land border with Haiti (276 km) to the west6. Due to its geographical position located in the route of tropical cyclones in the North Atlantic and the The country is frequently affected by the Great Atlantic Sargassum Belt. atmospheric phenomena such as storms and hurricanes78.

The territory has a rugged topography, with mountain ranges (including Pico Duarte) reaching 3,098 m above sea level), and depressions (with Lake Enriquillo approximately- (at 44 m below sea level) which generate strong altitudinal gradients contributing to the climatic, ecosystemic and productive diversity of the country.

Figure 2. Altitudinal ranges above sea level in the Dominican Republic. Source: Atlas of Statistics Environmental (ONE, 2024)6

The Dominican Republic has a tropical climate characterized by average annual temperatures of 25 to 28 °C. The large orographic variations generate notable differences, fluctuating between 26 and 28 °C for the Lower-lying areas, such as coastal zones, and ranging from 18 to 22 °C for higher altitude areas. Rainfall varies from approximately 900 mm to 2,200 mm/year by area, depending on the region, presenting the rainy season between the months of May and August9.

According to the Basic Demographic Characteristics Report of the X National Population and Housing Census10 The Dominican Republic had a population of 10,760,028 inhabitants in 2024 with a density of over 200 inhabitants/km² and a moderate growth trend. The population is mostly concentrated in certain areas urban areas such as the Santo Domingo Metropolitan Region and the Cibao Valley.

The Dominican economy is characterized by a predominance of the service sector, representing approximately 60%. of GDP, followed by the industrial sector at approximately 34% and the agricultural sector at around 6%.11. Sectors such as tourism, manufacturing in free trade zones, mining, construction, and mining services They are also key drivers of the country's development. The country has experienced growth in recent years. decades with a nominal GDP of around 124 billion USD in 2024 and a GDP per capita close to 10,876 USD12.

3.2. Climate Scenarios and Impacts

Climate change is presented as one of the most alarming environmental and development challenges. globally, with a high probability that human activities are the main cause of Global warming is driving recent climate changes, according to the Climate Change Service. According to the Copernicus Climate Change Conference13, 2024 was the warmest year on record, also marking the first time that the The average global temperature is more than 1.5°C above pre-industrial levels. This fact anticipates The projections regarding the critical threshold associated with the stability of the system have been considerably revised. global climate.

Although the effects of climate change vary by region, its impact is becoming increasingly evident worldwide, and projections indicate that the negative effects will continue intensifying14. In the case of the Dominican Republic, its geographical location, the physiography of its territory insularity, the high exposure of its population to hydrometeorological phenomena and the dependence on sectors sensitive sectors such as tourism, agriculture, and water resources place it among the most vulnerable countries to this phenomenon1516171819, occupying positions for several consecutive years among the top places in the Global Climate Risk Index20.

The most recent studies 212223 , which encompass both the analysis of historical trends and projections towards the end of the 21st century, They highlight an increase in temperatures and significant changes in rainfall patterns in the country. Specifically, the behavior of rainfall over the last century reveals two aspects Key. On the one hand, a reduction in rainfall is observed in the leeward areas of the main mountain ranges. mountainous regions21, that is, in typically drier regions, which would be subject to an increase in aridity and a greater pressure on water resources. On the other hand, historical trends also highlight a Intensification of rainfall, especially on the Caribbean coast21, with less frequent but more concentrated events, increasing the magnitude of flooding and landslides.

The available projections24, made using the CCSM4, FIOESM, MIROCS, MIROC-ESM, NorESM1-M models (which had better scores in representing the behavior of the 5 variables analyzed), They indicate a progressive reduction in precipitation under the higher emissions scenarios, reaching up to more than 20%, and even 30% in the south of the country, towards the end of the century under high scenarios emissions (RCP 8.5). In contrast, the temperature (maximum, minimum, and average) shows a sustained trend. upwards in all scenarios, with more marked increases below the RCP 8.5, where the maximum temperature It could increase between 3.2 and 3.8 °C and the minimum up to 3.3 °C. In the low emissions scenarios (RCP 2.6), The changes would be more moderate, with temperature increases close to 1°C and smaller reductions in rainfall (4–8 %). Due to rising sea levels, it is projected that the country could lose between 5 and 10% of its coastal area.24

These trends have direct effects on people and their well-being: they compromise safety food security due to the loss of agricultural productivity; they affect public health due to the proliferation of Vectors and diseases associated with extreme events threaten the water security of rural communities and urban areas; and they deteriorate essential infrastructure and housing, especially in informal settlements located in risk zones.

Recent studies indicate that, despite government efforts and commitment, climate change It will likely exacerbate some of the country's development challenges. Without adaptation measures, the projected impacts will affect economic activity, reducing the poverty and growth. By 2050, climate change is expected to reduce labor productivity and affects health, agricultural yields (which could fall by up to 30%), tourism, infrastructure and natural ecosystems such as forests and coastal areas. According to the models, the Climate-related GDP deviations could reach up to 16.7% by 2050, with more than 80% of the losses attributable to heat stress on the workforce, the increase in tropical storms, and the Decreased tourist demand. Furthermore, climate change will hinder poverty reduction: It estimates that by 2050 the poverty rate will be between 0.7 and 1.2 percentage points higher than in a scenario without [the government]. Climate change, which means that between 16 and 25% more people will live in poverty. Finally, the combination of the impacts of climate change, natural resource degradation and access Limited access to basic services is also expected to affect migration patterns, particularly among the most vulnerable populations.

In this context, the Dominican Republic chooses to lead with resilience, recognizing that adaptation is much more than a response to climate shocks, but rather a strategic opportunity to modernize the national economy, protect its natural heritage, and build stronger, fairer communities and equitable.

3.3. Institutional Arrangements, Governance and Legal Framework

The Dominican Republic, consistent with its commitment to safeguarding human rights, It recognizes and protects, constitutionally, the right to a healthy, balanced and adequate environment for the development, in a context of increasing climate risk.

The climate change governance framework in the Dominican Republic is based on a set of institutions and actors (State, civil society, private sector, local communities), mechanisms of coordination between multiple levels (national, sectoral, local) and regulatory frameworks that seek to guarantee The coherence, coordination, and effectiveness of actions to address climate change. This governance is based on in the mainstreaming of climate change in public policies, coordination interinstitutional and multi-stakeholder participation, including the incorporation of approaches cross-cutting issues (gender, human rights, ecological-territorial).

Climate governance in the Dominican Republic has evolved significantly in recent years, in response to the international commitments undertaken under the Paris Agreement and to the growing threats derived from climate change. This process has involved not only the strengthening of the legal framework and institutional, but also the progressive incorporation of participatory, inclusive and multisectoral approaches in the formulation and implementation of climate policies. The accumulated experience The implementation of NDC 2.0 offers valuable lessons that guide the construction of a more effective NDC 3.0. ambitious, equitable and effective.

The National Council for Climate Change and Carbon Market (CNCCMC) acts as the coordinating body of national climate policy, promoting the integration of climate change into the various sectors and levels of government, and coordinating the actions of public, private, and civil society entities. Its mandate includes the formulation of mitigation strategies and adaptation, as well as the promotion of a just and resilient transition.

The Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MMARN) exercises environmental leadership, in accordance with the Law No. 64-00, ensuring the conservation, regulation, planning and control of ecosystems and resources natural resources, providing technical, implementation and regulatory support to climate change policies in the related to the sustainable management of natural resources. As well as being responsible for the implementation and due monitoring of all Multilateral Environmental Agreements (MEAs) to which the country is a party.

The Ministry of the Presidency (MINPRE), with the approval of Law No. 45-25, has assumed the functions of Territorial planning integrates climate change into territorial planning and its alignment with the disaster risk management for national development planning, in accordance with Law No. 368-22.

The Ministry of Finance and Economy ensures the financial sustainability of exchange rate policies climate, integrating the climate variable into budget planning and mechanisms of climate finance.

The country has been developing a progressive institutional and regulatory framework, including plans and strategies national climate change adaptation and mitigation policies. However, the effective implementation of these Policies require strong alliances between public and private actors, academia, civil society, as well as the mobilization of local capacities to implement concrete actions.

Public sector governance on climate change is supported by ministries and other entities. sectoral institutions that have a leading role in increasing resilience and mitigating impacts of climate change depending on their functions and scope, such as the Ministry of Agriculture, the Ministry of Health and Social Assistance (MISPAS), Ministry of Education (MINERD), Ministry of Higher Education, Science and Technology (MESCyT), Ministry of Public Works and Communications (MOPC), the Ministry of Housing, Habitat and Buildings (MIVHED), Ministry of Tourism (MITUR), Ministry of Defense (MIDE), the Ministry of Energy and Mines (MEM), among others.

The private sector, academia, civil society organizations and communities all have a role to play predominant in the implementation of climate change policies, as well as in the efforts of capacity building, research and dissemination.

The Dominican private sector has a crucial role to play in both reducing emissions and Sectoral adaptation to the effects of climate change. Many companies, from large corporations to Small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) integrate sustainability criteria into their operations and supply chains. supply. This includes investments in energy efficiency, adoption of renewable energy, management of waste and product design with a smaller environmental footprint, such as the companies gathered in ECORED, as well as Industrial parks like Duarte Park, certified as an eco-park and supported by the Organization of the United Nations Industrial Development Organization (UNIDO) for the management of five treatment plants water and solar energy, promoting the circular economy and environmental responsibility.

The Dominican academy plays a fundamental role in the generation of scientific and technical knowledge necessary to understand the impacts of climate change and design effective education responses, Information and dissemination. Universities such as the Pontifical Catholic University Madre y Maestra (PUCMM), Ibero-American University (UNIBE), Pedro Henríquez Ureña National University (UNPHU), Institute The Technological Institute of Santo Domingo (INTEC), the Autonomous University of Santo Domingo (UASD), and others have programs that contribute to environmental sustainability, and specific programs that include climate change in undergraduate, postgraduate and research programs.

Civil society organizations (CSOs), including community groups, act as bridges between the Public policy and local realities. Their role is crucial to ensuring that policies change. climate policies should be inclusive, participatory, and sensitive to the needs of vulnerable groups.

CSOs and community organizations facilitate consultation and citizen participation processes, and monitor implementation of climate policies and promote accountability. Furthermore, they are active agents in education environmental awareness and social mobilization. Through community projects, campaigns of Awareness and sustainable management practices for natural resources contribute to strengthening the local resilience and fostering a culture of climate responsibility.

The legal and regulatory framework related to climate change in the Dominican Republic is broad and is contemplated at the highest level, from the Constitution, the National Development Strategy Law, among others that are detailed below in Table 3.

4. Mitigation

4.1. Context of Mitigation Policies, Measures and Actions

Since joining the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), The Dominican Republic has made a firm commitment to climate action26 recognizing its responsibility to implement measures to address climate change. The ratification of the Kyoto Protocol in 2002 also allowed for early participation in the Clean Development Mechanism (CDM), consolidating its trajectory as a country committed to the emissions reduction and international climate cooperation. Subsequently, with the ratification- Following the ratification of the Paris Agreement in 2017, the country assumes and renews more ambitious climate commitments, including significant efforts in governance and transparency.

The Dominican Republic reaffirms its commitment to the fight against climate change through the implementation of Policies, Measures and Mitigation Actions (PMAs) aimed at reducing the greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, while promoting a development model sustainable, inclusive, and resilient economic development. These actions are designed in accordance with the national circumstances and in line with the international principle of common responsibilities but differentiated.

In the global context, the Dominican Republic contributes a very small portion of the GHG emissions, accounting for less than 0.1% of the global total. According to the most recent EDGAR data, Global emissions at ~53.0 GT CO₂ eq in 2023 (excluding land use changes)27 , placing the country in lower ranks within the global inventory.

At the national level, the GHG Inventory 2022 It places emissions in the range of 45,000 kt CO₂ eq, which allows us to estimate its share relative to the countries, as well as per capita emissions, by sector and their historical evolution. Relative to the size of its economy, the country's carbon intensity is around 0.4 ktCO₂ per million USD of GDP (PPP) in 2022, an indicator that suggests moderate performance. in carbon-product efficiency28 This intensity is lower than the world average, reflecting that the Dominican economy is not as emissions-intensive as others, but there is still room for improvement.

In this context, the mitigation policy is based on an approach that combines risk management and taking advantage of opportunities, applying criteria of proportionality and hierarchy in their approach. This approach is based on the precautionary principle, established in General Law No. 64-00 on Environment and Natural Resources.

Compared to economies of a similar size to that of the Dominican Republic, it aligns itself as an issuer medium-low, so its climate strategy does not focus on drastic absolute reductions, but rather to improve the relationship between economic growth and emissions, through: the transition energy; efficiency in land transport; the reduction of Hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs) in the refrigeration and air conditioning chain (RAC); and integrated waste management.

Climate change has been defined in the Political Constitution29 as a cornerstone in the formulation and execution of territorial planning to ensure the use efficient and sustainable use of natural resources to ensure adaptation to this phenomenon. Furthermore, it has adopted an increasingly proactive stance, incorporating GHG mitigation measures, which generate co-benefits, in its national sustainable development planning. Likewise, the National Strategy of Development30 (END-2030), established by Law No. 1-12, incorporates in its fourth strategic axis the production and Environmentally sustainable consumption, with specific objectives to address climate change. This The strategy is aligned with the Public Planning and Investment Law, through indicators and goals quantitative data that allow verification of the progress achieved.

In 2020, the Dominican Republic presented its NDC 2.0, where it defined its climate ambition based on the a 27% reduction in GHG emissions compared to "business as usual" or BAU projections projected between 2010 and 2030. This ambition was broken down into 20% conditional on international financing and 7% unconditional (financed with national resources), of which 5% correspond to the private sector and 2% to the public sector.

This NDC included 46 mitigation options distributed across key sectors (Figure 3): Energy, Use of Industrial Products and Processes (IPPU), Agriculture, Forestry and Other Land Uses (AFOLU) and Waste. These measures aimed to reduce approximately 13,853.71 kt CO₂ eq, representing a 27.16% reduction compared to the scenario BAU 2030 estimated at 51,000 kt CO₂ eq, according to the Economic Development Plan Compatible with Climate Change Climate (DECCC). The required investment was estimated at USD 8,916,950,000.

Figure 3. Mitigation sectors considered in NDC 2.0. Source: Modified from NDC 2.0).

The articulation between the RD 2036 Target Strategy, established by Decree No. 33724, and the proposal The mitigation measures of NDC 3.0 RD-2025 constitute an essential step to ensure that the aspirations of the country's economic growth is realized within a genuinely sustainable model compatible with the climate.

Both instruments share a common strategic vision: while the Meta RD 2036 Strategy defines the route To double GDP by 2036 and achieve the goals of the END 2030, NDC 3.0 RD-2025 transforms that vision into tangible climate commitments and actions. Together, they drive a competitive, inclusive development model. resilient and low carbon, fully aligned with institutional and sectoral planning processes of the country.

This coherence allows us to take advantage of opportunities for investment, innovation, and capacity building. that arise in a favorable enabling environment, consolidating the link between economic development, environmental sustainability and social well-being.

The proposed mitigation ambition in NDC 3.0 RD-2025 is presented strategically, clearly and visual, reflecting a process of technical analysis and multi-sector consultation identifying a scenario consistent with national circumstances in form: transparent in its formulation and justification; ambitious, considering science, technological and commercial evolution; and politically viable, with Institutional and social support. In compliance with the reporting requirements of the Framework Enhanced Transparency (ETF), NDC 3.0 RD-2025 includes the ICTU table (Information needed to facilitate clarity, transparency and understanding) in section 11 “Information intended to facilitate clarity, the transparency and understanding of nationally determined contributions” .

Opportunities for emissions reductions and synergies with NDC 2.0 were identified, not only in terms of emissions, but also in terms of policy needs and creating an enabling environment within the sectoral scope.

The process of formulating the mitigation component was structured based on the emissions sectors. defined in the IPCC Guidelines 2006 for the key categories of the Inventory, which cover more than 85% of national emissions, and was aligned with the END-2030, the RD 2036 Goal and other national planning instruments.

4.2. Process of preparing and validating information

NDC 3.0 RD-2025 is based on a rigorous technical process of development and validation of information, which includes the quantification of emissions, the definition of the baseline, the identification Identification of sectoral goals and the construction of mitigation scenarios for the period of implementation 2026–2035.

4.2.1.Baseline Definition

The baseline was constructed from the historical series of the National GHG Inventories (INGEIs), including the years: 1990, 1994, 1996, 2000, 2010, 2015 and 2022. The projections of Emissions targets for 2030 and 2035 were developed using technical parameters and sector models. The emissions baseline is the fundamental starting point for understanding the trajectory climate, since it reflects the historical behavior of emissions and allows us to project how They will evolve in the absence of new mitigation policies. Based on these historical series and Trends observed in the main greenhouse gases (CO₂, CH₄, HFCs and N₂O) can be to assess the climate challenge.

The analysis of the Baseline presented ( Annex 4: Baseline ) reveals a clear picture: carbon dioxide (CO₂) dominates the growth trajectory, driven primarily by the energy and transportation sectors. Methane (CH₄) shows a progressive reduction of the growth rate, until reaching a trend contrary to Starting in 2015, with significant reduction potential that must be considered. Nitrous oxide (N₂O) has emerged in the last 15 years as a growing challenge linked to the use of fertilizers and agricultural practices. Added to this is a worrying phenomenon: the combination of increasing emissions and declining absorption exacerbates the overall trend.

To ensure that the defined ambition is consistent with capabilities and successfully leverages the potentialities, a set of PAMs has been identified that will allow the objective to be dimensioned and will support the ambition to be defined.

4.2.2.Construction of the PAMs portfolio

The portfolio of mitigation PAMs was built from multiple sources, in which It includes the following:

  • Monitoring and Follow-up Chapter of NDC 2.0, and of the First Biennial Transparency Report combined with the Fourth National Communication of the Dominican Republic.

  • Database of multilateral organizations and official bodies.

  • Expansion Plans, Indicative Plans, Multi-Year Public Investment Plan, Reviews

  • Project documents and studies carried out from the technical assistance in the different sectors.

  • The estimation of the technical potential was carried out using recognized methodologies such as IPCC, MDL, GHG Protocol, and international best practices. While the needs of Financing was defined based on project documents, public investment plans, and private, and interviews with key stakeholders.

The process of formulating the mitigation portfolio included: (1) developing scenarios of mitigation by 2030 and 2035, (2) identify possible targets, and (3) assess consistency, Improvement of scenarios and alignment with national circumstances. The definition of The mitigation component was based on a technical, participatory, and multi-sectoral process, aimed at ensuring the quality, relevance, and feasibility of the proposed measures. The process was developed in three complementary stages:

  1. Information gathering: A thorough review of documents was carried out- strategic plans, technical reports and previous studies, including NDC 2.0, assessments of progress and sectoral plans. This documentary analysis was complemented with the reco- stacking of primary data and identification of information gaps at the institutional level national and sectoral.

  2. Structuring and validating the portfolio: Through sector consultations, meetings bilateral meetings and technical workshops with sectoral ministries, technical entities, cooperation agencies and non-state actors. These spaces allowed for the identification of the key mitigation levers, analyze the technical and economic potential of the measures, and prioritize those with the greatest climate impact and the greatest feasibility of implementation.

  3. Scenario building and methodological review: Scenarios were developed for mitigation by 2030 and 2035, evaluating its consistency and alignment with the circumstances national. Specialized consultations with national and international experts allowed review assumptions, variables, and parameters, strengthening the methodological consistency of the scenarios. This continuous interaction with the technical community allowed for the incorporation of updated knowledge, validate preliminary results and ensure that the proposals They will reflect both the national context and international best practices.

Given that the identified initiatives respond to different sectoral strategies with For short, medium and long term horizons (up to 2040), a process of nor- mapping of indicators to homogenize their measurement (Figure 5 shows the distribution of PAMs in the three indicators).

Figure 5. Percentage distribution of targets across the three indicators for monitoring.

Source: Author's own work.

The calculation estimates, both for the historical baseline, the emissions projection and absorptions, and the technical potential of GHG emission reductions was carried out following

the 2006 Guidelines of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change31, supplemented with methodological updates 32 and global warming potential (GWP) values set out in the Sixth Assessment Report (AR6)33, where applicable.

The methodology adopted was based on a sector-based approach according to the categories defined in the National GHG Inventory System, including Energy, Industrial Processes and Product Use (IPPU), Agriculture, Forestry and Other Land Uses (UTCUTS) and Waste34. For each category, the following stages were applied:

This approach ensures transparency, accuracy, consistency, comparability, and completeness. (TACCC principles) required by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change Climate (UNFCCC) and facilitates the traceability of the results obtained.

The construction of the baseline and business-as-usual (BAU) scenarios was based on the use of series Historical data on activity and emissions were available for the different gases. Equations were fitted to projection selecting those that presented a statistically significant coefficient of determination (R²) acceptable. Given the lack of available national econometric models and comprehensive official projections, Simplified assumptions were made without specific technological or capacity restrictions.

The PESTAL analysis (Political, Economic, Social, Technological, Environmental and Legal) to complement the Building scenarios and contextualizing the results allowed for the identification of external factors. determinants in the evolution of emissions and in the feasibility of mitigation measures:

  • Political: the National Development Strategy 2030, the National Energy Plan (PEN) 2025/2038 and the NDC 2.0 establishes energy transition and emissions reduction goals that directly influence the sectoral trajectories.

  • Economic: GDP growth, energy demand, industrial dynamism, and investment Foreign direct investment determines the rate of increase in emissions in energy, waste, and IPPU.

  • Social: accelerated urbanization, population growth, and changes in consumption patterns affect the waste generation, transportation and pressure on land use.

  • Technological: advances in renewable energy, electric mobility, carbon capture, low-emission technologies Carbon and waste management offer significant mitigation opportunities.

  • Environmental: the vulnerability of the territory to climate change and ecosystem degradation They condition the absorption capacity and ecosystem services.

  • Legal: regulatory frameworks such as Law No. 64-00, General Law of Environment and Natural Resources, Law No. 57-07, on Incentives for the Development of Renewable Energy Sources and their Regimes Specials36 , Decree No. 601-08 on the creation of the National Council for Climate Change, Decree No. 541-20 on the National MRV System, Decree No. 158-23 on energy efficiency in the public sector, The law and the regulatory framework of the electricity sector constitute fundamental pillars for the implementation of climate policies.

Some of the assumptions for the mitigation scenarios are sector-specific, considering Macroeconomic, technological, demographic, regulatory, and structural variables. The main assumptions The following are used:

Energy:

  • Energy demand and planning in line with the assumptions and projections of the PEN 2038 and the series historical records of the Coordinating Body (OC).

  • The pace of integration of new units responds to what is stipulated in the final concessions that Granted by the Ministry of Energy and Mines (MEM), through the National Energy Commission (CNE).

  • Regarding the generation matrix, it is estimated that the structure and pace of operation will remain the same. current with moderate expansion of thermal capacity tending towards energy transition fuels.

  • It is understood that the carbon intensity of the national interconnected electricity system (SENI) will remain levels similar to the current ones.

  • The pace of integration is based on studies and analyses of the Integrated Transportation System (SIT), where the growth of the vehicle fleet is proportional to GDP and the urban population and The incorporation of electromobility responds to historical market strategies and dynamics.

Industrial Processes and Product Use (IPPU):

  • Industrial production and product usage respond to the economic growth trend. where current processes and technologies are maintained without the adoption of capture or efficiency systems Additionally, they must be disruptive. This includes the use of products with increasing and consistent usage rates. of HFCs and other substitutes in refrigeration and air conditioning, with imports similar to those trends of the last decade.

Agriculture

  • For the agricultural sector in enteric fermentation activities, sludge management, and the use of Chemical inhibitors and the reduction of mortality in cattle respond to the trend of growth in food demand, where current processes and technologies are maintained excreta treatment without the adoption of capture systems, treatment that allows for the recovery of methane, and an appropriate feeding and vaccination system that allows for efficient stratification of the Improved nutrient absorption and reduced species mortality. Improved farming practices. of rice that improve the efficiency of water use and fertilizer use, reducing the amount of wastewater and the organic load of the process.

Land Use and Land Use Changes (LULUCCS):

  • Forest cover, deforestation rate and average annual degradation based on data from National Forest Inventory of the Dominican Republic(2021)37 and reports from the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources, as well as the intention of continuity of the strategic program “Dominican Republic 30x30”, which seeks to ensure that the country achieves the me- tas of the global biodiversity framework, as well as maximizing adaptation and mitigation to climate change.

  • Changes in land use respond to ongoing patterns of agricultural expansion and urban.

  • Thus, it was assumed that the absorptions attributed to activity in UTCUTS would continue to maintain the rates current sequestration practices without implementation of additional reforestation or restoration programs ecologically disruptive.

Waste:

  • Taking into account that the generation of solid waste maintains a growth correlated with the increase in urban population and GDP per capita, and that in the baseline scenario it is estimated that persistence of the current open-air landfill disposal model, without biogas recovery or Non-energy use of materials. Material recovery measures and biogas combustion They are driven and supported by mitigation strategies.

  • For wastewater, treatment coverage shows limited progress, with systems mostly primary or non-existent and lack methane recovery and burning mechanisms.

4.3. PAMs NDC 3.0 Portfolio

The selection of the mitigation goal or objective is based on technical and strategic criteria, since This defines the time horizon within which an objective or result is expected to be achieved. Some of the The main criteria are: consistency with international commitments, alignment with plans and policies national, technical, economic and institutional feasibility, scalability and progressive monitoring, relevance for long-term transformation.

Considering that mitigation commitments will be updated in 2030, the following scenario is established potential for implementation, the making of investments in the period 2026-2030 and the obtaining of emission reductions in the period 2026-2035. Furthermore, given that the commitments will be updated again through the formulation of the NDC with a 2035 horizon, this timeframe is It also considers a valid implementation scenario.

The mitigation portfolio includes 26 sectoral action goals aimed at achieving a reduction of 9,997 kt CO₂ eq between 2026 and 2030, with cumulative effects until 2035. The measures cover the Four main greenhouse gases: CO₂, CH₄, N₂O, and HFCs. The sectors with the greatest potential for The mitigation priorities are: Energy, followed by LULUCF, and Waste. The total estimated investment to achieve these The goals amount to USD 11,468 million.

Progress towards the goals is assessed using three performance indicators: GHG reduction (73%), Strengthening enabling frameworks (23%), and other non-GHG benefits (4%). These will serve as a basis for the evaluation and monitoring of the implementation of NDC 3.0 RD-2025, in a period 2026-2035, For these purposes, each goal may have an impact on more than one indicator. Table 5 presents the estimate of GHG emissions reduction and the investment needed for each goal or action sectoral. The proposed portfolio of measures responds directly to the trends identified in the emissions baseline, offering a comprehensive approach to reversing the upward trajectory of GHGs and strengthen absorption.

First, the largest percentage of the reduction is concentrated in the sector energetic where the incorporation of new natural gas-fired generation units (as a transition fuel) (tion) and from renewables, along with the improvement of the distribution system and the development of Emerging technologies such as small modular reactors (SMRs) seek to curb growth accelerated of CO 2, the main driver of increased emissions. These actions are complemented by programs of energy efficiency and a transformation of land transport , focused on electrification, fleet modernization and the establishment of corridors sustainable, aligning with the recommendation to accelerate the structural decarbonization of these key sectors.

The portfolio addresses challenges identified in the baseline and projections, particularly the need to prioritize consolidating CH₄ reductions through interventions in waste solids, wastewater, agriculture and manure management, as well as the promotion of climate-smart agricultural practices that They incorporate N₂O reductions. The measures aimed at the comprehensive management of solid waste and wastewater contribute directly to the mitigation of CH₄ emissions. To address the issue of the decline of The absorptions, currently on a downward trend, raise the issue of protection and restoration of natural sinks, including forests mangroves and the expansion of protected areas, reinforcing the absorption component, strengthening environmental integrity and national climate ambition.

As a result of the implementation of NDC 2.0, the Dominican Republic has achieved by 2025 a emission reduction of approximately 4,734.73 kt CO₂eq compared to the business-as-usual (BAU) scenario.

In this context, NDC 3.0 incorporates an additional set of mitigation measures whose potential to The reduction is estimated at 9,997.37 kt CO₂eq/year by 2030. The joint implementation of the actions stemming from NDC 2.0 and the new measures proposed in NDC 3.0 would allow us to achieve a total reduction of approximately 14,732.1 kt CO₂eq, equivalent to a 28% decrease compared to BAU scenario for the year 2030.

Similarly, the projections to 2035 consider both the consolidation of existing measures such as the expansion of new actions, which would allow progress towards an estimated reduction of 18,349.32 kt CO₂eq, equivalent to 32% compared to the BAU scenario.

This approach reflects the continuity of efforts between NDC cycles, avoiding double counting. of measures, and ensures the progression of the country's climate ambition, in line with the Paris Agreement.

Mitigation Objective

The Dominican Republic commits to reducing CO₂ emissions by 14,732.10 kt by 2030, and by 18,349.32 kt by 2030. for 2035, compared to those estimated under a business-as-usual (BAU) scenario of 51,949.62 kt CO₂ eq and 57,292.65 kt CO₂ eq (respectively), which corresponds to a reduction of 28 % by 2030 and 32% by 2035, contributing to the achievement of global objectives of the Paris Agreement. Figure 6 shows the emissions reduction ambition scenario. for NDC 3.0 RD-2025.

Achieving these mitigation goals will require an estimated total investment of USD 11.468 billion , of this commitment, 8.5% is unconditional financed with national resources, and a 19.5% is conditional on external financing, Technology and international cooperation mobilized through national and international sources.

4.4. Levels of Ambition

According to the analysis of the documentation presented, the sectoral discussions and meetings bilateral agreements have developed the scenarios shown, such as how the Dominican Republic, through its NDC proposes to contribute to the achievement of the Convention agreements as set out in its Article 2, paragraph 1 (a), and to Article 4, paragraph 1 of the Paris Agreement.

Figure 9 shows the projected evolution of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from period 2020-2035, expressed in kilotonnes of CO₂ equivalent (kt CO₂ eq). The baseline (P_2022), represented by the dotted green line, reflects the expected growth in emissions under the Under current conditions, it will increase from approximately 45,327 kt CO₂ eq in 2022 to 57,293 kt CO₂ eq in 2035. Furthermore, the new ambition (dotted red line) incorporates additional mitigation measures, with a progressive reduction from 42,842 kt CO₂ eq in 2025 to 37,217 kt CO₂ eq in 2030, equivalent to about 14,732.1 kt CO₂ eq in 2030.

Considering the new portfolio, which presents a potential for reducing GHG emissions in the different sectors for a total of 9,997.37 kt CO₂ eq, a comparison is established in the new ambition for NDC 3.0. The Dominican Republic structures its national contribution to climate change mitigation in two Complementary levels, conditioned and unconditioned. These reflect not only the degree of dependence regarding international support, but also the magnitude of the investments required to achieve each level of ambition, as established in Article 4 of the Paris Agreement. Taken together, Both levels contribute to the Dominican Republic's decarbonization trajectory and its goals of sustainable development in the medium and long term.

In that sense, the country has sought to ensure that the goals reflect the greatest possible ambition, having Considered in the design of the goals were the historical and current contributions, the capabilities respective national circumstances, in application of the principle of common responsibilities but differentiated.

He unconditioned component , equivalent to 30% of the investments required for the total mitigation goal, represents the volume of emissions reductions that the country commits to achieving with national resources and other Sources managed by the country. This level reflects the financial effort that the Dominican Republic is prepared to assume and mobilize in a sovereign manner, prioritizing interventions that can to be implemented in the short and medium term within the framework of public priorities and policies existing and national sectoral strategies.

For his part, the conditioned component , corresponding to the remaining 70% of the investments required for the mitigation goal, is directly linked to the mobilization of additional international external resources. Considering the availability of concessional climate finance, technology transfers, cooperation innovative technical and financing mechanisms, essential for implementing high-impact projects impact and higher capital costs, such as structural transformation and matrix improvement energy and distribution networks, electrification and modernization of transport, valorization of waste and large-scale ecosystem restoration.

4.5 Mitigation Co-benefits

Within the framework of NDC 3.0 RD-2025, climate action is conceived as a fundamental piece of Sustainable development that generates benefits beyond emissions reduction. The portfolio of policies, actions and measures promote co-benefits in climate resilience, just transition, economic diversification and green jobs, health and air quality, and economic competitiveness, to This timeframe integrates synergies between adaptation and mitigation, and incorporates a cross-cutting gender perspective. These measures strengthen energy security, reduce macroeconomic vulnerabilities, and boost economic activity. emerging sectors and promote social inclusion, positioning NDC 3.0 as a strategic instrument for well-being, resilience and productive transformation long term.

Adaptation (resilience): The portfolio of measures systematically strengthens resilience climate of the country by reducing the vulnerability of the energy, urban, productive and Environmental initiatives in the face of extreme events, heat waves, droughts, floods, and storm surges. such as renewables with storage (BESS), more robust electricity grids, MEPS for cooling, Public LED lighting, electric mobility, and efficient bus corridors increase the Operational reliability and continuity of essential services. In the agro-environmental sector, the climate-smart livestock farming, biodigestion, composting, and irrigated rice management Intermittent water use (AWD), reforestation and mangrove protection reduce production losses, They control erosion, ensure water for irrigation and energy, and improve absorption capacity Climate impacts. In sanitation and waste, the Duquesa biogas plant and the modernization of plants Wastewater reduces health risks, fires and leachate, strengthening urban resilience and coastal.

Just transition: The set of measures promotes a just transition by prioritizing reconversion labor shift from carbon-intensive sectors towards clean, formal and technologically advanced activities more advanced, guaranteeing social inclusion and equal opportunities. Local jobs are promoted. in the installation, operation and maintenance of renewable energy, energy efficiency, sustainable mobility, sanitation, waste management, and bioeconomy. Programs such as MEPS, Kigali, vehicle inspection and scrapping, and Intelligent logistics (FMS/TMS) strengthen technical training, combat informality, and raise workplace safety standards. Territorial integration through cable cars, monorails and Bus corridors improve access to employment and services in outlying neighborhoods, while Rural and coastal measures strengthen the income of small producers, artisanal fishermen and forest communities.

Economic diversification and green jobs: The portfolio catalyzes a productive transformation towards a more diversified, resilient and knowledge-based economy, creating value chains in renewable energy, electrical storage, efficient RAC services, digital logistics, bioenergy, Recycling, ecotourism, and blue tourism. Lean manufacturing and industrial innovation are encouraged. (low-carbon cement, co-processing), technology transfer (including small-scale nuclear) scale), and the development of nurseries, bio-inputs, biodigesters, and value-added centers. These These activities broaden the export base, reduce dependence on imported fossil fuels, and They generate higher quality green jobs in urban, rural, and coastal sectors.

Health and air quality: The measures produce substantial public health benefits by reducing local pollutants such as SO₂, NOx, PM₂.₅, odors and pathogens, especially in dense urban areas, road corridors and Communities near landfills and industrial facilities. The replacement of fuel oil with gas. natural resources, the deployment of renewables, energy efficiency, clean mobility, and modern management Waste and wastewater treatment improve air and water quality and reduce diseases respiratory and gastrointestinal health issues, and reduce chronic health risks. In rural areas, the Biodigestion, composting, and good livestock practices limit vectors, odors, and pollution. soils and bodies of water, raising the quality of community life.

Economic development: From a macroeconomic perspective, the portfolio reduces systemic costs and external vulnerabilities through reduced fossil fuel imports, the Strengthening energy security and improving production efficiency. Savings are generated. fiscal (LED lighting, energy efficiency), greater reliability of the electrical system, lower logistical and production costs, and greater sectoral competitiveness (energy, agribusiness, cement, tourism). The associated investments attract private capital, drive innovation, and boost activity urban centers and strengthen rural and coastal hubs, with multiplier effects on employment, national income and productivity.

Committee for adaptation measures: A key feature of the portfolio is its ability to integrate adaptation with climate mitigation, Maximizing net climate benefits. Measures such as Kigali, Minimum Energy Performance Standards (MEPS) for Refrigeration and Air Conditioning (RAC), Alternating Humidification and Drying (AWD) in rice, climate-smart livestock farming, biodigestion, composting, modern sanitation, Reforestation and mangroves reduce CO₂, CH₄ and N₂O emissions while increasing resilience thermal, water, and productive. These synergies allow for a reduction in the climate footprint of adaptation. Strengthen natural carbon sinks (forest and blue carbon) and improve energy and material efficiency of the economic system as a whole.

Gender: The portfolio integrates gender equity across all areas by promoting participation active participation of women in traditionally male-dominated sectors such as energy, logistics, sanitation, transport and waste management, through technical training, professional certification and jobs Quality formal education. Opportunities for rural women in bioeconomy and livestock farming are strengthened. sustainable practices, composting, forest nurseries and coastal conservation, increasing their autonomy economic development and community leadership. Improvements in mobility, public lighting, sanitation, and Improved air quality reduces health risks and disproportionate household burdens, facilitating Women's labor market participation and equitable access to services, employment and decision-making in the climate transition.
