4.6 Carbon Market

The Dominican Republic does not contemplate participation in international carbon markets for the compliance with its NDC.

With the aim of adapting the national institutional framework to the mandates of the Paris Agreement and its handbook rules, in August 2025 the Executive Branch, through Decree No. 358-25, renamed the National Council for Climate Change and Clean Development Mechanism (CNCCMDL) as the National Council for Change Climate and Carbon Market (CNCCMC).

The Dominican Government, through the CNCCMC, is in the technical preparation phase and institutional framework for the establishment of a Domestic Emissions Trading System (STS) and has developed a roadmap for a Pilot Program with the support of the “Collaborative Instruments” initiative for Ambitious Climate Action” (CiACA)38. A national market allows for limiting GHG emissions in certain sectors and offering incentives for A long-term investment in low-carbon technologies. This also stimulates economic transformation. and sustainable development, innovation and competitiveness and mitigation co-benefits.

Similarly, the Dominican Republic participates voluntarily in the mechanisms of Article 6 of Paris Agreement, which includes the cooperative approaches of Article 6.2 and the “Paris Agreement Crediting” Mechanism” (PACM) of Article 6.4. Within this framework, the country prioritizes the application of the criterion of Additionality, high standards of environmental integrity, and transparency in transactions. In Consequently, the mitigation outcomes intended for international transfer, both under the Mechanisms of Article 6, such as the voluntary market, will come from activities that are not included in the goal committed to in this NDC.

Both projects developed under the SCE and those implemented under Article 6 will employ a unified national registry and monitoring mechanism, in order to ensure consistency between instruments, avoid double counting, ensure environmental integrity and promote transparency of the transactions.

5. Adaptation

5.1. Context of Adaptation Policies, Measures and Actions

In the Dominican Republic, adaptation is addressed by linking development planning with climate vulnerability and resilience building, highlighting a “top-down” approach downwards” (national policies, sectoral planning) combined with one “from the bottom up "up" (local participation, community adaptive measures). This coordination is carried out at based on a legal and strategic framework, including the National Adaptation Plan (PNACCRD), which guides the reduction of the country's vulnerability, integrates adaptation into planning development and focuses on increasing the resilience of key sectors such as agriculture, biodiversity and forests, water, health and infrastructure.

In this context, the National Council for Climate Change and Carbon Market (CNCCMC) acts as the coordinating body for national climate policy, the Ministry of Environment and The Ministry of Natural Resources (MMARN) exercises environmental leadership, in accordance with Law No. 6400; The Presidency, with the approval of Law No. 45-25, has assumed the functions of territorial planning integrates climate change into territorial organization and its alignment with disaster risk management for national development planning, In accordance with Law No. 368-22; the Ministry of Finance and Economy ensures sustainability financial aspects of climate change policies, integrating the climate variable into the Budget planning and climate finance mechanisms (see National Context) Chapter 3).

Likewise, sectoral ministries, such as Agriculture, Tourism, and Health, play a role operational in the implementation of adaptation measures with mitigation co-benefits, with- contributing to reducing the vulnerability of the productive and social sectors.

Adaptation to climate change occupies a prominent place in the legal framework of the Republic The Dominican Republic, which incorporates a specific mandate on the subject in its Constitution. In its Article Article 194 establishes that the State must prioritize the creation and implementation of a land-use plan. territorial through a law, with the objective of guaranteeing the efficient and sustainable use of The country's natural resources, aligning with the needs for adaptation to climate change.

The National Development Strategy 2030 (END), established by Law No. 1-12, recognizes the importance of integrating climate change as a cross-cutting theme in public policies at all levels. Its fourth axis affirms the commitment to achieving a society with a culture of sustainable production and consumption, which manages risks and protection fairly and effectively of the environment and natural resources, and promotes appropriate adaptation to change climatic.

5.2. National priorities for adaptation to climate change

This chapter presents the Dominican Republic's ambitious vision to transform adaptation into The driving force behind a new sustainable development. The national adaptation goals established in the NDC 3.0 RD-2025 are aligned with the Global Adaptation Goal (GGA) of Article 7.1 of the Paris Agreement, aimed at “ to improve adaptability, strengthen resilience, and reduce vulnerability to change global climate ” In this context, the adaptation approach adopted by the Dominican Republic responds according to climate justice criteria, seeking a fair and equitable distribution of the burdens and benefits of climate action, so that sectors, groups and more vulnerable territories, making the costs associated with the transition to resilience assumed in a proportionate manner.

The NDC 3.0 RD-2025 raises national ambition by translating this commitment into concrete and measurable goals, that integrate adaptation as part of the country's sustainable development. Based on the prioritization of strategic sectors, a broad participatory and inter-institutional process was developed (see Annex 5: Participatory Process (for more details), from which emerged the national commitment to adaptation, expressed in 7 sectoral goals, for a total of 41 measures, reflecting the diversity of challenges and opportunities of Dominican territory.

These goals and measures are consistent with the main planning and policy instruments national public policy, including the END, the National Climate Change Policy (PNCC), the PNACC-RD and the Sectoral strategies linked to the management of natural resources, environmental sustainability, health and food security. This articulation ensures that climate action is integrated in a way that cross-cutting in the national development agenda, enhancing synergies and avoiding duplication in the institutional efforts.

The distribution by sectors is as follows:

  • Water resources and water security: 5 measures

  • Agriculture and food security: 6 measures

  • Resilient cities: infrastructure and human settlements: 6 measures

  • Health: 5 measures

  • Tourism: 7 measures

  • Coastal and marine resources: 6 measures

  • Ecosystems, biodiversity and forests: 6 measures

Each goal and measure has SMART indicators (Specific, Measurable, Achievable, Relevant and (Defined temporarily), which will allow for a systematic evaluation of progress towards the objectives proposed and guide the processes of monitoring and periodic review of the NDC.

The graphic sheets presented below summarize the prioritized adaptation measures in NDC 3.0, organized according to the sectors and strategic axes that support the national policy towards to climate change. These measures represent the concrete expression of the adaptation ambition of country, aimed at strengthening the resilience of ecosystems, communities and systems productive in the face of climate impacts.

Each fact sheet integrates goals for 2030 and 2035, progress indicators, overarching objectives, and information regarding the responsible entities, which facilitates their incorporation into planning instruments, national monitoring and reporting. Its formulation responds to the methodological framework of NDC 3.0, which promotes integrated, inclusive and evidence-based management, ensuring consistency with the GGA of the Agreement Paris and with the commitments established in the national sustainable development plans and strategies.

Taken together, these measures reflect the country's commitment to transformative adaptation, aimed at reduce vulnerabilities, strengthen institutional and community capacities, and guarantee the sustainability of livelihoods in the face of climate change.

The details regarding the specific measures defined for achieving the overall goal of each sector are presented at the Annex 2: Adaptation Measures by Sector .

The measure of Water Resources and Water Security It promotes integrated water resources management and the protection of priority basins such as It is a foundation for guaranteeing the country's water security. It seeks to reduce vulnerability to droughts. floods and other hydrometeorological events, through the planning and sustainable management of watersheds and improved access to and quality of water and sanitation. It also promotes water governance. inclusive and resilient, strengthening coordination between institutions and local actors to ensure to ensure the sustainability of ecosystem services and associated livelihoods.

The measure of Agriculture is fundamental to guaranteeing the Food Safety national in a context of climate change. It focuses on making the use of water and soil more efficient through resilient technologies and practices, from efficient irrigation systems to agroecological management of soils and pests. The specific and measurable goals reflect the NDC's ambition to move towards a climate-smart agriculture that reduces its environmental footprint and increases its productivity sustainable. By integrating priority producers and watersheds, the measure builds resilience simultaneously in food systems, water resources, and the communities that depend on them they.

The measure of Resilient Cities It seeks to strengthen urban adaptation and resilience through territorial planning, innovation and the modernization of infrastructure, along with the implementation of safe construction standards and climate-resilient. Promotes the development of sustainable and accessible human settlements, capable of maintaining the continuity of essential services in the face of emergencies and weather events extremes. Furthermore, it promotes the integration of gender and human rights perspectives into management urban, promotes the active participation of women and youth in the construction sector, and supports the creation of inclusive, safe and sustainable urban environments.

The measure of Health It seeks to strengthen the capacity of the national health system to anticipate, prepare, respond and To recover from the risks associated with climate change. It promotes the integration of variables climatic and environmental data in monitoring and early warning systems, in order to monitor communicable and non-communicable diseases and protecting public health in the face of extreme events. Furthermore, It promotes the improvement of essential health infrastructure and services, ensuring their continuity. in an equitable, safe and sustainable manner, with a gender and human rights approach that prioritizes attention to populations in vulnerable situations.

The measure of Tourism It promotes the transformation of the tourism sector towards a low-emission, resource-efficient model resources and resilient to climate change. Promotes the implementation of the National Strategy of Tourism Sustainability and the incorporation of adaptation and mitigation criteria throughout the entire chain value, promoting responsible practices in water, energy, and waste management. Furthermore, It seeks to strengthen the planning and management of tourist areas, and reduce waste. food in the hotel sector and promote the restoration of coastal ecosystems, ensuring that the Tourism development contributes to environmental sustainability and the well-being of communities premises.

The measure of Coastal and Marine Resources It is of great relevance to Dominican resilience, with coastal-marine ecosystems being the first line of defense against climate impacts, such as sea level rise and events extremes. This measure directly addresses this strategic vulnerability, establishing an ambition Clear: Protect and restore between 80% (2030) and 100% (2035) of priority areas by Adaptive management plans. This action seeks not only ecological resilience, but also social resilience. by reducing the vulnerability of coastal communities through inclusive management that prioritizes their Participation and empowerment. Thus, the measure consolidates adaptation as a pillar for security territorial and the sustainable development of our coasts.

The measure of Ecosystems, Biodiversity and Forests It consolidates Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) as a central strategy of NDC 3.0. It seeks to increase climate resilience through conservation, restoration and sustainable management of priority ecosystems, with specific targets of 50% by 2030 and 70% by 2035. Their implementation with An inclusive approach strengthens environmental governance with the participation of youth, local communities and Rural women. In this way, a comprehensive vision is articulated that combines environmental protection with equity. social, positioning ecosystems as key natural infrastructure for national adaptation.

He Estimated investment amount for the implementation of adaptation measures in the Dominican Republic it is more than USD 12.251 billion for the seven priority sectors, which include a total of 41 measures with 155 specific goals (see Table 6). This value should be considered a minimum estimate, since for some measurements it was not It is not possible to perform an economic valuation due to the lack of specific information, while others could involve additional costs beyond those conservatively estimated.

6. Losses and Damages

6.1. Losses and damages in the national context

The Dominican Republic, due to its geographical position in the Caribbean, has a high level of exposure to extreme weather events such as hurricanes, storms, floods and droughts, whose The intensity and frequency of these events is exacerbated by the effects of climate change. The magnitude of the The damage and losses frequently caused by these events demonstrate the high susceptibility to disasters caused by hydrometeorological phenomena pose a challenge to sustainability economic, social and environmental.

In the last 90 years the country has been impacted by more than 40 hydrometeorological events, affecting more than 4 million people39. Likewise, the direct and indirect economic losses associated with disruptive events are They estimate USD 8.606 billion40, associated with essential infrastructure, the agricultural and tourism sectors. Along the same lines, The CDRP estimates average annual losses (AAL) of USD 1,401 million (equivalent to 1.2% of GDP). 2023), broken down into USD 676 million for tropical cyclones, USD 467 million for earthquakes and USD 259 million for floods, only in damage to buildings and contents (does not include infrastructure or other sectors). Additionally, PMLs (probable maximum loss) are their- essential; for a 100-year return period they amount to USD 13.8 billion (wind), USD 10.4 billion (earthquake) and USD 5.96 billion (flood).

Historical evidence reflects that the economic impact has been considerable; in 1979, the hurricanes David (Category 5) and Frederick, just six days apart, scored one of the Most destructive periods: 2,000 deaths, >1.2 million people affected and USD 577 millions in damages (12% of 1978 GDP), in 1998, Hurricane Georges left >150,000 people homeless, >220,000 homes damaged or destroyed, 4.2 million affected, 70% of the bridges destroyed and significant losses in agriculture, with USD 1.01 billion in damages (4.7% of GDP) and USD 1.16 billion in losses, Jeanne (2004) caused flooding and landslides with estimated damages of USD 270 million; Noel (2007) left 129 dead and severe Impacts on road and water networks39, in 2017, storms Olga and Noel caused damage and losses estimated at USD 437 million (1.2% of GDP), while the rains of November 2016 and April 2017 generated direct losses of USD 862 million (1.1% of GDP), more recently, Hurricane Fiona in 2022 caused losses direct economic benefits of USD 381.74 million, equivalent to 0.3% of nominal GDP4142 Furthermore, slow-evolving phenomena such as coastal erosion, aquifer salinization, and Loss of biodiversity and degradation of ecosystems increase structural risk and They exacerbate non-economic losses, affecting cultural identities, mental health, and ecosystem services and the livelihoods of coastal and rural communities. In this context, The loss of human life associated with extreme weather events is one of the more serious and evident manifestations of increased climate risk.

This highlights the urgent need to prioritize vulnerable populations and improve the generation of climatic, socioeconomic and territorial data and statistics, in order to inform the decisions, and monitor the evolution of national vulnerability.

In the national context, losses and damages must be addressed from a people-centered perspective, considering both observed and potential impacts, with an emphasis on social inequalities and territorial factors that amplify exposure and sensitivity to climate change. This implies recognizing the climate justice dimension, given that the communities with less historical responsibility in the Greenhouse gas emissions face the greatest consequences, including damage irreparable damage to their well-being, their fundamental rights and, in the most extreme cases, the loss of life human.

6.2.Observed and potential impacts of climate change

The impacts observed in the Dominican Republic include recurring damage to infrastructure critical, disruptions to essential services, agricultural and fishing losses, and displacement of communities, in addition to impacts on public health and cultural heritage.

A growing indicator of the climate threat is the annual average of extreme events, where it has A notable increase in the frequency of hydrometeorological events has been observed, going from a average of one (1) high-impact climatic event, to 0.5 times per year in the 1960s to almost one event per year in the 1990s and 2.6 events per year in the 2000s. Between 2010 and 2017, the The average was about 2 events per year. Similarly, recent climate projections indicate an increase in the average annual temperature of 2°C to 3°C in minimum temperatures and 1°C to 3°C in maximum temperatures. This increase, along with the expected reduction in rainfall, will intensify the cycle. hydrological, increasing the frequency of extreme rainfall events and thus a greater propensity to Flash floods and the increase in the average number of events per year (PNACC/TNCC, 2016). The scenarios Climate forecasts project a further increase in the intensity and frequency of these events, as well as the intensification of droughts, rising sea levels, and increased incidence of heat waves.

According to the study “Vulnerability and Risk Indices to Climate Variability and Climate Change At the “Dominican Republic Municipal Level” 10 municipalities were identified as presenting a higher risk to at the sectoral level across the country. In the agricultural sector, the municipalities with the highest Vulnerable areas include Pedro Santana, Higüey, Yamasá, El Seibo, Santo Domingo Norte, La Vega, and Moca. Peralvillo, Neiba, and Villa Riva. Regarding human settlements, the greatest risk is concentrated in the National District, Santo Domingo East, Santo Domingo West, Santo Domingo North, Los Alcarrizos, Higüey, Santiago, Pedro Brand, Eugenio María de Hostos, and Bajos de Haina. Regarding tourism in the municipalities Coastal areas include Higüey, San Rafael del Yuma, Puerto Plata, the National District, and San Pedro de Macorís. Sosúa, Boca Chica, Las Terrenas, Samaná, and La Romana. Finally, in the livelihoods sector, the The most exposed municipalities are Santo Domingo Este, Distrito Nacional, Santo Domingo Norte, Santiago, Los Alcarrizos, San Cristóbal, La Vega, Santo Domingo Oeste, San Francisco de Macorís and Baní.

6.3. Actions aimed at the prevention, reduction and management of losses and damages

The country acknowledges that, although there are relevant initiatives, loss and damage management still presents challenges. significant gaps. Platforms such as the Collection System and are currently being implemented Dominican Republic Damage Assessment (SIRED-RD) for Post-Disaster Data Collection on direct economic damages and losses, and training has been conducted on assessments of post-disaster recovery needs, however, there is no comprehensive national approach that include both direct and indirect economic losses and non-economic losses, nor the impacts of slow evolution. In that sense, the actions that should be prioritized include: the standardization of methodologies for measuring and reporting losses and damages; the integration of this approach into plans such as the PNACC and the National Disaster Risk Reduction Plan, capacity building institutional and local frameworks for rapid assessment and preventative planning; and the design of financing mechanisms for recovery and rehabilitation.

In line with these efforts, the country has initiated discussions with the Santiago Network through a Request for technical assistance focused on the design of protocols and systems for monitoring and reporting losses and damages, in order to systematize the assessment, recording and documentation of losses economic and non-economic. This initiative seeks to strengthen national capacities and improve the access to climate information and finance, while promoting greater coordination interinstitutional and sectoral to guarantee a timely and effective response.

In parallel, and as part of a comprehensive approach, the country is making progress in actions aimed at prevention. of new risks and the reduction of losses and damages through the implementation of measures adaptation, including ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) and preventative infrastructure, as well as through financial mechanisms for recovery and rehabilitation mentioned within the instruments intended to promote the implementation of the NDC. These actions include the design and implementation of early warning and climate monitoring systems, through the development of the National Policy on Early Warning Systems led by the Ministry of the Presidency, for Strengthen planning and response to climate risks.

Measures such as conducting risk and vulnerability studies (index of) are also considered. Vulnerability and Risk to Climate Variability and Change at the level of the municipalities of the Republic Dominican Republic), and the formulation of roadmaps for the development of insurance and mechanisms for risk transfer, with special emphasis on vulnerable populations, the relocation of communities in at-risk areas. These actions include social protection programs and support for livelihoods, as well as specific projects such as the Neighborhood Resettlement Action Plan Azul, financed by the European Investment Bank (EIB) through a loan of approximately 50 millions of dollars. This project is part of a public works improvement program. Aimed at reducing disaster risk. It contributes to decreasing exposure to and the impacts of disasters. extreme weather events when resettling homes located in high-risk flood zones43.

6.4. Institutional arrangements to support the implementation of actions for the prevention, reduction and management of loss and damage

In the Dominican Republic, loss and damage management is framed within the institutional arrangements for the Risk management and climate change. In the area of risk management, Law No. 147-02 establishes The National System for Prevention, Mitigation and Response was established, and Law No. 1 is currently being implemented. 368-22, which aims to establish the regulatory framework for land use planning, taking into account guidelines of environmental, social, economic, cultural, and risk management interest and sustainable development. Furthermore, it establishes adaptation as a criterion for territorial planning and resilience to climate change.

In climate governance, the responsibilities fall to the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources Natural Resources, the National Climate Change Council, the Ministry of Finance and Economy, and the Ministry of the The Presidency, which is working on updating regulatory frameworks to integrate adaptation, resilience and Loss and damage management in national, sectoral, and territorial planning. The creation of a mechanism a specific institutional framework, as well as a legal framework that specifies the responsibilities in collection, assessment and reporting of losses and damages, with clear roles and responsibilities, intersectoral coordination and Access to financing represents a strategic opportunity to comprehensively address this issue. challenge. In this sense, in 2025 compliance with the Sendai Framework was reported for the first time, specifically- Specifically for Goals A, B, C, F, and G. From the MMARN, National Statistics Office, and the National Commission The Emergency Department is working on reporting additional goals for the remainder of 2025 and 2026.

7. Transversal Elements

The Dominican Republic continues to strengthen and work to place people at the center of action national climate. For this reason, it reinforces the commitments of the cross-cutting elements presented in its Previous INDCs (2015) and NDC 2.0 (2020): gender, youth, human rights, just transition and action for climate empowerment, local communities, and includes as new priorities working to early childhood, childhood and people with disabilities.

Through its efforts, the country recognizes the vulnerabilities and differentiated impacts of change climate. It also recognizes that these are exacerbated by inequality and marginalization linked to gender, low income, disability status, age range, among other social categories, or a a combination of these, especially for local communities.

The need to strengthen disaggregated climate and environmental information systems is highlighted. This allows us to delve deeper into this differentiation, but also to better understand how these highly Vulnerable people are also agents of change. Only in this way can progress continue. effective in planning and implementing gender-responsive or transformative actions in environmental and climate matters.

The sectoral adaptation and mitigation measures were developed through an extensive dialogue process. and national and sectoral consultations, with the participation of actors from the public and private sectors, of the civil society, academia, as well as women's, youth and community-based organizations, with the support from international cooperation. This process allows for mainstreaming climate action into climate measures gender focus, the needs of early childhood, childhood, youth and people with disability, as well as the priorities of the most vulnerable communities and groups.

7.1. Private Sector Participation

Private sector participation is crucial to achieving national climate goals and To accelerate the country's productive transformation. Within the framework of NDC 3.0 RD-2025, the aim is to promote investments in clean technologies and the circular economy through the definition of enabling frameworks and clear market signals, such as green public procurement, technical standards, auctions/PPAs, incentives fiscal measures and green financing or “blended finance”. These actions will allow the expansion of energy renewables with storage, waste valorization (recycling, composting, biogas), the replacement of high-impact inputs and new business models (servitization, remanufacturing), with direct impacts on competitiveness, on the sustainable and efficient use of resources, reduction of costs and creation of green jobs along value chains.

Additionally, the business sector has the capacity to promote energy efficiency and processes through management systems, implementation of strategies for the efficient and sustainable use of Resources such as cleaner production, digitalization, and predictive maintenance, Electrification of thermal uses, heat/water recovery, and improved logistics. Also the Improvement of production inputs (eco-inputs) by using materials with a percentage of recycled content, substitution of clinker, low GWP refrigerants, bio- Sustainable fuels, agricultural bio-inputs, among others, will allow for a reduction in direct emissions and indirectly, increase their competitiveness, become more sustainable and strengthen local suppliers.

Finally, adaptation measures aimed at reducing climate vulnerabilities will be promoted. of the productive apparatus: infrastructure and operations resilient to floods and heat waves, water and basin management, nature-based solutions (reforestation, mangroves), Urban sanitation and resilient supply chains. The adoption of insurance and financing climate (guarantees, green bonds/loans, results-based payments), along with programs of Job training will ensure a just transition, with special attention to MSMEs and territories vulnerable. These investments in adaptation generate mitigation co-benefits, lower consumption energy, carbon capture and CH₄ and N₂O reduction, and are aligned with diversification plans economic, consolidating new and exportable sectors of environmental services, sustainable tourism and bioeconomy.

Private sector participation in the NDC context occurs within a regulatory framework and Adequate oversight of business due diligence by the Dominican State. Highlighting that the country has adopted all legislative and administrative measures to reduce emissions by part of the private actors emitting greenhouse gases from its territory. The country also has with a legal and institutional framework that guarantees that all projects, particularly with regard to Actions on climate and biodiversity should be properly assessed for compliance with the State obligations, especially in matters of climate and human rights.

7.2. Human Rights and Climate Justice

Climate change is recognized as a cross-cutting threat to human rights according to the World Balance Sheet Report 2023 (GST). The Dominican Constitution establishes a legal framework that guarantees the fundamental rights of Citizens, recognizing human dignity and equality before the law. These rights are essential for the functioning of the rule of law and are protected by the Constitutional Court and others oversight bodies. The existence of the right to live in a healthy, ecologically balanced environment and suitable for the development and preservation of the various forms of life, the landscape and the This nature has also been established constitutionally. Among the fundamental rights that are seen Rights directly affected by climate change include: the right to life, the right to health, and the right to access water, food and housing, privacy, the environment, as well as specifically recognized rights known to particularly vulnerable groups.

In the advisory proceedings before the International Court of Justice (ICJ) on climate change The country stated that adequate protection of the climate system is indispensable for the human rights4445 Therefore, the national climate goal responds both to environmental requirements and to the obligation to safeguarding fundamental human rights, supported by international treaties and the law customary law recognized by the ICJ in this context.

The measures established for both mitigation and adaptation reaffirm the usual obligation to to ensure that activities carried out within the national territory do not cause damage to the environment, as well as the general duty to promote, respect and preserve the human rights of their populations against Harmful impacts of climate change. Private sector participation is governed by business conduct. responsible duly regulated and supervised by the State, given the existence of a legal framework and enabling institution.

The adaptation measures reflect, in addition to the constitutional priority, the climate justice approach. On the other hand, the instruments designed to promote implementation highlight the use of solutions based in nature, taking into account the constitutional mandate that, in international relations, the The country must speak out in favor of any initiative in defense of biodiversity.

As a small island developing state, the country has reflected its circumstances in its commitment. special and addresses inequalities that could impede development progress with a view to ensuring that no one is left behind. be left behind. It has also identified contributions to mitigation outcomes through measures of Adaptation. Integrating a rights-based approach and protecting vulnerable groups into the measures is essential. of particular relevance considering the impacts already evident on people's rights and in the environment.

Based on the strategic vision and national goal, with the contributions of a strategy for the Integration and mainstreaming of this approach in NDC 3.0 RD-2025 has been sought through the measures identified, reduce the risks arising, on the one hand, from the degradation of the climate system global and, on the other hand, the exposure and vulnerability of people to the effects of said degradation.

NDC 3.0 RD-2025 incorporates the rights-based approach by including considerations on fundamental rights in The climate context, according to international obligations, including the right to development. These Obligations seek economic, social, cultural and environmental rights, integrated into the elements cross-cutting themes such as gender, childhood and youth, Climate Empowerment Action (CEA) and transition fair.

The consultation process carried out is a testament to the commitment to intersectoral coordination and the cooperation to transform key sectors towards building resilience and reducing vulnerability in the country. In this sense, NDC 3.0 RD-2025 constitutes a decisive roadmap for the compliance with international obligations on climate change and human rights with ambition and progressiveness.

7.3. Gender, Early Childhood, Childhood and Youth Approaches

With regard to the national and sectoral consultation process, this NDC acknowledges the efforts and processes and documents promoted and led by young people in support of climate action. Among them The International Youth Declaration on Climate Change presented at COP27 is highlighted. (2022), which constitutes a manifesto for greater climate ambition, inclusion and fair financing, reaffirming youth as key actors in climate justice. Likewise, the Local Youth Conferences on Climate Change in the Dominican Republic (LCOYRD) as national spaces for youth engagement that generate substantial contributions from young people Dominicans to global climate agendas.

These conferences are especially linked to YOUNGO, a dynamic global network of children, Girls and young activists (up to 35 years old), as well as youth organizations, who contribute to the formulation of intergovernmental policies on climate change and promote empowerment and the formal participation of youth in the UNFCCC processes.

Similarly, the recommendations of National study on climate change and children's rights in the Dominican Republic (CLAC-RD, 2025), reference document for the inclusion of early childhood, childhood and Adolescence in national climate planning. These contributions strengthen coherence intergenerational NDC, broadening the stakeholder base and ensuring that the voices and needs of pregnant women, infants, children and youth should be integrated into the design, the implementation and monitoring of climate policies.

Within this framework, cross-cutting objectives were defined to guide national climate action in all its stages and that strengthen coherence and coordination between sectors. These objectives seek ensure that adaptation and mitigation measures systematically incorporate the approaches of equality, equity, inclusion, accessibility, and sustainability. They also guarantee that the processes of planning, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation are developed in an integrated manner, enhancing the synergies between sectoral commitments and national and international priorities in the area of climate change.

The following are the cross-cutting objectives:

  • Health: Progressively strengthen the national health system in the face of climate change. ensuring the continuity of essential services and integrated disease surveillance climate-sensitive, promoting sustainability, fostering a gender and rights-based approach humans, and paying special attention to the needs of vulnerable groups, especially pregnant and breastfeeding women, early childhood, childhood, youth and people with disability.

  • Coastal and Marine Resources: Promoting inclusive and participatory ecosystem management coastal and marine areas, incorporating a gender perspective and fostering climate resilience. This This objective will be promoted by strengthening the participation and economic empowerment of the coastal communities, highlighting the differentiated impacts, promoting local associations and the inclusion of youth in environmental governance.

  • Tourism: Promoting sustainable and climate-resilient tourism in the Republic Dominican Republic, seeking to progressively mainstream the perspective of gender and human rights in the planning, legislation, and management of the sector. This objective will be promoted through the generation of information on differentiated impacts, raising awareness among tourism stakeholders and The development of data systems that support inclusive decision-making will be encouraged. Community and rural ecotourism, seeking to provide technical and financial support to initiatives managed by local communities, particularly women and youth, so that the economic benefits contribute to the sectoral transition, conservation and reduction of vulnerability in tourist areas.

  • Water Resources and Water Security: Guaranteeing universal, equitable and safe access to water and Sanitation through sustainable management, improved service quality, and reduced risks hydrometeorological and promoting inclusive and resilient water governance, considering the differentiated needs of women, early childhood, childhood, adolescence and youth, adults older people, and people with disabilities.

  • Human Settlements and Infrastructure: Promoting the development of infrastructure and settlements sustainable, accessible, and climate-adapted human resources that ensure the continuity of essential services in emergency contexts. This objective will be promoted by incorporating the gender perspective and human rights, promoting the active participation of women and youth in the construction sector, strengthening regulations for barrier-free construction, areas green and promoting equity in decision-making.

  • Agriculture and Food Security: Strengthening food and nutritional security in the Dominican Republic through the promotion of sustainable, technologically advanced agricultural practices and climate-smart, ensuring a local, stable, nutritious and culturally appropriate supply adequate food, incorporating sustainable water management, soil protection and Early warning systems. This objective will be progressively promoted with a gender focus. responsive and intersectional, addressing the differentiated needs of pregnant women and infants, girls, boys, adolescents, young people, elderly people and vulnerable communities; increasing the autonomy of rural women, the meaningful participation of youth, and promoting equitable access to resources, technical capabilities, financing and policies resilient publics in the face of climate change, fostering a just transition in the sector agricultural.

  • Ecosystems, biodiversity and forests: Promoting ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) and management sustainable management of biodiversity and forests with an inclusive approach, which strengthens participation of youth, local communities and rural women in environmental governance. This This objective will be promoted by integrating gender equality, human rights, and environmental education. in territorial and sectoral planning, and promoting sustainable livelihoods that contribute to conservation, the restoration of ecosystems and biological corridors, and the resilience of priority landscapes to climate change.

  • Energy: Promoting a just, sustainable, inclusive and resilient energy transition climate, promoting the production, distribution and storage of clean, renewable energy and affordable for all sectors of the population. This objective will be promoted by incorporating the gender perspective and intergenerational equity in sector planning and management, through the integration of data disaggregated by sex and age in energy statistics, the strengthening the active participation of women and youth in all phases of the programs and projects, and the promotion of equitable access to use for vulnerable communities, production and monitoring of renewable energies.

  • Transportation: Promote a mass transit system that is clean, safe, interconnected, and accessible, promoting walkability and pedestrian safety, with incentives for girls, Children, adolescents, youth, older adults, and people with disabilities. This objective is It will promote by incorporating a cross-cutting approach to gender and human rights in the Design, implementation and monitoring of transportation policies, addressing needs differentiated measures related to security, violence, and discrimination will be strengthened. Furthermore, the active participation of women and youth in the transport sector, along with the production of statistics disaggregated by sex and age, and the promotion of solutions innovative, inclusive and sustainable that reduce social and environmental vulnerability.

  • Waste: Promote universal, equitable and safe access to water and sanitation, through inclusive, sustainable, and climate-resilient water management. This objective is It will foster by promoting the circular economy and waste reduction, encouraging lifestyles of sustainable living and strengthening the integrated management of solid waste and wastewater. It will seek to incorporate data disaggregated by sex and age into sectoral planning, and promote the equal participation of women, men and youth, and recognizing the role of recyclers and local communities in building more inclusive, efficient and resilient.

These cross-cutting objectives are based on the consultation process developed for the preparation of NDC 3.0 RD-2025, which integrated 27 new public policy instruments as inputs published in the period 2021–2025, as well as 9 additional instruments prior to 2020, considered relevant to strengthening the coherence of the national climate action framework.

Among the recent instruments, but not included in NDC 2.0, the following stand out: the Gender Strategy for the Sustainable Management of Productive Mountain Landscapes, the National Policy on Equality of Gender in the Agricultural Sector, the Cross-Cutting Policy Guide, the National Plan for Sovereignty and Food and Nutritional Security 2023–2026, the Conceptual Model of the Identification System and Classification of Public Spending on Gender Equity, the Conceptual Model of the Identification System and Classification of Public Spending on Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management and the exercise of Updating and prioritizing the Gender and Climate Change Action Plan. Other instruments that Considered prior to 2020, are the Organic Law on Equality of Rights of Persons with Disability (Law No. 05-13) and its implementing regulations and Law No. 352-98, on the Protection of Elderly Person. Other instruments currently in the publication process are also being considered. They will contribute to consolidating sectoral and cross-cutting frameworks, such as the Plan National Adaptation in Health and Climate Change (PNASCC) of the MSP.

In the quest to advance and strengthen the governance of climate action, climate finance In this regard, capacity building and the implementation of gender-focused actions are highlighted. Formalization of the National Roundtable on Gender and Climate Change (MGyCC), officially formed by the Ministry of Women, the National Council for Climate Change and Carbon Market (CNCCMC) and the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MMARN).

This body constitutes a permanent space for dialogue, consultation and inter-institutional coordination. It is a constitutional body that coordinates efforts among various national actors. Its work is focused on the following sectors: priorities of the Dominican Republic's Gender and Climate Change Action Plan, to the Topic National V of the National Plan for Equity and Gender Equality (PLANEG III), as well as the commitments of gender of the current and future NDCs and other instruments defined by its Steering Committee. The Board It also functions as a platform for capacity building and monitoring of It makes progress on gender and climate change issues, and acts as a coordinating body for other efforts. Key cross-cutting themes in this section.

In addition, the integration of actors has been promoted and is proposed to continue. strategic entities such as the National Institute for Comprehensive Early Childhood Care (INAIPI), the Council National Council for Children and Adolescents (CONANI), the Ministry of Youth, the National Council of the National Council for the Elderly (CONAPE), the National Council for Disability (CONADIS), the municipalities and especially the Ministry of the Presidency (MINPRE), whose involvement strengthens the incorporation of the perspectives of gender, early childhood, childhood, youth and other vulnerable groups in the climate policies and measures. Your participation helps to broaden the base of committed actors. to ensure greater intersectoral coherence and to promote the decentralized implementation of national climate action.

Goals:

  1. Strengthen national climate and environmental information systems through the generation, Collection and use of data disaggregated by sex, age, territory, disability and others intersectional criteria, ensuring their availability for the formulation and evaluation of evidence-based climate policies

  2. Increase meaningful participation and leadership of women, youth, and local communities and vulnerable groups in the planning, implementation, and monitoring and evaluation of climate action, ensuring formal spaces and decision-making mechanisms inclusive decisions.

  3. Develop institutional and technical capacities for the systematic incorporation of the approaches gender, human rights, childhood, youth, accessibility, inclusion and sustainability in the policies, programs, budgets and regulatory frameworks related to climate change.

  4. Strengthen the National Roundtable on Gender and Climate Change (MGyCC) as a coordination body, technical advice and monitoring of the integration of the gender approach and other approaches intersectional aspects of national climate action.

  5. Strengthen inter-institutional coordination by effectively integrating INAIPI, CONANI, CONAPE, CONADIS, the Ministry of Youth and the MINPRE in climate governance, ensuring their active participation in the design, implementation and monitoring processes of the NDC.

  6. Promote fair and equitable climate finance, including mechanisms to identify, to track and report climate spending with a focus on gender, age and disability, and to promote the mobilization of national and international resources with inclusive criteria.

  7. Identify, design, and implement indicators, methodologies, and monitoring mechanisms that integrate gender, age and disability perspectives in national monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) and in the National Climate Transparency System (SNTC), guaranteeing consistency with the Enhanced Transparency Framework of the Paris Agreement.

Action for Climate Empowerment (ACE)

In action for climate empowerment, the measures proposed in NDC 2.0 show progress. While significant, technical and financial needs are recognized to continue moving forward. in national climate empowerment. To this end, the country reiterates its commitment to action to climate empowerment and proposes a global measure with a sectoral goal.

Global Measure 2030

Strengthen the implementation and monitoring of the six elements of the Action for the Climate Empowerment (CEA) for its effective integration into national planning mitigation, adaptation, transparency and climate finance measures.

Sector Goal 2030

By 2030, environmental and climate education, continuous training and certified, inclusive awareness campaigns, permanent mechanisms of citizen participation and access to climate information; so that at least 80% of the The population aged 14 to 34 shows considerable or significant interest in climate change or climate and environmental problems.

Sector indicator

Percentage of the population aged 14 to 34, broken down by sex, with considerable or high interest in topics of Climate change (base year 2021: base proportion 77.5%) (Education Horizon 2034 and CNCCMDL survey).

Integrative objective

Strengthening the empowerment, participation, and integration of all sectors of the society, especially civil society and vulnerable groups—such as women, early childhood, childhood, youth and people with disabilities—, so that they take action as agents of change in decision-making and in the implementation of climate action national.

This overall measure, as well as the subsequent goals and measures, were developed through a broad process of national and sectoral dialogues and consultations, with the participation of actors from the public and private sectors, civil society, academia, as well as women's organizations, youth and community-based organizations, with the support of international cooperation. This process enabled Increase ambition and update the measures of the six elements of ACE.

Education

  1. By 2030, strengthen and expand climate education at different levels, cycles, and grades. modalities and stages of the school system, in public and private educational centers; as well as, in a transverse and articulated.

  2. By 2030, it must be ensured that all undergraduate students in Higher Education Institutions (IES) take at least one subject on environmental science and climate change, incorporated as prerequisite for graduation in all majors.

  3. By 2030, strengthen and expand climate education within the national education system professionalization, as well as in the training of technical, administrative and teaching staff of all the levels of the national education system.

  4. By 2030, expand and strengthen specialization in climate education in teacher training programs that They are taught in the country through the curriculum regulated by the Ministry of Higher Education, Science and Technology.

  5. By 2029, train 250 teaching staff members at various levels of formal education in How to address the topic of climate change in a timely manner in the classroom.

    Training

  6. Strengthening the capacities of national negotiators for international negotiations climate change.

  7. By 2030, to have trained at least 250 social communication professionals and practitioners on how address disseminating news about climate change.

  8. Promote training programs for planners, economics professionals and related fields for the understanding and adoption of sustainable economic practices, with special attention to purchasing green, green jobs, carbon markets and climate finance.

  9. Promote training programs on climate risk management and adaptation to climate change with civil society actors from the most vulnerable areas.

  10. Strengthening the private sector's capacity to implement adaptation measures and cleaner production and energy efficiency for the reduction of GHGs.

    Awareness

  11. By 2030, to have developed and implemented a strategic public awareness campaign through traditional and digital media outlets explaining the NDC and its components.

  12. By 2030, to have implemented an advertising program through social media, traditional media and public events promoting appropriate adaptation to climate change in priority sectors and promote mitigation measures at all levels.

  13. By 2027, to have designed, implemented and monitored a public awareness and empowerment campaign with different sectoral actors in climate action aimed at local governments.

  14. Promote training and awareness strategies for decision-makers from different sectors with the objective of integrating climate change as a strategic axis in the formulation of public policies.

  15. By 2030, identify and train at least two community leaders per province as multipliers in risk management and climate adaptation, through awareness and training processes participatory processes adapted to local contexts, taking into account civil society organizations and the board of neighbors.

    Public access to information

  16. Strengthen public communication on climate change through the active promotion of channels information officers, ensuring its accessibility, accuracy and suitability for different audiences.

  17. Ensure translation into Spanish, adaptation to the national context, and accessibility of documents key information on climate change, including its availability in formats suitable for people with visual and hearing impairment.

    Public participation

  18. By 2030, develop a National ACE Strategy that includes consultations with stakeholders public, private and civil society.

  19. Establish a Citizen Consultation Council on Climate Change, with gender representation, generations and people with disabilities, functioning as a permanent space for participation and impact on the design and monitoring of climate policies and instruments.

    International cooperation

  20. Keep the appointment of the national focal point for ACE up to date, to strengthen the exchange direct collaboration within ACE with other national ACE focal points around the world, as well as with the UNFCCC, UNESCO, UNITAR and other UN agencies, and participate in annual ACE dialogues and opportunities training for ACE focal points.

  21. Establish and strengthen national mechanisms for linking to easily accessible regional platforms to citizens, which allow for the collection, systematization, and sharing of best practices in the six elements of ACE (education, training, awareness, participation, access to information and cooperation) international).

From the various consultation processes, as well as the monitoring and implementation efforts of the Based on ACE actions in recent years, key actors responsible for implementation and execution have been identified. monitoring of these measures. In the public sector, the National Council for Climate Change stands out and Carbon Market (CNCCMC), the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MMARN), the Ministry of Education (MINERD), the Ministry of Higher Education, Science and Technology (MESCyT), the National Institute of Teacher Training and Development (INAFOCAM), the Higher Education Institute in Teacher Training Diplomatic and Consular (INESDYC) and the National Institute of Technical and Professional Training (INFOTEP).

Likewise, recognition is given to the ongoing collaboration and contributions made by civil society, especially the academy, through the Dominican University Environmental Network (RAUDO); the efforts of the sector private sector, particularly through the Business Articulation Platform for Climate Action (PAEAC), also known as Businesses for Climate, and the support of international cooperation through various agencies and programs.

7.4. Just Transition

The Dominican Republic recognizes that climate action and the implementation of its commitments Mitigation and adaptation imply profound structural transformations in production systems, energy and territorial issues, with direct and indirect impacts on employment, living conditions work, the demand for skills, and the livelihoods of large segments of the population. In this In this context, the country adopts the just transition approach as a cross-cutting pillar of NDC 3.0 DR 2025, with the aim of ensuring the decarbonization process and strengthening resilience climate action should be carried out in a socially inclusive, equitable, and decent work-generating manner.

In accordance with the Preamble to the Paris Agreement and the Organization's Guidelines According to the International Labour Organization (ILO), a just transition is conceived as a process aimed at maximizing the social and economic benefits of climate action, while anticipating, preventing and manages the potential adverse impacts on workers, productive sectors and territories. This approach recognizes that the creation of green jobs, the transformation of occupations Existing jobs and the eventual disappearance of certain jobs are an inherent part of structural change associated with climate action, and that these processes must be managed in a planned manner, participatory and evidence-based.

Just transition is integrated into NDC 3.0 as a cross-cutting theme, articulated with the components of mitigation and adaptation, and aimed at strengthening coherence between climate policy, the employment policies, vocational training, productive development, and social protection. This This approach is particularly relevant for a country with high exposure to climate risks, a productive structure concentrated in climate-sensitive sectors and persistent challenges in the area of informal employment, gender gaps and territorial inequalities.

In this context, a just transition represents not only a challenge of structural change management- natural, but also a strategic opportunity for job creation and development sustainable production. The implementation of the mitigation and adaptation measures contemplated in NDC 3.0 opens up significant potential for green job creation, particularly in key sectors of the Dominican economy such as renewable energy and tourism. sustainable development, waste management, and environmental protection. According to preliminary estimates, there is a potential of up to 72,000 green jobs associated with the transformation of these sectors, which It positions climate action as a relevant driver for inclusive economic growth.

The approach to a just transition does not start from scratch in the Dominican Republic; it derives from the results of the preparation project “Mobilizing international climate finance and private investments for low-carbon development in the Dominican Republic” under the leadership of the MMARN, financed by the Green Climate Fund (GCF). In 2024, within the framework of this process, They developed prospective studies of the green labor market and sectoral analyses focused on anticipate the impacts of climate action on employment, skills and livelihoods, as well as to identify priority sectors, capacity gaps and financing needs. These exercises, led by the MMARN, were carried out in close coordination with the Ministry of Labor, the National Institute for Technical and Vocational Training (INFOTEP) and included the participation of actors from the productive sector, trade unions, academic sector and civil society. However, the findings derived from these exercises still require to materialize and operationalize through a series of strategic commitments and concrete actions, both of a cross-cutting and sectoral nature. These commitments are organized into four cross-cutting dimensions, complemented by priority sectoral actions, and guide the progressive implementation of the transition fair until the year 2030.

Governance of the just transition

  • By 2026, have a formally established national just transition governance mechanism established, which integrates the participation of actors from the world of work, the environmental sector and productive, and incorporate institutionalized spaces for social dialogue on just transition.

  • By 2027, publish and adopt a National Plan for a Just Transition and Promotion of Green Jobs, as a guiding instrument for the operationalization of the commitments established in NDC 3.0.

    Skills development and training for green jobs

  • By 2027, develop a National Strategy for Green Technical and Vocational Education and Training that includes updating study plans and programs aligned with the needs of the energy sectors, waste, sustainable tourism and environmental protection.

  • By 2026, have at least three prospective skills studies in high-skills sectors employability linked to the green economy.

    Active labor market policies

  • By 2030, to have incorporated just transition criteria and green jobs into policies active labor market services, including public employment intermediation services and programs employment.

    Promotion of green entrepreneurship and productive transformation

  • By 2027, develop and implement a capacity-building program for MSMEs, aimed at facilitating the transition towards greener, more sustainable and resilient production models.

    Priority sectoral actions

    In addition to cross-cutting commitments, the Dominican Republic is committed to promoting actions Specific sectors for a just transition. In particular:

  • By 2026, publish, within the framework of the National Plan for a Just Transition and Promotion of Jobs Greens, a sectoral roadmap for four priority activities: waste management, energy renewables, environmental protection and sustainable tourism.

7.5. Climate Risk Management

Disaster risk management is a cross-cutting theme of NDC 3.0 RD-2026 and a fundamental pillar to consolidate a coherent, territorially informed climate action aimed at strengthening Structural and social resilience of the country. The Dominican Republic, in its condition as a Small Island State Developing Countries (DIS), face recurring exposure to hydrometeorological and geophysical hazards that These events intensify under climate change scenarios. Hurricanes, prolonged droughts, urban flooding, coastal erosion, increased sea level rise and landslides do not represent isolated events, but rather dynamics that interact with pre-existing vulnerabilities in infrastructure, human settlements, production systems and ecosystems strategic. In this context, risk management is not limited to emergency response, but rather It is integrated as a preventive and structuring approach within development planning.

In accordance with Law No. 147-02 on Risk Management and Law No. 64-00 on the Environment and Natural Resources, climate risk management is linked to the National Planning System and Public Investment (SNIP), and with territorial planning in accordance with Law No. 368-22 and the National System of Climate Transparency (SNTC). This integration allows for the analysis of threats, exposure, and vulnerability should be progressively incorporated into the formulation of sectoral policies, in the evaluation of public investment projects and in climate monitoring and accountability processes.

Integrating climate risk management and land-use planning is essential to avoid generation of new risks or amplification of existing vulnerabilities to climate change. decisions related to territorial infrastructure, efficient energy systems, land use or Waste management, for example, must incorporate climate risk analyses that allow for a reduction in exposure to threats and strengthening the resilience of assets and infrastructure to climate events extremes. The planning of mitigation actions incorporates territorial considerations that allow to reduce exposure to climate threats and strengthen the security of strategic assets. In this In this sense, spatial observation and geospatial analysis systems contribute to monitoring changes in land use and cover, deforestation and restoration processes, urban expansion and exposure of critical infrastructure.

In the field of adaptation, risk management provides an operational perspective that facilitates Identification of territorial priorities and efficient allocation of resources. Watershed protection highlands, mangroves, coral reefs and other strategic ecosystems not only respond to objectives of conservation, but also their function as natural barriers against floods, storm surges and Coastal erosion. Similarly, resilient infrastructure planning in cities and settlements. Humans need to integrate multi-hazard analyses that consider future climate scenarios and trends historical impact.

The country has already taken steps to strengthen the assessment of risks and vulnerabilities in the face of the impacts of Climate change. In the process of updating the National Climate Change Adaptation Plan of Dominican Republic (PNACC-RD), climate vulnerability and risk assessments are being developed at the level regional through a spatial approach aimed at identifying risk levels, climate “hotspots” and priority actions in key economic sectors. Furthermore, the analysis, which focuses on the period 2040-2060, integrates the potential effects of climate change on natural, social and economic, which provide strategic information for decision-making and planning adaptation at a regional scale. Despite the limitations associated with data availability and the level of socioeconomic and sectoral desegregation, which restricts the estimation of specific impacts, the spatial approach It is recognized as a robust and functional framework for understanding regional climate risks. In this In this context, it is considered pertinent to move towards the development of sectoral models that allow for quantification damage to basic services, economic losses, and impacts on health and education.

As a concrete expression of this approach, the country has made progress in consolidating the National System of Multi-Threat Early Warning (SAT-M / SAT-MA), conceived as a robust and interoperable platform for to anticipate, monitor, and respond to emergencies. This system, operated by the Operations Center of Emergency Operations Center (COE) and developed through inter-institutional collaboration, has strengthened coordination technical access between entities responsible for planning, territorial information, and climate management. international financing, channeled through the Coalition for Resilient Infrastructure Disaster Risk Reduction and Development (CDRI) through the IRIS program has supported the development of databases and models of risk and early warning tools adapted to the country's island context.

The SAT-M framework guides the transition towards a multi-hazard early warning system supported by Geospatial information, integrating hydrometeorological hazards, earthquakes, fires, tsunamis and others relevant risks. This process has included institutional assessments of governance, knowledge of risk, monitoring and forecasting, dissemination mechanisms and preparedness for response, thus strengthening the national risk management architecture and the monitoring system, which is under development, Assessment and Learning (MEL).

Among the tools developed, the following stand out: Atlas of Risk and Vulnerability in Emergencies (ARVE / RVEA), which consolidates more than seventy layers of official geospatial information and integrates variables of threat, exposure, vulnerability, and response capacity at different territorial scales. This The tool allows prioritizing risks such as flooding from hydrometeorological events, strong winds, earthquakes, tsunamis, landslides, fires and vector-borne health risks, facilitating evidence-based decisions for territorial planning and resource allocation.

Likewise, the development of the Historical Geo-Record of Impacts contributes to systematizing events and impacts georeferenced since 1925, strengthening recurrence analysis and prospective planning. This The registry seeks to align with national and international standards and support reporting linked to the Framework. Sendai and the Sustainable Development Goals, promoting coherence between risk management and action climate and international commitments.

Strengthening geolocated alert mechanisms and developing territorial capacity indices Response tools, such as the Neighborhood Emergency Response Index (IRBE), reinforce the local dimension and community-based risk management, bringing information closer to citizens and promoting a culture preventive. The progressive expansion of alert dissemination capabilities through technologies of Communication strengthens the system's coverage and effectiveness. SAT-M governance is based on broad communication. inter-institutional participation and in the adoption of national geospatial information standards that They promote interoperability between entities within the National Risk Management System. The processes of Training and technical cooperation have contributed to strengthening national capacities in analysis spatial, construction of indicators and use of geographic tools for decision making.

Climate risk management complements the loss and damage chapter by facilitating assessments rapid post-event assessments, preliminary estimates of affected areas and exposed population, and historical analyses that support resilient recovery and reconstruction planning. In this way, DRM articulates the prevention, reduction, preparedness, response, and recovery phases within a comprehensive vision of climate risk.

In accordance with the Sendai Framework 2015–2030, the 2030 Agenda and the Paris Agreement, the Dominican Republic It consolidates a multi-hazard approach based on scientific evidence and territorial analysis, promoting the progressive integration of climate risk into public planning, strategic investment and decision-making interinstitutional decisions.

Without constituting new sectoral measures within this NDC, the advances described represent a foundation enabling factors to further integrate climate risk analysis in the upcoming cycles of planning, strengthening national resilience to present and future climate scenarios and consolidating the coherence between sustainable development and climate action.

8. Implementation Means

The Dominican Republic recognizes that achieving the climate commitments established in NDC 3.0 RD-2025 requires a robust framework of means of implementation, capable of mobilizing resources, driving innovation and strengthening public management. This section details the financial instruments, technological and capacity-building aspects that will facilitate the implementation of the prioritized actions.

8.1. Climate Finance

Climate finance is a key pillar for the implementation of NDC 3.0 RD2025, Therefore, the country will prioritize financial, regulatory, fiscal, and tax mechanisms. administrative and monitoring roles in the areas of sustainable finance, energy transition, nature-based solutions, circular economy and new green infrastructures for the adaptation. Through these mechanisms, the Dominican Republic invites the international community to already non-state actors, to contribute to the national effort with financial, technological and Capacity building to promote the implementation of the NDC. With the support of cooperation global and worldwide mobilization of resources to address climate change, Dominican Republic It can serve as an example by demonstrating that climate ambition can be an engine of prosperity. socioeconomic, improvement of equity and protection of the integrity of substantive elements of right to the environment, such as biodiversity and ecosystems.

Therefore, the successful implementation of this NDC will depend to a considerable extent on access to external means of implementation, primarily through non-reimbursable funds, financing on preferential terms, technology transfer, strengthening of capabilities and foreign direct investment from developed countries. The Republic The Dominican Republic believes that the provision of these resources by developed nations is fundamental to carrying out climate action within the framework of a just transition and the climate justice.

Despite adaptation efforts, the impacts of climate change continue causing losses and damage to Dominican society, its livelihoods, infrastructure and ecosystems. Therefore, it is essential that the country has access to specifically designed funds to address and avoid these consequences.

The Dominican Republic recognizes that climate finance is central to its agenda. Effective implementation of its NDC 3.0. The country has identified a significant gap between the nationally available resources and the investments needed to meet mitigation and adaptation goals. The availability of financial resources is a critical condition, essential- cial, for the fulfillment of the NDCs and the progressive increase of ambition in climate action in developing countries. The provision of these resources by developed countries must be consistent with the financing needs to address climate change that countries in development expressed in their NDCs, in accordance with the commitments made by developed countries in the framework of the international climate regime.

However, the provision of funds by developed countries, particularly resources public budgets are far from sufficient to achieve the objectives outlined in the NDC. Furthermore, the resources to finance adaptation are a small proportion of the total, and the The volume of concessional funds and donations is not adequate to meet the needs of financing.

These limitations are especially significant when capital costs in countries In developing countries, these rates are very high compared to those of advanced industrialized countries. External debt is often very high, while foreign direct investment is concentrated their flows within the developed economies themselves.

Likewise, developed countries have sought to replace the commitments made regarding Provision of domestic public resources through the mobilization of private capital, which is not always This means that there are funds available from that source for investment projects in adaptation or for to facilitate transitions to carbon-neutral economies in countries where the risks are potentially considerable.

The gap in ambition for climate action often largely reflects the gap in the Climate finance. Among the actions suggested to monitor the achievement of the goals and To promote transparency and coordination in the implementation of NDC 3.0 RD-2025, there is the creation of an implementation means matrix that links NDC goals, type of financing, responsible entity and potential source (GCF, GEF, IDB, CAF, etc.). This exercise will be developed and will expand within the framework of the preparation of the national climate finance platform with the GCF support.

Monitoring climate finance flows

Regarding Foreign Direct Investment (FDI), defined as financial flows between countries, where a foreign investor places their money in companies in other countries with the aim of to maintain this investment in the medium or long term, and having a minimum percentage of control 49. In the Dominican case, for statistical purposes, the Central Bank of the Dominican Republic (BCRD) It calculates FDI as the monetary flows of a foreign company that invests in a company Dominican and controls 10% or more of its capital. According to Lane and Milesi-Ferretti (2018), foreign direct investment (FDI) accounts for around 30% of the international liabilities and represents more than half of these liabilities in most countries in development50.

For the Dominican Republic, with USD 53,514.8 million accumulated up to 2023, FDI represents half of its liabilities. nationals with the rest of the world. A study by the Association of Foreign Investment Companies (ASIEX) In 2023, it details that foreign investment companies have a return on their investment of 5% (for 2022) and reinvest 60.9% of their net profits in the country; that is, the companies of Foreign investment reinvests its profits in the equivalent of 3% of its investment balance in the country, one of the highest in Latin America51.

Foreign direct investment (FDI) amounted to USD 4.381 billion in 2023, an increase of around 9.2% compared to the previous year. amount received in 2022, within which the evolution of the tourism, energy and sectors stands out In real estate, the main investing countries are: the United States, Canada, Brazil and Spain.

The leading sector was energy, which attracted USD 272.5 million. In terms of concentration, it is followed by... The tourism sector, which registered USD 271.9 million. According to “Tourism Doing Business: Dominican Republic Republic” (2022), thanks to the opening of foreign investment and its commercial integration with the rest of the world, the country is becoming the main recipient of FDI in the entire Caribbean, being one of the main sources of foreign exchange earnings for the country. In the period 2010 to 2020 they generated an average of USD 2,539 million per year and the tourism sector represented 21.35% of the accumulated FDI flows in the period, attracting more than 26 projects52.

In this regard, the country has developed a Methodology for monitoring and evaluating Public Spending Climate in the Dominican Republic , in order to advance the monitoring and evaluation of climate spending, which is crucial for to ensure that resources are used effectively in the fight against climate change. This has the potential to facilitate transparency, accountability, and the development of the empirical base, improve current interventions and expand learning to inform design and planning future interventions.

Following the interest in mapping and providing tracking of climate finance, the application of this The methodology has the potential to effectively leverage synergies between climate finance and International cooperation, through coordination between the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources Natural Resources (MMARN), the Ministry of Finance and Economy (MHE), and the Ministry of the Presidency (MINPRE) in their new role with non-reimbursable international cooperation.

By virtue of its position on climate policy and its vision on long-term sustainable development Over time, the Dominican Republic has progressively developed a legal and regulatory framework and has developed tools and mechanisms to access international and national financing resources, at the same time that encourages private participation in a way that makes climate action viable and aims to scale up the ambition in mitigation and materializing its adaptation strategies, improving the resilience of the society and natural systems.
