This aims to improve access to funds from different sources and create the conditions enabling factors to facilitate long-term transformations that make it possible to face the challenges emerging issues that raise the issue of decarbonization and strengthening resilience to change climate, while limiting the effects on the country of a geopolitical context that discourages international cooperation.

National Climate Finance Strategy (NCFS)

The Dominican Republic's NDC 3.0 describes a combination of unconditional and conditional commitments, thus recognizing the importance of the support needed to fully meet its climate ambitions. This framework, the National Climate Finance Strategy (NCFS) ) Currently in preparation, it can serve to strengthen the coordination of public and private resources. and international. This strategy will allow for a clear distinction between:

  • Unconditional resources, mobilized from national budgets, domestic private investment and Domestic fiscal instruments. These include those already supported by policies or regulations. national, with funding allocated through the national budget or already committed by other Sources of financing, including the private sector, both national and international.

  • Conditional resources, whose implementation depends on international cooperation, access to funds multilateral and innovative financing mechanisms. They encompass a broader range of high-level actions This impact can only be fully realized with international climate finance support. Support focuses on providing technical assistance for capacity building and projects for the Implementation of interventions. The conditionality of the objectives and actions committed to in the NDC 3.0 includes the protection of some of the most vulnerable sectors, such as biodiversity and eco- natural systems, coastal and marine resources, health, and infrastructure, which must be protected by making them climate-resilient, for which national funding is insufficient. Meeting these needs will require accessing climate finance through multiple channels bilateral and multilateral financial mechanisms, and development assistance, mechanisms based on Results and opportunities for access to resources under Article 6 of the Paris Agreement. Support Additionally, it will also be key to boosting public-private partnerships in potential areas with high initial costs or greater potential or perceived risks.

In this sense, the actions included in the NDC for climate change mitigation and adaptation They require an investment of more than USD 23.7 billion for the implementation of these actions, whether unconditional and conditional. The amount constitutes an estimated reference budget, the details of which This will be specified in line with the implementation needs of the measures and actions. This estimate has has been developed based on the information available, in accordance with the circumstances and national guidelines on this matter.

In developing economies and, to some extent, in the Dominican Republic, capital markets They tend to be still weak or insufficiently developed, and the level of funding is reduced. Long-term. However, companies require long-term financing for the acquisition of assets. fixed or for financing projects that may require a relatively long period for the recovery of the investment; however, commercial entities Local businesses can often only provide short-term loan facilities at interest rates relatively high and, in some cases, make the investment unfeasible.

Businesses, particularly SMEs, do not always have a solid and consistent financial structure to provide adequate guarantees to institutions that may provide potential sources of Financing. According to the structure of the financing composition (equity, Profits, credit) of this category of companies in the country, in general, tend to be financed with resources Own funds (capital and earnings), rather than third-party funds. Therefore, the need arises to Innovative financing mechanisms that support the private sector, primarily for the segments with less access to financing, and at the same time complement the development of green finance in the country. In general, the added value offered by debt and equity instruments is that they allow to reduce the funding gap specifically for certain projects and programs, and with its expansion And with easier access, the number of programs and projects could be increased. can implement.

Climate finance generally refers to the resources mobilized to facilitate to materialize or expand climate action and its provision, allocation and delivery, from public and Private funds, which are used to facilitate mitigation and adaptation measures, are needed. significant investments to support the country's transition to a low-emission future carbon and climate resilient, and international climate finance is essential, especially for developing countries, including the Dominican Republic.

Government resources alone cannot provide the necessary funding for this transition, therefore it is essential to mobilize private sector capital at the national level to achieve a transformative and lasting impact. Climate finance is highly effective in driving the long-term transformation, unlike short-term project-based financing and supply-oriented. The private sector is exposed to various climate-related risks that They can encourage the adoption of measures and investment. These risks range from risks that affect The entire economy, down to specific risks at the sectoral, industrial, or business level. The effects The adverse effects of these risks can be direct, such as damage to infrastructure and disruption of services. production processes; they can also be indirect, through the disruption of supply chains supply and changes in regulations, product demand, and corporate reputation. Therefore Therefore, climate finance accelerates investments that reduce vulnerability.

One of the main advantages of climate finance is the expansion of the pool of funds. available for development. This includes access to specific funds (international and, in (some cases, national), multilateral and bilateral development institutions, investors strategic private entities (such as pension funds), as well as non-governmental organizations and philanthropic. In addition, in some specific cases or sectors, more favorable instruments are available. (donations, seed funding, loans on favorable terms).

However, access to climate finance requires multiple levels of governance, including national and international actors and institutions; and it also involves a large number of policies national and sectoral, as well as various financial instruments to mobilize and deliver the funds, as well as the capacity to complete that work.

In Annex 6: Sources of Financing Details are presented on the various sources of climate finance. Their characteristics are described there. main features, in order to offer a more complete view of the financial landscape and potential opportunities for access to financing.

In this context, the Dominican Government will promote the use of sovereign thematic bonds, partnerships public-private climate initiatives (PPPs) and results-based payment mechanisms as means of mobilizing capital. At the international level, priority will be given to access to the Green Climate Fund (GCF), the Fund of Adaptation, and the climate windows of the World Bank and the IDB, among other sources that will need to be evaluated according to the country's financing needs. Participation will also be explored in carbon markets under Article 6 of the Paris Agreement, strengthening transparency and environmental integrity.

The instruments designed to promote the implementation of the NDC include:

  • Green procurement: In 2021 the Dominican Republic endorsed the Green Government Initiative (“Greening Government Initiative – GGI”) in order to accelerate climate action and promote sustainability in government operations. Sustainable and inclusive public procurement also seeks to to guarantee transparency and strict control of public funds, and to maximize the value of resources invested in social benefits and reducing the impact on the environment. In 2022, through the Decree No. 617 declares the promotion of sustainable public procurement to be of national interest and inclusive, understood as the acquisition of goods, works and services by the institutions of State, which at the same time generate a lower impact on the environment and maximize the impacts socioeconomic factors. In 2023, the Green Procurement Policy was published with the aim of promoting [the use of green procurement] in the National System of Public Procurement and Contracting: the incorporation of best practices, the introduction of management instruments and tools that allow public institutions to Acquisition of goods, services and works with less environmental impact, promoting the efficient use of resources and seek to obtain greater value for the allocated money. To date, the following have been published: 1,893 processes with an awarded amount of over RD$1.5 billion. Recently, the Executive Branch enacted Law No. 47-25 on public procurement, which includes a section to promote purchases State-level policies based on criteria of inclusion, sustainability, and respect for the environment. By 2025, the DGCP It published technical specifications for public procurement of goods such as lower-emission refrigeration equipment. This demonstrates that the Government is working on procurement policy tools that will allow Strengthen the catalog of goods and services with a focus on the environment and climate change.

  • Green Taxonomy: The CCSBSO Regional Taxonomy of Green Finance, published in December 2024, represents a pioneering effort in Latin America and the Caribbean to... to establish a common framework that promotes sustainable investments and facilitates mitigation and adaptation to Climate change in member countries. Similarly, the Green Taxonomy of the Dominican Republic, Published in June 2024, it positions the country as the first in the Caribbean to establish a framework for Transparency for green investments. “Taxonomy is defined as a classification system of economic activities and assets that contribute substantially to the achievement of environmental objectives and/or social, aligning with the commitments, strategies, and policies established by the country. Thus, the first green taxonomy Developed in the Dominican Republic, it acts as a framework that seeks to promote initiatives that support both sustainable development and the transition to a low-carbon economy.” With the The development and implementation of the taxonomy in the country seeks to direct “financial flows towards activities and assets that not only contribute significantly to environmental and climate objectives, but they also serve as catalysts for change towards a sustainable and resilient economy.” Taxonomy helps financial market participants use a common language and a common conceptualization regarding the policies, strategies, and commitments outlined by the government in Environmental matters, offering green financing instruments. By offering solid and uniform criteria for In addition to categorizing investments, taxonomy also facilitates the dissemination of information, as well as the Monitoring the use of mobilized resources. Establishing a taxonomy also facilitates Introduction of differentiated regulatory incentives based on the environmental and/or social impact of projects. The green taxonomy will be implemented gradually in the local context. In an initial phase, it will be used A volunteer program to promote climate action within the financial sector will be conducted periodically. updating the taxonomy, in accordance with the update times defined by the Monitoring Committee of Taxonomy.

  • Public Spending The Dominican government's planning and budgeting structure has been updated, and has begun to To make government investment in climate mitigation and adaptation visible in fiscal accounts. In 2022 The Conceptual Model of the System for Identifying and Classifying Public Spending on Exchange was developed Climate and Disaster Risk Management, published by the then Ministry of Economy, Planifica- Investment and Development, now integrated into the Ministry of Finance and Economy, which seeks to identify and quantify and to monitor public spending on climate change in the country. This model allows us to continue these efforts. to strengthen the correct classification of budgetary spending on climate change, protection of biodiversity, disaster risk management and activities that, by their technical nature, have Climate impact (CVI), both positive and negative. This document serves as the theoretical basis for new classification of expenditure associated with climate change from the Directorate General of Budget (DIGEPRES). Within the framework of the initial work on the development of the National Financing Framework Integrated Dominican Republic (INFF) for the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), the The United Nations Department of Economic and Social Studies (UNDESA-UN) conducted a study to label (“tagging”) government spending in relation to various categories linked to the different SDGs. Currently, the Directorate General of Budget (DIGE- PRES) began publishing public spending in line items that detail its impact on the exchange rate climate. Based on the classifier, a methodology for monitoring and evaluating the climate public spending. The 2024 Annual Budget Performance Evaluation Report It already includes climate spending.

  • Multi-Year Plans of the Public Sector The National Multi-Year Public Sector Plan (PNPSP) 2025-2028, which creates government planning in 17 policies, including objectives, strategic results and prioritized public production that contribute to the achievement of the objectives of the successive NDCs. In particular, “Environmental Sustainability and Climate Change "Climate" as a key value chain for the NDC, with objectives to increase forest cover, CO2 emissions reduction, improved risk management index, among others. Similarly, there was The following prioritized public production includes: increased forest cover; reduced pollution, among others. It is important to note that other supply chains Values also impact the achievement of the NDC objectives, among which the following stand out: access to water and improvement of the resource, permanent and quality energy, the transformation of the sector transportation, industrial development, among others.

  • Multi-Year Public Investment Plans: The National Multi-Year Public Investment Plans (PNPIP) They allow the performance of activities that contribute to the realization of the objectives and goals of the National Development Strategy (END), including Axis 3 (A territorially and sectorally organized economy) integrated, innovative, diversified, pluralistic, quality-oriented and environmentally sustainable, which creates and It decentralizes wealth, generates high and sustained growth with equity and decent employment, and takes advantage of and enhances local market opportunities and integrates competitively into the global economy) and the Axis 4 (A society with a culture of sustainable production and consumption, which manages equitably and efficiently the risks and the protection of the environment and natural resources and promotes adequate adaptation to climate change). In 2024, the PNPIP 2025-2028 incorporates for the first time the dimensions associated with climate change. climate and gender, identifying public investment projects that have an impact on both dimensions, using the criteria of the budget classifier for such purposes.

  • PEFA Climate: In 2024, the performance of the public finance management system was assessed. as effective support for the implementation of public policies on climate change in the country, in accordance with the Framework for Assessing Public Finance Management for Climate Change. It is highlighted the construction of budget classifiers and programmatic structures, the preparation of the circular budgetary measures for climate change, forecasts of fiscal risks from climate change, and the green purchasing policy (already detailed above).

  • Medium-Term Public Debt: The Dominican Republic has developed a Medium-Term Strategy for the Public Debt Management 2024-2028. It establishes that in order to integrate financing Sustainable debt management seeks to develop financial instruments that allow investment in Projects and activities with a positive impact on the environment and society. On the one hand, it is defined as The objective is to move towards the consolidation of the thematic bond market, making these an integral part of the Medium-Term Debt Management Strategy. In turn, obtaining sources of financing will be encouraged. Alternative financing options to thematic bonds; multilateral and bilateral organizations are playing a role an important role in mobilizing funds for sustainable development. In parallel with the creation of New instruments that facilitate the mobilization of sustainable financing will be promoted, as will the creation of frameworks to enable the large-scale adoption of these instruments, such as the first framework of reference to green, social and sustainable bonds of the Dominican Republic, already prepared, whose purpose is channel resources to finance the country's sustainable finance strategy and social investment.

  • Carbon pricing: The country has been working on a set of policies aimed at reducing emissions, among them are efforts to introduce a price on greenhouse gas emissions greenhouse effect, so that certain emission-intensive technologies are relatively less competitive from an economic point of view. Among these taxes or contributions, the following can be mentioned: ecological taxes, the special contribution fund for the integrated management and co-processing of solid waste (Law No. 225-20), and the resources of direct collection for the promotion and development of natural gas in the vehicle fleet. On the other hand, progress has been made since the establishment of a vehicle tax high-displacement engines, constituting the first effort made to penalize CO2 emissions. It had Its origin lies in a package of tax reforms that were introduced in 2012, with the aim of starting to reduce emissions and achieve compliance with the END indicators. The CO2 tax that is This applies to new and used vehicles imported into the market according to their carbon emissions in grams. per kilometer traveled.

    Since 2018, the Dominican Republic has also been part of the countries supported by the Secretariat of the UNFCCC, based on a request from the Parties made at COP22, of the Instruments initiative Collaboration for Ambitious Climate Action (CiACA). Within this framework, it has developed a study to date. for the introduction of a carbon pricing instrument and has drawn up a Roadmap for the identification of the key characteristics and actions for the implementation of a Trading System Domestic Emissions.

  • Accreditation to climate funds: The country has made progress in the accreditation of national entities to international climate funds as a mechanism to increase access to climate finance, among They, the MARENA Fund before the GCF and the Dominican Institute for Integral Development (IDDI) which acts as National Implementing Entity (NIE) of the Climate Change Adaptation Fund.

  • Debt-for-nature swap strategy: The feasibility of implementing a debt-for-nature swap transaction has been analyzed for This mechanism allows a country to exchange its sovereign external debt in exchange for financing projects to finance the conservation of its natural heritage. Nature conservation. These investments include programs to increase climate resilience of country, to protect biodiversity, rehabilitate degraded ecosystems, among other things. This In this way, the impact of climate change on public finances is considered and incorporated into decision-making. decision.

  • Payments for environmental services (PES): This mechanism seeks to financially reward those who contribute to the protection and restoration of natural resources and ecosystem services. The services Environmental benefits are the benefits that ecosystems provide to society, such as the purification of Water, climate regulation, crop pollination, and protection against natural hazards. PES They are based on the premise that people who conserve or restore ecosystems provide a benefit to society as a whole, whether by mitigating climate change, conserving biodiversity, or protecting the Water resources. The Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) financed PES programs for reforestation. and forest cover care in the Dominican Republic through three loans totaling US$300 million in 2018 (loans between the IDB and the Dominican Republic as follows: 1) DR-L1120 for US$150 million; 2) DR-L1134 for US$105.63 million; and, 3) DR-L1135 for US$44.37 million).

  • Public-Private Partnerships (PPPs): Through the joint participation of private and governmental entities, a PPP combines the skills and resources from both sectors are combined so that risks and responsibilities are shared. This It allows the government to benefit from the expertise of the private sector under mixed investment schemes. In particular, in the Dominican Republic there is a PPP framework for the active participation of the sector private. Law No. 47-20 on Public-Private Partnerships, enacted on February 20, 2020, establishes PPPs as “the mechanism by which public and private agents voluntarily enter into a contract of long term, as a result of a competitive process, for the provision, management or operation of goods or social interest services in which there is total or partial investment by private agents, contributions tangible or intangible assets on the part of the public sector, risk distribution between both parties, and the Remuneration is linked to performance as established in the contract” (MEPYD). The introduction of APP as a way to involve the private sector in climate change mitigation and adaptation projects Climate allows, among other things, that:

    • The private sector should introduce technology and innovation, as well as high standards of operational efficiency

    • the projects are delivered on time and within the agreed budget

    • local capacities are developed

    • Businesses and associated industries are boosted through infrastructure development

  • Dominican Multiple Banking Green Protocol: Collaborative initiative, from March 2024, which establishes the Action plan to which the entities of the Dominican banking system commit to address the growing environmental and climate challenges and create the conditions for productive dynamics conducive to Sustainable and inclusive development. It represents a growth strategy for Dominican multiple banks. focused on environmental sustainability, with a view to promoting a resource-efficient economy, and aligned with the goal of achieving net-zero greenhouse gas emissions by 2050. an initiative promoted by the Association of Multiple Banks of the Dominican Republic (ABA) in Collaboration with the International Finance Corporation (IFC), part of the World Bank Group. The Green Protocol It also aims to protect, conserve and improve the natural capital of the Dominican Republic, by at the same time as it seeks to preserve the integrity and well-being of citizens in the face of challenges environmental. The document was signed by the Dominican banks Popular, BHD, Banreservas, Citibank, Caribe, Santa Cruz, BDI, Banesco, Promerica, Scotiabank, López de Haro, Vimenca, Lafise, Ademi, JMMB and Qik Bank. In order to achieve its objectives, the Green Protocol is composed of five strategic pillars, each with specific objectives: i) Strategy and Governance for Sustainability; ii) Solutions Green; iii) Environmental, Social and Climate Risk Management; iv) Eco-efficiency and Decarbonization Practices; and, v) Measurement and Disclosure. As part of the green solutions, the following are proposed: Green loans, green mortgages, green leasing, green credit cards, climate insurance, green bonds and capital markets, “project finance ” in renewable energy, energy efficiency, agriculture, among others.

  • Themed Bonds: Thematic values emerge as a key tool for mobilizing capital towards projects that address these challenges. These financial instruments allow for the financing of initiatives with an impact positive, aligning with national and international sustainability goals. The country has published in 2024 Reference Framework for Green, Social and Sustainable Bonds of the Dominican Republic , aligned with international standards, which governs the issuance of financial instruments of this type by the public sector. In this way, the aim is to protect the environment and the needs social and sustainable development, as well as ensuring strict transparency in the use and management of the funds. In this sense, in order to integrate sustainable financing into debt management Publicly, the aim is to develop financial instruments that allow resources to be channeled towards projects and Activities with a positive impact on the environment and society. To achieve this, the aim is to consolidate the market. of thematic bonds, making these an integral part of the Medium-Term Debt Management Strategy Timeframe. Increasing sovereign issuance of ESG (environmental, social, and governance) bonds allows to establish a benchmark curve for public and private issuers seeking funds, both in the external as well as the local sphere, and enables presence in indices such as the JESG-EMBI, which serves as benchmark for sustainable debt issued in international markets. This framework of The reference establishes a state committee for thematic bonds to define financing priorities for programs. In this regard, the country has already made progress in issuing thematic bonds. In June 2024, the The Dominican government, through the Ministry of Finance, issued a green bond. It was the first placement of This type of bond from the country, for an amount of 750 million dollars with a premium reduction or “greenium ” of approximately 15 basis points versus similar bonds in the secondary market. In 2024, BANFONDESA issued the first sustainable bond dedicated to microfinance in the Dominican Republic for an amount of RD$500 million to finance social impact projects, green initiatives, or a combination of Both. Thematic bonds, including green, social, gender, blue, sustainable, and linked bonds Sustainability facilitates the expansion of the investor base, as it fosters interest in part of institutions that choose to invest in thematic bonds, due to their broad commitment to Mitigation of and adaptation to the effects of climate change, for both the public and private sectors private. It also improves the corporate image, reputation, and visibility as a leader in sustainability. This, accompanied by an obligation to be transparent about the impact and use of the funds. In this In this sense, the “Guide for the Issuance of Thematic Securities”59 was published, a roadmap for issuers interested in structuring green, social or sustainable bonds under international standards. With the aim of promoting the issuance of sustainable, green securities and In 2020, the Superintendency of the Securities Market of the Dominican Republic published the social data. Guidelines for the issuance of sustainable, green and social public offering securities in the Market Country values. For private sector thematic debt issuers, it is key to make progress in the governance in sustainability, definition of sustainability strategies, identification of eligible projects, environmental and social risk management systems, adopting frameworks Robust for issuing these instruments, using and managing the funds, and preparing annual reports. The company Dominican company EGE Haina issued a sustainability-linked security in November 2021, which was placed in international markets for an amount of USD 300 million, marking a milestone for the local private sector. In May 2024, Banco Popular Dominicano made the first issuance of a green security by an entity financial, with the placement of RD$300 million to be allocated to projects with environmental benefits.

  • Innovative financial mechanisms: In accordance with the regulatory framework, taxonomy, protocols, frameworks of References and guides are used to develop sustainability-related solutions through mechanisms innovative financial products that are in the analysis, design or implementation phase in the Republic Dominican Republic, among which we can mention:

    Parametric Insurance60: The initiative, titled “Parametric Insurance for Vulnerable Populations in the Dominican Republic”, seeks to cover the risks of drought, wind and heavy rains for 10,000 beneficiary heads of household from the Supérate program, for the 2026 hurricane season. This initiative has the support of InsuResilience Solutions Fund (ISF), a global platform supported by more than 120 members of governments, private sector, academia, civil society, and international organizations. Funded by The German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ) has created the fund to support the development of innovative climate risk insurance products, and contribute to increase the capacity of developing countries to adapt to climate change. The Government of Germany has allocated 1 million euros for the design, development and implementation of the project in the Dominican Republic. This instrument will be developed in close coordination with Supérate. Members of the “Insurance Development Forum”, made up of Guy Carpenter Mexico, AXA Climate, Blue Marble, and Celsius Pro and Seguros Reservas, with the facilitation and technical assistance of the Program of the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP). Also, in 201161

  • “Green Leasing”62: At least since 2020, in the Dominican Republic there has been leasing focused on green products such as electric vehicles and solar panels for commercial entities. This type of instrument is a financial lease agreement that gives the client the right to use and enjoyment of movable and immovable property for a specified period, during which ownership of The asset belongs to the lessor, in this case, the bank. At the end of the contract, the asset becomes the property of the lessee. client or lessee. Banco Popular of the Dominican Republic was a pioneer in this type of product.

  • Green loans: Financing for low environmental impact technologies, such as renewable energy renewables from solar panels, or hybrid and electric vehicles, boosting mobility sustainable or facilitate access to credit to improve environmental quality outcomes of the productive sector in rural areas of the country, based on the reduction of the use of agrochemicals, savings water, waste management and the reduction of sources of pollution in the processes productive.

  • Local climate investment funds: Local climate investment funds represent a mechanism key to advancing NDC compliance. By channeling resources towards mitigation projects and adaptation within the country, these funds facilitate the implementation of priority initiatives, especially in sectors such as energy, water, agriculture, tourism, and risk management. Furthermore, by attracting Private capital, combined with other sources of financing, helps to close the gap in resources to create a more dynamic and sustainability-oriented financial market.

  • Risk reduction mechanisms such as insurance: Risk reduction mechanisms, such as weather insurance, can be an instrument effective for the effective implementation of the NDC in the Dominican Republic. These instruments They allow the transfer and management of risks associated with extreme weather events such as hurricanes and floods. droughts that can severely affect strategic sectors such as agriculture, infrastructure, energy and water resources. By offering financial protection against losses, insurance provides stability to producers, businesses and local governments, facilitating the continuity of projects and reducing the economic vulnerability. Furthermore, when these are linked to early warning systems and programs of Community resilience, climate insurance drives investment in preventative measures, and fosters Evidence-based adaptation and generate incentives for risk reduction. They can also insurance policies are designed to reduce or transfer the risks associated with performance uncertainty. new or innovative technologies, encouraging their adoption and accelerating the transition to solutions more efficient and sustainable. Together, these mechanisms strengthen the country's capacity to to meet its climate goals, promoting sustainable and resilient development. There are already [measures/measures] in the country Examples of insurance to reduce climate risk in certain sectors, such as, for example, in the case of AGRODOSA63, which offers agricultural insurance that guarantees investment against unpredictable events such as cyclones, tornadoes, floods and drought.

To promote the guarantee and respect for human rights, the use of the detailed instruments must be subject to the identification of social, environmental and human rights risks, as well as compliance with applicable social, environmental and human rights safeguards.

In addition to access to financing, long-term investment in human and institutional capacity, Access to appropriate technologies and cohesive and centralized monitoring and reporting systems will be fundamental to achieving the NDC objectives.

The Government of the Dominican Republic remains fully committed to collaborating with regional partners and international agreements to ensure that assistance reaches where it is most needed, thus laying the foundations for a an inclusive, equitable, sustainable and prosperous future that protects the entire population and natural systems of country.

8.2. Technology Transfer

Technology transfer is a strategic pillar for accelerating the transition to a new model resilient and low-carbon development. It also allows for strengthening national capacities to Climate change mitigation and adaptation, consistent with sustainable development priorities of the country. In this context, the Dominican Republic has made progress in strengthening the transfer technological through the General Policy and Strategy for the Operation of the National Network Centers for Prototypes and Technology Transfers (CPTT Network)64, a public policy instrument that establishes an institutional, operational and governance framework for Facilitating access to technologies, prototyping services, technical training, and support specialized. This policy promotes collaboration between the State, the productive sector, academia, and international cooperation, fostering the adaptation and local appropriation of technologies, the development of national capabilities and applied innovation are laying relevant foundations for technological adoption. progressive, inclusive and sustainable

As a priority, efforts will be made to ensure that technology transfer is accompanied by programs for training, access to financing and enabling regulatory frameworks, thus avoiding dependency technological and ensuring a just transition. Within the framework of NDC 3.0, the country will strengthen an approach strategic technology transfer that:

  1. Promote the adaptation and local appropriation of technologies, prioritizing solutions appropriate to national and territorial context.

  2. Promote national innovation, applied research, and technological development, including alliances with universities, research centers and the private sector.

  3. Integrate just transition considerations, ensuring that technology adoption contributes to the creation of decent employment, development of local capacities and reduction of social gaps and territorial.

  4. Strengthen coherence between public policies by aligning technology transfer with the NDC, the National Adaptation Plan, sectoral policies and productive development strategies.

Important National Advances and Experiences

The Dominican Republic has been developing and actively participating in multiple initiatives that They lay the foundations for a comprehensive approach to technology transfer, including:

  • Implementation of clean technologies in the energy sector, particularly through the expansion of renewable energies (solar, wind and biomass), the modernization of electricity networks and the use of intelligent energy measurement and management systems, supported by cooperation international and climate finance.

  • Incorporation of technologies for climate adaptation, such as monitoring systems hydrometeorological, climate modeling, early warning tools, risk management and nature-based solutions for coastal protection, water security and resilience agricultural.

  • Strengthening digital and institutional capacities through information platforms climate, monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) systems, greenhouse gas inventories greenhouse, and tools for data-driven decision making.

8.3. Capacity Development and Strengthening

Capacity building constitutes a strategic and cross-cutting means of implementation for to guarantee the effective implementation of commitments to mitigation, adaptation, just transition and climate transparency established in the Nationally Determined Contribution 3.0 of the Republic Dominican Republic.

NDC 3.0 recognizes that achieving its emissions reduction and climate resilience goals depends of the sustained development of technical, institutional and human capacities, both at the national level and subnational, which allow for the design, implementation, monitoring and adjustment of policies, measures and actions climate in the prioritized sectors.

In this sense, capacity building within the framework of NDC 3.0 is geared towards:

  1. Capacities for the implementation of mitigation measures

    Priority is given to strengthening technical and sectoral capacities for planning, execution and monitoring of Mitigation Policies, Measures and Actions (MPAs), including the development of scenarios, the use of emissions quantification methodologies, and the development of capacities for new Low-carbon technologies and the identification of economic and social co-benefits. These Capabilities are essential to sustain climate ambition and improve the efficiency of investments and ensure the progression of mitigation goals towards 2030 and 2035.

  2. Capacities for adaptation and climate risk management

    NDC 3.0 reinforces the need for capacity building for planning and implementation of adaptation measures at the national and local levels, considering the prioritized sectors and management of risk, ecosystem-based adaptation and the use of climate information for decision-making decisions. These capabilities allow for the reduction of vulnerabilities and the strengthening of territorial resilience. and minimize losses and damages associated with extreme weather events.

  3. Capacities for mainstreaming other complementary approaches

    This NDC recognizes the need to strengthen capacities at different levels to integrate cross-cutting approaches to gender, human rights, and Climate Empowerment Action (ACE), early childhood, childhood and youth, recognizing that mitigation and adaptation require specific skills to address differentiated impacts, promote meaningful participation and to ensure inclusive and equitable processes in the implementation of the NDC.

  4. Capacities for transparency and monitoring of climate commitments: Implementation Effective mitigation and adaptation actions require strong monitoring capabilities, reporting and verification (MRV), monitoring the adaptation and operation of the National System Climate Transparency (SNTC). These capabilities strengthen the traceability of the commitments of the NDCs facilitate accountability and ensure consistency with the Enhanced Transparency Framework (ETF).

  5. Capacities for the generation and use of climate knowledge

    This NDC recognizes the need to continue strengthening capacities for generation, systematization and use of scientific, technical and socioeconomic information that supports the formulation of Public policies for mitigation and adaptation, the prioritization of investments, and the evaluation of climate impacts. This axis includes collaboration with academic institutions, centers of research and national climate observation systems.

Capacity building will be driven by national resources and cooperation international, technical assistance and strategic alliances with public, private, academic and of civil society. In this way, NDC 3.0 DR-2025 consolidates capacity building as a key factor in transforming climate commitments into measurable results, sustaining the climate ambition and moving towards resilient, low-emission and socially just development.

Capacity building is a necessary condition to ensure the sustainability of the action climate in the country. The Dominican Republic will promote a comprehensive approach that includes:

  • Technical capabilities: training in measurement, reporting and verification (MRV), climate modeling, Risk management, energy transition, and ecosystem-based adaptation. As well as the identification and management of human rights risks arising from climate change, promoting technical approaches that integrate principles of climate justice.

  • Institutional capacities: strengthening inter-ministerial coordination and coordination with governments local areas, with special emphasis on coastal and rural municipalities.

  • Social capacities: education, awareness and community participation programs that include women, youth and vulnerable populations.

The following are some of the existing strategic alliances and national programs. in the country for capacity building, conceived as a multi-actor process, where academia, government, the private sector, and international cooperation are coming together to accelerate action climate.

Academia and the scientific sector:

  • Network of Environmental Universities of the Dominican Republic (RAUDO): integrates 16 universities in research and training programs on climate change and sustainability (CNCCMC, 2023).

  • The Technological Institute of Santo Domingo (INTEC) and UASD offer diplomas and master's degrees in management. Climate change and renewable energy.

Government and public institutions:

  • National Council for Climate Change and Carbon Market (CNCCMC): coordinates programs of training on climate policies and MRV (Decree 358-25).

  • Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MMARN): leads the implementation of Law 64-00 and training programs in environmental management and climate change. Ministry of Energy and Mines (MEM) : promotes training in energy transition and efficiency, in coordination with organizations multilateral.

Civil society and the private sector:

  • Business Alliance for Sustainability of the National Council of Private Enterprise (CONEP): It promotes training in circular economy and climate risk management for companies.

  • Environmental NGOs (Sur Futuro, Fundación Moscoso Puello, Fundación Plenitud): develop programs community education and climate resilience.

International organizations and cooperation:

  • MMARN-UNDP-CEDAF – CBIT RD Project (2019–2023): strengthened national capacities in climate transparency and MRV.

  • Inter-American Development Bank (IDB): supports training in climate finance management and urban resilience.

  • Spanish Agency for International Development Cooperation (AECID) and GIZ: implement technical training programs and climate governance in the water and energy sectors.

    • FVC-UNEP-MMARN Project Developing Capacities to advance the Plan process National Climate Change Adaptation Plan.

9. Climate Transparency

9.1. Advances in climate transparency

In recent years, the Dominican Republic has made significant progress in the construction and implementation of its Enhanced Transparency Framework (ETF), in compliance with the provisions of the Article 13 of the Paris Agreement. This process has involved the development of regulatory frameworks, institutional, methodological and technological aspects that lay the foundations of the National System of Climate Transparency (SNTC), and which allow the country to report with greater quality, consistency and periodicity of progress on their climate commitments.

The initial milestone, following the adoption, signing and subsequent ratification by the country of the Agreement From Paris, was the promulgation of Decree No. 541-20, which created the National Monitoring System, Report and Greenhouse Gas Monitoring and Verification (MRV), and which also established the basis for a monitoring and evaluation of adaptation actions. This legal provision marked the starting point for the Institutionalization of the ETF in the country. Subsequently, a series of actions have been implemented. strategic measures that have allowed the system to be progressively strengthened:

  • Creation of the Department of Metrics and Transparency within the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (through Resolution No. 017/2014) as the technical unit in charge of the System National Greenhouse Gas Inventory.

  • Development of the Roadmap for the implementation of the National MRV System (ICAT II-M, 2021), that identifies the priorities, actors, and steps needed to move towards an integrated system of transparency.

  • Initiative for Climate Transparency in Adaptation (ICAT-A, 2019-2022), which identified the baseline for the future definition and establishment of a national Monitoring and Evaluation methodology (M&E) of adaptation measures in the agriculture and tourism sectors.

  • Climate data portal (TNC, 2021), with the objective of having a repository for registration, monitoring and evaluation of adaptation actions.

  • Design and creation of the online platform for adaptation actions and measures, M&E (AECID, 2022), for the inclusion of missing components of the national transparency system climate.

  • Creation of the Climate Transparency Department (CDT) within the National Council for the Climate Change and Carbon Market (CNCCMC), as the technical unit responsible for coordinating and consolidate national climate information (CNCCMC-WB, 2022).

  • Conceptual design of the Electronic Climate Transparency Platform, which represents the first systematic effort to build a robust digital infrastructure to manage information climate (CNCCMC-UNDP/Climate Promise, 2022).

  • Implementation of the online tool “NDC-P – DR Action Plan”, a web application that It allows tracking the achievement of NDC goals through the recording and monitoring of policies and actions through the NDC Partnership portal (CNCCMC-MEPyD, 2022-2023).

  • Technical training through the CBIT-1 project, with diplomas and training programs in GHG and ETF inventories, developed by the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MMARN) with support from UN Environment and the GEF (2021-2023).

  • Development and dissemination of the National Transparency System Guidelines, as well as the preparation of draft inter-institutional agreements to define governance rules, roles and flows of information (CNCCMC-ICAT, 2023-2024).

  • Design of the National Climate Transparency Framework and creation of the electronic platform of Transparency System, currently under development with support from AECID for the period 2024–2026.

  • Preparation of the first Biennial Transparency Report combined with the Fourth National Communication (BTR1/CN4), which consolidates for the first time national progress in mitigation, adaptation, support received and needs, under the new ETF guidelines.

These advances reflect a systematic evolution towards the implementation of an institutional system of Robust and sustainable climate transparency in the Dominican Republic, with a focus on improvement continuous development of national capacities, inter-institutional integration and adoption of tools technological solutions that allow compliance with international requirements and, at the same time, improve decision-making internal decisions for the creation of more effective and efficient public policies in the fight against Climate change. The creation of a specific subsystem for the NDC within the SNTC is part of This trajectory, as a response to the need for more robust, coordinated and participatory mechanisms to manage the country's climate commitments.

9.2. Description of the NDC subsystem within the SNTC

As part of the NDC development process, the Dominican Republic has undertaken the task of designing a subsystem for updating the NDC within the SNTC, based on the previous design of the module of “NDC tracking”. The new NDC subsystem significantly expands the scope of that initial component, since it is not limited solely to the technical monitoring of commitments. Through This subsystem will be used to formulate and update the NDC based on the inputs generated. For the various subsystems of the SNTC, an Action Plan will be developed to guide its implementation, and They will produce outreach materials to communicate the country's progress and results in the area of climate action.

The objective of the NDC subsystem is to establish a mechanism for updating and monitoring The Dominican Republic's NDC within the SNTC, coordinating with the other subsystems to receive technical inputs and information. Its operation is based on processes of analysis and evaluation of the ambition and participatory consultation with relevant actors, ensuring compliance with the ETF requirements and the provision of information necessary for the Biennial Transparency Reports (BTR). In addition, the subsystem is linked to broader planning instruments, such as plans territorial and sectoral strategies, to ensure that data, analysis and indicators contribute to decision-making and effective compliance with NDC commitments.

The main tasks of the NDC subsystem are concentrated in three fundamental areas: development of the NDC, the development of the Action Plan and the monitoring of progress towards the objectives established in the NDC. Regarding the development of the NDC, the subsystem does not directly produce all of the It does not provide technical information, but rather compiles, consolidates, and organizes inputs from other subsystems of the SNTC, such as GHG inventories, climate actions and financing needs, transforming that Evidence-based information for strategic decision-making on climate ambition and commitments sectoral and content of the NDC components. These inputs, collected and processed in a coordinated by the responsible institutions in each subsystem of the SNTC, they are delivered with due consistency methodological and compatibility with international standards. Subsequently, once the NDC is approved, The subsystem develops the Action Plan, which translates the commitments into concrete actions, identifying responsible actors, policy instruments, resources, timeframes and internal monitoring indicators, organizing information strategically and ensuring its inter-institutional coordination. Finally, the subsystem continuously monitors progress towards the NDC goals through the definition and updating of measurable and verifiable indicators, as well as the collection, processing and dissemination of annual information that allows for the evaluation of compliance with commitments national and provide data for the Biennial Transparency Reports, ensuring consistency, traceability and effectiveness of the NDC planning, implementation and monitoring cycle.

The NDC subsystem involves various actors with clearly defined roles and responsibilities defined, which ensure the coherence, technical robustness and political legitimacy of the process within the SNTC. The coordinating entity of the subsystem is the Climate Transparency Department (DTC) of the National Council for Climate Change and Carbon Market (CNCCMC), in charge of coordination technical-operational aspects, process planning, coordination with other subsystems, and management of the technical inputs and the preparation of the NDC drafts. In his role as general coordinator From the SNTC, the CNCCMC also oversees that the various actors follow up on the implementation of the NDC and channels progress to the subsystem. Strategic management of the process rests with an ad-hoc committee. for the formulation of the NDCs, which acts as the governing body of the subsystem and is made up of the CNCCMC, the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MMARN) and the Ministry of Finance and Economy (MHE). In this committee, the CNCCMC and the MMARN function as focal points to the UNFCCC. while the CNCCMC, MMARN and MHE lead the international relations linked to the NDC and They ensure their consistency with the country's multilateral commitments. Each ministry also contributes from their specific mandates in environmental management, sectoral and territorial planning, and allocation climate budget.

The Ad-hoc Committee operates on two levels: political-strategic, made up of the heads of the institutions mentioned, and technical-operational, made up of specialists and subject matter experts designated by these institutions and other entities. This technical level is responsible for the control of quality of the products and results of the subsystem, including the review of the draft NDCs, the validation of methodologies used to estimate goals, impacts, and support needs, the consistency of the monitoring indicators with the rest of the SNTC, compliance with the policies social and human rights, and verification of the internal consistency of the NDC with other national policies, such as the National Development Strategy, the Baja California Development Strategy Long-Term Emissions, the National Climate Change Adaptation Plan and sectoral plans and territorial. In this way, the subsystem articulates the technical information coming from other modules. The SNTC organizes broad consultations with public, private, academic and civil society institutions civil, and guarantees the transparency, legitimacy and technical quality of the formulation process, NDC update and implementation.

Taken together, this module constitutes an essential component of the SNTC, providing a robust framework for the NDC update that integrates technical inputs, analysis and validation processes, and a Clear governance based on defined roles and responsibilities. Its design allows not only for compliance with international transparency and reporting obligations, but also to strengthen the national capacities to plan and adjust climate actions based on evidence and ongoing dialogue among different sectors and actors involved.

9.3. Indicators for monitoring NDC commitments

Effective NDC monitoring requires a robust, operational, and fully functional indicator system integrated into the SNTC. In the Dominican Republic, the indicators compiled to date within the framework of the NDC Partnerships cover a wide variety of areas and sectors. However, their large number and the lack of prioritization and the absence of indicators specifically designed to measure compliance with NDC mitigation commitments have created difficulties for decision-making and traceability. of the progress and the preparation of mandatory reports such as the BTR.

The analysis carried out within the framework of NDC 3.0 highlighted the need for a a reduced subset of strategic indicators that allow for direct and verifiable monitoring the achievement of the central objective of reducing greenhouse gas emissions by 28%. (GHG) to 2030, compared to the business-as-usual (BAU) scenario. These strategic indicators have been defined to ensure its alignment with the international commitments undertaken and to facilitate reporting transparent and timely before the UNFCCC.

The prioritized set includes three indicators directly linked to mitigation (total reduction) of emissions, emissions reduction attributable to domestic financing and reduction attributable to international financing) and a fourth cross-cutting indicator aimed at monitoring the frameworks enabling factors established, in recognition of their key role in the effective implementation of measures climatic.

The responsibility for managing and updating these indicators lies with the NDC subsystem. while detailed monitoring of individual mitigation measures will continue in the thematic subsystems of the SNTC, ensuring coherence and traceability between actions, results intermediate and aggregate impacts.

9.4. Monitoring, Evaluation and Adaptive Learning (MEL)

Monitoring, Evaluation, and Learning (MEL) is a systematic approach that allows for reviewing the progress of climate policies and actions, identifying lessons learned, improving decision-making and Strengthen the implementation of adaptation and mitigation strategies. Through the MEL, the actors of systems can ensure that the actions taken are effective, consistent, and aligned with the national and international objectives.

In the Dominican Republic, the MEL plays a central role within the SNTC, which constitutes the framework institutional and technical resources to comply with the Paris Agreement ETF. The SNTC ensures the collection, analysis and communication of climate information to monitor NDC commitments and report both nationally and internationally. Its operation is based on Decree No. 541-20, which established the GHG MRV system, and assigned its coordination to the CNCCMC, incorporating in 2023 the Department of Climate Transparency (DTC) as an operational unit.

Although the SNTC has several functional subsystems that respond to the requirements of the ETF, these They still do not fully cover all the information needs on climate action, especially in adaptation. For this reason, the CNCCMC is currently working alongside other state institutions. Dominican, in the need to deepen and strengthen MEL's efforts in adaptation, ensuring that the policies and actions implemented are effective, consistent, and evidence-based.

Within this framework, the contracting of specialized consulting services for the “Design and creation of the National MEL System of Adaptation measures, with its legal and regulatory framework in the Dominican Republic.” This process aims to develop the adaptation subsystem within the SNTC, facilitating the availability and periodic presentation of country adaptation information.

In preparation for this development, the Dominican Republic is making significant progress in the design of a national M&E subsystem for adaptation. This subsystem is designed with a focus participatory, multi-level and multi-sectoral, guided by the ICAT methodology and the National Plan Adaptation (PNACC 2015–2030). The subsystem visualizes the monitoring of both processes (planning, institutional framework, financing) as well as results (reduction of vulnerability, increase of resilience), and aims to generate empirical evidence on the effectiveness of the implemented measures, to facilitate learning, improve decision-making, and promote accountability.

The subsystem could be structured into three key areas: (1) recording of adaptation measures implemented; (2) monitoring and evaluation of their effectiveness, impact and coverage; and (3) losses and damages, which It quantifies the adverse impacts of climate change. These areas are projected to operate through interoperable electronic platforms, with the aim of using standardized tools for the data collection, involving both public institutions and non-governmental actors.

Furthermore, the aim is to validate qualitative and quantitative indicators that allow for the evaluation of coverage. resilient services, ecosystem restoration, beneficiaries, climate investment, reduction of Risks and co-benefits in sustainable development. The effectiveness of the measures could be assessed by performance matrices, case studies and ex-post evaluations, with criteria such as sustainability environmental, gender-differentiated impacts and resilience.

The subsystem also envisions promoting transparency, access to information, and participation of key actors, in accordance with Law No. 64-00, on Environment and Natural Resources and the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development. The development of related reporting protocols is projected. to SINAREI, as well as promoting the inclusion of local governments, academia, civil society and the sector private.

Finally, the sustainability of the subsystem could be ensured through the institutionalization of the Climate finance M&E unit (GEF, IDB, WB, etc.), and the continuous updating of Policies, indicators, and tools. All of this is intended as a contribution to strengthening the coherence of public policies and the maximization of the collateral benefits of adaptation, aligning with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), especially those related with health, water, reducing inequalities, climate action and governance.

10. Participatory Climate Action

Taking into consideration that NDCs constitute the main instrument of national climate planning, by guiding the prioritization of public and private investments, the mobilization of climate finance and Intersectoral collaboration for the transformation of strategic sectors. In that sense, and as part From the inclusive climate governance approach promoted in NDC 3.0, a specific process was developed consultation with the private sector, conceived as a structured mechanism for technical dialogue and generation of inputs to strengthen mitigation and adaptation measures, reinforcing their technical feasibility, financial and institutional. The workshop had significant participation from the sector, although not entirely representative of all sectors.

The overall objective of the consultation process was to incorporate technical contributions, proposed goals, and inputs. business sector operations, aimed at strengthening climate ambition, the feasibility of implementation and the co-responsibility of the private sector in national climate action.

Specifically, the process allowed:

  • Socialize the updated matrices of adaptation and mitigation measures, including the progress of the NDC 2.0 and the preliminary proposals of NDC 3.0, with their respective goals and indicators.

  • Gather input from the private sector to strengthen goals, indicators, and sources of verification and those responsible for implementation.

  • Identifying opportunities, barriers, and priorities for effective private sector participation in the Implementation of NDC 3.0, including public-private partnership schemes and access to financing climatic.

The main milestone in the consultation process with the private sector was the holding of a pre- essential, generating the following results:

  • Technical feedback from the private sector, represented in the workshop, on the mitigation matrices and adaptation, contributing to improving the coherence, feasibility and prioritization of the measures.

  • Identifying implementation gaps, regulatory barriers, and strengthening needs capacities for the effective participation of the private sector.

The Added Value of the Process for NDC 3.0 included the following aspects, which were not binding in their in its entirety to the business structure as a whole, but also as an aspiration to be achieved over time implementation of the NDCs:

  • The private sector is expected to continue contributing to achieving climate ambition with technically feasible and financially structuring measures.

  • Strengthening efforts to promote ownership of the business sector is recommended. NDC 3.0 as an instrument for planning, investment and sustainable competitiveness.

  • Structuring portfolios of climate projects with private participation.

  • The design of climate finance mechanisms and private capital mobilization, consistent with the financial instruments of multilateral partners.

  • Identifying opportunities for private sector participation in market mechanisms, technological innovation and just transition schemes.

In conclusion, the implementation of the Dominican Republic's NDC 3.0 requires involvement active, structured and sustained private sector as a key actor for the realization of the goals of mitigation and adaptation, particularly in sectors that contribute most to greenhouse gas emissions greenhouse effect and exposure to climate risks, such as energy, transport, construction, tourism, agriculture and extractive industries.

The business sector plays a complementary role in the integration of climate objectives. nationals in their corporate strategies, investment plans and value chains, promoting adoption of clean technologies, energy efficiency, the progressive decarbonization of production processes and Strengthening the climate resilience of its operations. This strategic alignment allows to translate national commitments into concrete actions at the territorial and sectoral levels, increasing the coherence between national climate planning and the dynamics of private investment.

It also contributes to the process of mobilizing climate finance and structuring projects. bankable loans aligned with the priorities of NDC 3.0, facilitating the channeling of resources from multilateral banks, climate funds, blended finance mechanisms ” ) and carbon markets, in accordance with applicable national and international regulatory frameworks. In this In this context, business participation is key to scaling up nature-based solutions. resilient infrastructure, circular economy models and low-carbon production systems, contributing to the reduction of investment gaps and the acceleration of the transition to a more sustainable economy resilient and competitive.

The role of the private sector in Dominican climate action transcends the realm of investment and is key for monitoring and accountability processes. This involves their participation in platforms of public-private dialogue, monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) systems, and co-creation spaces of policies and regulatory instruments that incentivize corporate climate action, consistent with the commitments of the Paris Agreement and the decisions of the Conferences of the Parties. An involvement A structured private sector strengthens the sustainability of NDC 3.0 goals by improving quality of its implementation and contributes to consolidating a low-carbon, resilient development model and inclusive for the Dominican Republic.

11. Information intended to facilitate clarity, transparency and understanding of contributions determined at the national level (ICTU)

According to the Paris Agreement, particularly in accordance with the Decision of the Conference of the Parties (CP) 1/CP.21, paragraph 27, Annex 1, the NDC section must present information intended to facilitate clarity, transparency and understanding, following the guidelines set out in decision 4/CMA.1. Therefore, the The Dominican Republic presents Information to Facilitate Clarity, Transparency and Understanding (ICTU, by (its acronym in English), a mechanism designed to transparently report updates to the NDCs. This This instrument provides clarity on the objectives, the methodologies applied, and the assumptions used. facilitating its understanding and international monitoring. Next, in Table 8, Dominican Republic It outlines the progress and assumptions that support NDC 3.0 RD-2025.

Table 8: Progress and assumptions supporting NDC 3.0 RD-2025. Source: Prepared by the authors.

Component 1. Mitigation Information

NDC RD 3.0

1. Quantified information on the reference point (including, where applicable, a base year, year trend, ambition and transparency in goals, others)

a. Reference year(s), base year(s), reference period(s), or other starting point(s).

For the definition of the emissions reduction target with respect to a baseline scenario, corresponding to The projection of emissions to the year 2035 under a trend or Business As Usual (BAU) scenario was taken For reference, the most recent information available from the National Gas Inventories of Greenhouse Effect (INGEI).

The construction of the baseline was based on the historical series of the INGEI for the years 1990, 1994, 1996, 2000, 2010, 2015 and 2022. Based on these series, emission projections were developed for 2030 and 2035 using technical parameters and specific sectoral considerations, configuring Thus, the BAU scenario. This exercise resulted in projected emissions of 51,949.62 kt CO₂ eq for 2030 and 57,292.65 kt CO₂ eq for 2035, values that constitute the baseline reference for defining the ambition level of NDC 3.0.

Also considered was the goal set in the RD 2020 NDC and its 2022–2023 Action Plan, which defines a ambition to reduce emissions by 27% compared to projected emissions by 2030. This goal was used as starting point and strategic reference for the formulation of the corresponding new level of ambition to the period 2026 to 2035.

b. Quantifiable information on the reference indicators, their values in the year(s) of re- reference, base year(s), reference period(s) or other starting point(s) and, as appropriate, in the target year.

The Dominican Republic has carried out a process of improvement and updating of NDC 3.0 RD-2025 and has expressed its ambition in GHG indicators, non-GHG targets, and the incorporation of enabling frameworks for the sectors with mitigation potential that require expedited implementation.

The Dominican Republic is increasing its ambition to achieve a 28% reduction in emissions by 2030 and 32% by 2035 of projected emissions in the business-as-usual (BAU) scenario, with targets unconditioned and conditioned.

This implies a reduction in emissions in 2030 of 14,732.10 kt CO2 eq (higher than the 13,853.71 kt CO2 eq committed in NDC 2.0) and 18,349.32 in the year 2035.

For reference to the current level of emissions, the most recent national inventory estimates emissions of 45,327 kt CO₂ eq in 2022, used as the reference year for monitoring the evolution of emissions.

The difference observed between the ambition described in NDC-RD 2020 and NDC 3-0 –RD 2025 lies in the improvement of the baseline estimate (incorporation of more data into the projection), as well as by the reassessment of the policies, measures and actions (PMAs) proposed by the sectors, who have identified new opportunities in light of technological changes and improved conditions.

c. For the strategies, plans and actions referred to in Article 4, paragraph 6, of the Pa- Agreement ris, or policies and measures as components of the NDCs.

To establish the level of ambition, various national instruments were considered Planning and public policy. The main strategic frameworks include:

  • Economic Development Plan Compatible with Climate Change (DECCC Plan, 2011).

  • National Development Strategy (END), Law 112.

  • National Climate Change Policy, Decree 269 15.

  • Third National Communication on Climate Change (TCNCC, 2017).

  • Dominican Republic's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC RD, 2020).

  • NDC Action Plan with evaluation for the period 2022 to 2023.

  • First Biennial Update Report (BUR, 2020).

  • Biennial Report on Transparency and National Communication (BTR/CN4), especially the chapters on inventories, monitoring and progress of the NDC.

  • Pluriannual Public Investment Plan 2025 to 2028.

  • Other relevant national climate and sectoral planning documents.

In addition, sectoral plans and medium and long-term strategies were incorporated. linked to climate action, such as:

  • Goal 2036.

  • Compendium of the National Energy Plan 2025 to 2038.

  • Preliminary long-term operating program for the electrical system published by the Agency Coordinator.

  • Updated Integrated Public Transportation System Plan 2025.

  • Sustainable Urban Mobility Plan for Greater Santo Domingo.

  • Roadmap for the Cement Industry.

  • Implementation of the Kigali Amendment pursuant to Act 233 20.

  • Application of AWD practices for emissions reduction in rice cultivation.

  • National Comprehensive Waste Management Plan 2025 to 2035.

The Dominican Republic's mitigation contributions will be implemented through a set integrated set of national strategies, sectoral plans, policies and measures that operationalize the national climate goals. Key actions include expanding renewable energy, energy efficiency programs, reduction of HFCs in RAC chain equipment, electrification of transport, sustainable waste management, industrial technological improvements and Strengthening forest carbon sinks. Implementation will be supported by the NDC Action Plan and by sectoral programs that establish implementation routes, institutional arrangements and mechanisms for Follow-up. These actions will be updated progressively as capacities are strengthened. technical, regulatory and institutional, and the country will continue to prepare, communicate and maintain successive updates of its Nationally Determined Contribution in accordance with the objectives projected to 2035.

d. Target related to the reference indicator, expressed numerically, related to the percentage or amount of reduction.

The Dominican Republic commits to reducing CO2 emissions by 28%, equivalent to 14,732.10 kt, by 2030. 32% equivalent to 18,349.32 kt CO2 eq for 2035, compared to those estimated under the scenario trend (BAU scenario), which for 2030 is expected to be 51,949.62 kt CO2 eq and for 2035 is expected at 57,292.65 kt CO2 eq, contributing to the fulfillment of the global objectives of the Agreement Paris.

e. Information on the data sources used to quantify the benchmarks.

The quantification of the reference points was based on historical data from the National Greenhouse Gas Inventory, harmonized and validated by the institutions competent national reports, and expressed in CO₂ equivalent to ensure comparability with reports Previous data were used. Historical series for the period 1990 to 2022 were supplemented with information national sectoral and methodological parameters consistent with the IPCC 2006 guidelines, its 2019 refinement and AR6. The projections were developed using statistical methods applied to the continuation of historical trends, including polynomial regression, annual growth rates composed of different configurations and mobile approaches, with performance evaluation through Statistical indicators of fit and error. This approach made it possible to generate consistent trajectories by gas for CO₂, CH₄, N₂O, HFC and CO2 removals, constituting the quantitative basis of the scenario trend used in NDC 3.0.

Reference values may be updated when inventory recalculations are available. national, improvements in the quality or coverage of sectoral data, incorporation of new factors of issuance, methodological revisions aligned with IPCC updates, or integration of variables economic, technological, or demographic factors can be considered in the projection models. These can also be reviewed in the context of new versions of official statistics, strengthening of information on removals forestry, improvements in the estimation of HFC emissions associated with the implementation of the Amendment of Kigali, or in future updates to the Nationally Determined Contribution and reporting processes under the Enhanced Transparency Framework.
