    Annex 5: Sources of Financing

    Conceptual framework

    With the aim of achieving the relatively low market penetration of low-tech and low-cost practices To reverse carbon emissions, specific factors related to low-carbon investments need to be addressed and modified. carbon intensity, as well as infrastructure resilient to the impacts of climate change.

    According to Frisari et al. (CPI 2013), risk, whether real or perceived (subjective in nature), is the factor The most relevant singularity in terms of preventing (or facilitating) projects from finding funding or making them Investors demand higher returns on their investments. These are the criteria for decision-making. These are mainly due to the perceived risk associated with the type of projects – in the absence of quantitative metrics measured or calculated by independent third parties.

    Investments in productive activities or in low-carbon or carbon-resilient infrastructure climate, they tend to be subject to higher perceptions of risk, due to a certain dependence on the performance of those projects within the context of the current regulatory frameworks.

    Also, with some frequency, risk is associated with and depends on the relative immaturity of the markets in which that those are promoted, as well as the innovative nature of the technologies themselves that are used (Frisari et al., 2013). Therefore, in this area, credibility and predictability of public policies.

    Development finance institutions (DFIs) face the challenge of contributing to boosting the Low-carbon investments in developing countries, making it possible, in particular, for businesses small and medium-scale projects can be implemented in greater numbers, thereby strengthening the process of acquisition of knowledge, new technologies, development of new infrastructure or adaptation of the existing.

    In any case, in order for financial and regulatory instruments to be applied and achieve To have tangible impacts on private investment decisions, it is necessary to create suitable conditions for the adoption of these decisions in areas that extend beyond the control of the private decision-maker himself.

    The The conditions that make investment possible and encourage depend on measures introduced within the framework of the policies aimed at promoting greater private participation in investment , which include:

    • the encouragement of the development of technology provider networks and service networks;

    • the contribution to expanding the capacity of commercial banks to meet these types of demands financing and the introduction of innovative financial instruments, including policy measures credit lines established by central banks;

    • the stimulus to accelerate the learning curve in these green segments, so that they allow for increased the social capital of countries and mobilize national resources with a view to becoming firmly included in the path to sustainable development;

    • the stability of policies, regulatory and tax frameworks.

    The policies and measures that may be put into effect usually affect the environment for investments in the area. at least three different ways. Indeed, Policies condition: the allocation of costs and revenues; the allocation of risks; and the practices of business and the choice of technologies.

    These impacts can modify investor behavior to some degree by transforming the risks to which they They face challenges and the returns they can obtain, as well as the information they access and, eventually, the criteria they use in making investment decisions. It should be noted that, even in the context of a an environment that is supposed to facilitate investments such as the one described, the introduction of instruments Appropriate financial resources, however, remain key.

    A. Climate finance

    He Climate finance refers to capital—from both public and private sources—used to facilitate mitigation actions by helping to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and Implement efforts to improve adaptability and increase resilience in the face of the negative impacts of climate change.

    Climate finance plays a fundamental role in the long-term transition to a low-cost economy carbon-neutral and resilient to climate change. Currently, the recent shift towards the adoption of trails Net-zero emissions by a large number of countries creates additional demand for financial flows to address the needs of developing countries in order to position socio-technical transitions Proposals that would allow us to achieve that goal in the long term. Sustainable infrastructure is fundamental. to achieve the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and the climate goals of the Paris Agreement.

    There is a wide variety of climate finance sources, with different funders and approaches. diverse objectives, criteria, and processes. As the effects of climate change continue to create greater Despite challenges and threats, more stakeholders are mobilizing to invest and contribute to various funds and channels. of financing dedicated to mitigating and adapting to climate change. At one end of the spectrum is purely private, purely commercial investors are found who are primarily motivated by the Risk-adjusted expected rate of return on your investment. This type of market-driven investment It is virtually indistinguishable from commercial loans for any other type of project, as in the case of bank loans for financially viable renewable energy projects. From the perspective of For lenders, climate impacts can be seen as an added benefit, but they are not the primary driver of the investment decision. At the other end of the spectrum are non-profit investors, including non-governmental and philanthropic organizations, which can grant subsidies for activities climate-related with little or no expectation of financial return. In between are a number of public and private funders who place varying degrees of emphasis on achieving climate outcomes that in obtaining financial benefits.

    Currently, access, eligibility criteria, and monitoring, reporting, and verification (MRV) frameworks They differ considerably among the multiple sources of funding for the fight against climate change. The different sources will have specific approaches and criteria that will influence the types of activities that They will finance. Some of the most common features to consider include:

    • Thematic approaches: Some only grant loans to specific sectors (e.g., energy) renewables, public transport, agriculture, water and sanitation) or for activities that generate results specific (e.g., biodiversity, soil degradation, disaster risk reduction). Some Funders may support only mitigation or only adaptation, although most of the largest sources major ones finance both.

    • Geographical approaches: This includes, for example, specific funds that invest only in countries or regions specifics.

    • Focus on the borrower: Some sources only lend to public sector agents, others only to the private sector. private and others prioritize public-private partnership projects.

    • Direct lenders versus intermediaries: Some sources lend and accept proposals at the level of project (i.e., direct access), while others invest through public funds or bodies or private entities (at the national level or through IFDs) that in turn invest in projects and programs (i.e., intermediaries).

    • Types of support: Some support the preparation or execution of projects or programs, while others are They focus on technical assistance and capacity building to improve the overall enabling environment for access climate finance.

    Traditionally, public funds have been the primary source of capital available for financing the fight against climate change, including the financial resources of multilateral development banks (BMD), multilateral and bilateral organizations, governments, climate funds and agencies of help.

    • Bilateral Sources and Channels: Bilateral financial institutions are institutions, agencies, or funds primarily belonging to or governed by a single country. This includes bilateral IFDs and development cooperation agencies of individual countries. These institutions are capitalized through from the sponsoring country's public budget, supplemented by its own funds and funds obtained in the global capital markets. Examples of bilateral channels for climate finance include the Germany's International Climate Initiative (IKI), the International Financing commitment on the UK's International Climate Fund (ICF), Germany's Global Climate Partnership Fund (GCPF), and the Norway's International Climate and Forest Initiative (NICFI), among many others.

    • Multilateral sources and channels: Multilateral financial institutions and funds have multiple Governing members, including both borrowing developing countries and donor countries developed. This includes multilateral development banks, which can be global in scope (by for example, the World Bank Group) or regional (for example, the Inter-American Development Bank), as well as Other regional institutions and United Nations (UN) agencies. The funds of these institutions. Multilateral funds come from various sources, such as the capitalization of member governments, fees for services provided and income from loans.

    • Climate funds: These are climate-specific funds, often from multiple donors, created and managed by national, bilateral and multilateral organizations that provide fiduciary services and administrative. In addition to the money pledged by donors, many of these funds leverage significant administrative resources. funding sums, often from IFDs, and by reducing investment risk to mobilize private financing Additionally, multilateral climate funds play a crucial role in assisting Developing countries must adopt low-carbon development pathways and adapt to the adverse effects of climate change. Some examples of climate funds are (i) Donor funds global standards established by UN agencies, such as the Global Environment Facility (GEF), the Climate Investment Funds (CIFs) and the Green Climate Fund (GCF); (ii) other donor funds global, such as the Global Energy Efficiency and Renewable Energy Fund (GEEREF); and (iii) regional and national climate funds and channels, such as those established by several developing countries, with a variety of forms and functions, funded through international funding and/or allocations national budgets, as well as by the national private sector.

    However, The cost of climate change mitigation and adaptation increasingly exceeds the amount of funds Available public funds, coupled with the fiscal constraints of developing countries, result in a global need to increase and capitalize funds from the private sector and from private sources such as commercial financial institutions, philanthropic organizations, investors institutional, non-profit organizations, corporate actors, institutional funds, etc.

    This pressing need has led to the introduction of mixed financing ( blending ), in which public and private sources contribute to a single funding stream, helping to reduce the Investment risks for the private sector. Mixed financing refers to the alternative selective use of for high-impact projects (i.e., large infrastructure projects) where the real risks or perceived are too high to attract commercial lenders.

    Public sources

    Traditionally, the contributions of the Dominican government and international institutional actors have been the main source of financing available for climate finance, including resources financial institutions of multilateral development banks (MDBs), multilateral and bilateral organizations, climate funds and aid agencies.

    Public spending. The Dominican government contributes a significant portion of climate finance through the Public spending. This spending is mainly concentrated on capital or infrastructure expenditures, and goes aligned with the various medium- and long-term public planning and investment plans and frameworks. Larger public funds are increasingly focused on ensuring a high multiplier effect of the limited resources, which makes the financial leverage ratio a key indicator for these Sources. Public funds are essential to unlocking climate finance for the sector private, by assuming the types of risks that it is unwilling to assume, such as regulatory risks or technological.

    Bilateral channels and mechanisms: Bilateral financial institutions (BFIs) are institutions or funds that belong to or are governed primarily by individual countries. They include institutions bilateral development finance institutions (FDIs) and development cooperation agencies of countries individual. Their capitalization is carried out through the public budgets of donor countries, supplemented by bilateral banks' own funds and funds raised in the capital markets global. Examples of bilateral channels for climate finance include: IKI (International Climate Initiative) of Germany, AFD (French Development Agency) of France, JICA (Japan International Cooperation Agency), Japan, ICF (International Climate Finance) of the United Kingdom, the GCPF (Partnership Fund Global Climate Initiative (GCI) of the German Government and the NICFI (International Climate Initiative on the Climate and Forests (for its acronym in English) of Norway, among others.

    Multilateral channels and mechanisms: Multilateral financial institutions and funds have multiple governing members, which include both borrowing developing countries and donor countries developed countries. This includes multilateral development banks (MDBs) such as the Inter-American Development Bank. (IDB) and the World Bank, regional development banks, and United Nations agencies. The funds are They raise funds from various sources, including government capitalization and loan programs, as well as such as income from loans.

    Climate funds: These are climate-specific funds, usually with multiple donors, created and managed by national, bilateral and multilateral organizations that provide fiduciary services and administrative. Multilateral climate funds play a fundamental role in collaborating with developing countries to adopt low-carbon development pathways, as well as to adapt to the effects of climate change. Some examples of climate funds are:

    global donor funds established by United Nations agencies, such as the Environment Fund Global Environment Facility (GEF), Climate Investment Funds (CIF) English), the Adaptation Fund (AF) and the Green Climate Fund (GCF). acronyms in English).

    Global donor funds managed by EU institutions, such as the Global Efficiency Fund Energy and Renewable Energies (GEEREF).

    regional and national climate funds and channels, such as those established by several developing countries, with diverse forms and functions, financed through international funds and/or budget allocations nationals, as well as by the national private sector.

    Private Sources

    International climate finance has a key role to play in addressing the financial deficit and, given that public sources are too scarce to finance this transition, it It expects that additional funding will come from the private sector.

    Given the global need to increase private sector funding and the fact that the cost of Climate change mitigation and adaptation increasingly exceed the volume of available public funds, It is essential to mobilize the private sector to guarantee a transformative and long-term impact on the developing countries.

    Private funding sources include funds and savings from individuals and businesses, which are often manage, group and/or invest through intermediaries such as commercial financial institutions, portfolio management companies, asset management companies and/or pension funds, investors institutional investors, asset managers, corporate actors, institutional funds, organizations philanthropic and non-profit organizations, among others.

    Examples of these include Clarmondial, Circulate Capital, Amplifica Capital, Acumen Capital Partners LLC, and Bamboo. Capital Partners, Deetken Asset Management Inc., EcoEnterprises Fund, Social SAPI de CV, among others.

    The main reason for this type of investment is to obtain a risk-adjusted return for the investors, as well as collateral benefits, as a result of sustainable development, which may be included explicitly or implicitly in their mandate.

    Blended finance can play a vital role in unlocking, expanding, and channeling financing for sustainable development in developing countries.

    Blended finance refers to the use of public and private financing when real risks or The perceived costs are too high for commercial lenders to absorb on their own. Public funding is used to reduce investment risks for the private sector and to reduce the cost of financing. Blended financing attracts commercial capital to projects that contribute to sustainable development, while providing financial returns to investors.

    Blended finance is not an investment approach, but rather an approach to structuring transactions. in a way that attracts multiple types of investors. Blended financing allows an agency government, a private equity investor, and an impact investor invest together, at the same time who achieve their own goals.

    Impact investing is an investment approach (or philosophy) that generates both financial and social benefits. Impact on development. Impact investors often, though not always, invest in transactions of blended finance along with other types of capital. Impact investors play a role This is important because they occupy a unique space among private investors, who expect high returns. risk-adjusted, and public and philanthropic investors, who can absorb more risk and expect lower performance.

    Impact investors, on the other hand, have expectations of profitability, but are more flexible. especially if they see a fundamental impact from their investments.

    While private investors focus on obtaining a risk-adjusted rate of return, their Risk appetite can vary significantly. For example, angel investors and venture capitalists Risk managers focus on projects in earlier, higher-risk stages, so they expect a rate of Higher returns in case of success. The largest institutional investors seek projects of lower risk, but they expect relatively lower rates of return.

    Available Instruments

    Climate finance is, first and foremost, finance, so the available instruments are the Typical financial instruments that can be used for any type of financial transaction. In other words, What makes an investment climate finance is not the nature of the instrument used, but rather the fact that the investment supports activities that contribute to mitigating climate change or to the adaptation to it.

    Consequently, the four main categories of climate finance instruments that could The typical financial instruments available for any type of financial transaction are those that can be used. namely:

    1. debt instruments

    2. capital instruments

    3. credit improvement instruments; e

    4. Risk transfer instruments.

    The difference between an ordinary financial instrument and a financing instrument for the fight against [the virus/crisis] Climate change means that funds raised through climate change financing sources climate-related funds are allocated to the achievement of a specific objective, project, or physical asset related to the climate.

    The set of instruments and mechanisms to promote and facilitate sustainable investment is usually considered as green financing, although there are disagreements about the taxonomy with which it can be classified This financing needs to be classified, and strictly speaking, there is no universally agreed definition of what constitutes it. This financing is understood on a global scale. According to the Dominican Republic's Green Taxonomy, it is considered green if it “seeks to promote initiatives that support both sustainable development and the transition towards a low-carbon economy.”65

    In any case, to consolidate a strategy that allows us to cover the wide financing gap today There are three different challenges that need to be addressed:

    • Build capacity to identify appropriate programs, projects, and actions;

    • Develop programs that, within the framework provided by the regulatory framework conceived from the sector public, fully involve the private sector in systemic transformation efforts; and,

    • Structuring financing according to the needs of key stakeholders and the conditions prevalent in the priority areas of action.

    The mobilization of private resources, in particular, primarily takes into consideration the rate of return and The risks associated with planned investment often go beyond environmental priorities and sustainable development.

    Furthermore, in each case the financing instruments that are eligible may differ depending on the policy priorities and goals, the policies and measures already underway, and the national strategy that has been designed to address the main issues that the national institutional response aims to make facing climate change.

    In developing economies and, to some extent, in the Dominican Republic, the financial sector and capital markets tend to They are still weak or insufficiently developed, and the level of long-term funding is low. Without However, companies require long-term financing for the acquisition of fixed assets or for the project financing that may require a relatively long time for recovery investment, particularly in large-scale investment projects such as infrastructure; however, the Local commercial entities can often only provide short-term loan facilities to Relatively high interest rates, and in some cases, make investment unfeasible. On the other hand, Companies, particularly SMEs, do not always have a solid and consistent financial structure to provide adequate guarantees to institutions that may provide potential sources of financing.

    Therefore, This creates a need for innovative financing mechanisms to support the private sector, primarily for the segments with less access to financing , and at the same time complement the development of green finance in the country.

    In general, the added value offered by debt and equity instruments is that they allow for reducing the gap in specific financing for certain projects and programs, and with its expansion and greater ease of Access would increase the number of programs and projects that can be implemented. So, even if, despite the A considerable improvement in the cost competitiveness of renewable energy projects, for example, has not yet been achieved. This produces a massive expansion of this type of investment; it is necessary to carefully examine what the potential ones might be. Barriers to investment and ways to remove them. A set of instruments is included in the following table. financial instruments and also instruments called non-financial, but which are used in conjunction with the former, because they contribute to facilitating its application and thus achieving its massive deployment.

    Definition

    Provision of financing (Public or private source)

    Commercial and corporate banks

    Concessional loan

    Loans granted for the purpose of addressing climate change/sustainable goals, characterized by longer repayment terms, lower interest rates, and extended grace periods.

    Governments, IFDs, climate funds

    Donations

    Sums of money that are granted for activities related to climate change but that are not These funds need to be returned.     Climate funds, Governments, IFDs, public and private institutions (including philanthropic ones)

    Financing climate change mitigation or adaptation project activities by raising small amounts of capital from many individuals, usually through an online platform.

    Credit improvement instruments

    Interest Rate Smoothing Mechanisms

    Re-loan

    Obtaining loans from external or national sources and subsequent transfer of the loan to another entity in order to address climate change.

    Governments, IFD

    Refinancing

    Substitution of an existing debt obligation for another debt obligation under different conditions in order to address climate change.

    Commercial Banks, Governments, IFDs

    Project Bond Credit Enhancement

    Subordinated instrument, whether a loan or a contingent credit line, to back bonds of preferred projects issued by a project company for low-carbon projects.

    Governments, IFD

    Credit subordination: Excess spread, over-collateralization, reserve accounts, etc.

    Prioritization of secured debts, one after the other, for the purpose of collecting repayment from a debtor. Subordinated debt is riskier than higher priority loans, transferring the risk to in case the results of the climate projects are not fully achieved.

    Local and national governments

    Instruments and mechanisms for risk transfer/distribution

    Insurance

    Creation of risk transfer mechanisms that provide resources for disasters related to climate and transfer responsibility for losses to capital market investors.

    Insurance Companies

    Guarantees

    Promise to repay another's debt, in connection with an exchange-related activity climate, in case of non-payment.

    Governments, IFD

    Results-based climate finance

    The funds are disbursed by the donor or investor once the requirements have been met and verified. previously agreed results of climate activity.

    Governments, IFD

    Venture capital

    Funds (capital/loans on favorable terms) allocated to mitigation/adaptation activities to climate change with a high level of uncertainty.

    Annex 6: Participatory Process

    The update of the Dominican Republic's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 3.0) has developed through a participatory, inclusive and technically robust process, which has involved a wide range of institutional, technical and social actors.

    Led by the NDC3.0 Steering Committee, made up of the National Council on Climate Change and Markets of Carbon (CNCCMC), the Ministry of Finance and Economy (MHyE) and the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources Natural Resources (MMARN), the approach of the Dominican Republic's NDC 3.0 update process has allowed gather multi-sectoral contributions that strengthen both the mitigation and adaptation components, ensuring that the proposed goals respond to the realities of the country and its international commitments in subject of climate change.

    The NDC update process has received support from the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB), providing technical assistance and coordination, as well as organizing discussion spaces for the construction of the country's climate ambition in mitigation and adaptation. Additionally, a number of workshops were organized in coordination with the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) for the inclusion of cross-cutting considerations.

    This annex summarizes the different components of the participatory process for updating NDC3.0, presenting methodologies and main results from the different discussion spaces.

    Apart from bi-weekly coordination meetings with the NDC3.0 Steering Committee between February and In November 2025, a total of two (2) NDC dialogues were held to define the formulation of measures and transparency mechanism, as well as six (6) workshops for the inclusion of cross-cutting considerations. At the same time, a public consultation form was made available to broaden participation and gather input. This tool allowed for the incorporation of additional technical, academic, and citizen stakeholders. relevant observations on adaptation and mitigation measures, strengthening legitimacy and representativeness of the process.

    Finally, the organization of sectoral working groups and ad-hoc bilateral consultations with institutions Key and specialized technical personnel have made it possible to present a proposal with solid ambition and aligned with the sectoral interests.

    NDC Dialogues

    a. NDC Dialogue 1: Mitigation and Adaptation

    The first NDC Dialogue was held on the days June 3 and 4, 2025 in it Hotel Catalonia, Santo Domingo , and was organized by the Steering Committee, made up of the MEPyD, CNCCMDL, Ministry of Finance, Ministry of Environment, with support from the IDB and facilitation from the OCA Global – Guakía Ambiente consortium – Plenitud Foundation. This space marked the beginning of the expanded participation of key actors in the process, with the objective of reviewing and enriching the proposed mitigation and adaptation measures.

    The dialogue took place over two thematic days: Mitigation (day 1) and Adaptation (Day 2). Each day included plenary presentations, working sessions in sectoral groups (energy, water, cities, health, waste, etc.), and synthesis plenary sessions. Tools such as matrices were used Evaluation of measures, fact sheets for new proposals, and maps of key stakeholders. The methodology was participatory. results-oriented and designed to gather technical, political, and social inputs that would strengthen the NDC feasibility.

    Participants reviewed previously identified measures, assessed their relevance, feasibility, and alignment. tion with national priorities, and proposed adjustments or new actions. Responsible actors were identified. sabers, strategic allies, and enabling conditions for implementation. The final plenary session allowed share key findings and define next steps, including new spaces for consultation and validation technical. The dialogue laid the methodological groundwork for subsequent sectoral meetings and strengthened the national actors' ownership of the process.

    Figure 1 Steering Committee and IDB team at the NDC 1 Mitigation and Adaptation Dialogue.

    b. NDC Dialogue 2: Validation of the NDC component within the Enhanced Transparency Framework (ETF)

    The second NDC Dialogue was held on July 22, 2025 at the Catalonia Hotel, Santo Domingo and was Organized by the Steering Committee, with technical support from the IDB and the OCA Global – Guakía Ambiente – Fundación Plenitud consortium. The dialogue focused on validating the design of the NDC subsystem of the National Climate Transparency System (SNTC), which has been designed to meet the climate transparency commitments set out in the NDC 2025 update. The main objective was to consolidate the structure and functionality of the system national climate transparency, ensuring its alignment with international standards and priorities nationals.

    The day unfolded with a participatory and technical approach, combining informative presentations, validation and plenary discussion tools. The tools used included:

    • Structural and functional validation matrix (evaluation of inputs, flows, roles, reports and deadlines).

    • Mapping of actors and associated processes (links with systems such as SNIGC, MRV, statistics) sectoral).

    • Integration and validation plenary session, to collectively adjust the technical and operational design.

    During the plenary session, the draft NDC subsystem was reviewed, roles and responsibilities were assessed, and Improvements and gaps were identified. Participants validated the system's functionality and reinforced its meaning. of institutional affiliation and proposed adjustments to optimize inter-institutional coordination. The dialogue This allowed progress towards a robust monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) system, key to ensuring the transparency and credibility of NDC 3.0.

    Cross-cutting workshops

    a. Inclusion of Early Childhood, Childhood and Youth in NDC 3.0

    The workshop took place on Thursday July 24, 2025 at the Holiday Inn Hotel in Santo Domingo, with the participation of 64 people, of whom 70% were Women and 48% young people under 35, including people with disabilities. The event It allowed for the integration of concrete proposals to strengthen climate adaptation and mitigation measures from a children's and youth rights perspective. Through sectoral working groups (health, water, infrastructure, agriculture, energy, transport and waste), technical adjustments were formulated to Existing measures were considered, new integrative objectives were proposed, and gaps, instruments, and opportunities for networking.

    The workshop was conducted using a participatory and structured methodology that combined presentations Techniques with group dynamics. It began with presentations on the NDC 3.0 update process and the importance of integrating early childhood, childhood and youth approaches into climate action. Subsequently, five sectoral working groups were organized (health, water, infrastructure/education, agriculture/ecosystems, energy/transport/waste), where participants assumed specific roles (moderator, rapporteur, presenter) and worked with methodological guides, technical inputs and materials of support. The exercises included reviewing existing measures, formulating integrated proposals, the design of sectoral objectives with a focus on children's and youth rights, and the identification of instruments, gaps, and opportunities for collaboration. The workshop concluded with a plenary session to share the following: findings and an assessment survey.

    The workshop made it possible to generate concrete contributions to strengthen NDC 3.0 from a perspective intergenerational and rights-based. Technical adjustments were made to existing measures, such as the unification of actions in health (S3–S4), the consolidation of measures in energy (MM.E.11–MM.E.17), and the reformulation of Proposals in agriculture, ecosystems, and human settlements. Integrative objectives were formulated by sector that directly links climate action with the protection and empowerment of children and youth.

    In addition, the recommendations from the youth meeting were incorporated. “NDC Café: Youth for Our Common Destiny” , held on August 9, 2025 in Mirador Sur Park, with the participation of 25 young people. This space It allowed the visions and proposals of young people to be channeled into five thematic areas: nature and territory, Energy and mobility, production and waste, water and health, and their cross-cutting nature. Among the proposals Highlights include: mandatory environmental education at all levels, and a boost to ecotourism. community, energy model reform to facilitate distributed generation, youth participation binding in decision-making, strengthening of regenerative agriculture, modernization of the water infrastructure, and the creation of citizen oversight mechanisms.

    The youth also expressed concrete commitments such as leading local initiatives and raising awareness among their communities, reduce their carbon footprint and actively participate in the implementation and monitoring of the climate policies. The workshop was highly positive, with satisfaction levels reaching 88%. and 98% in content, methodology, organization and usefulness, which confirms the relevance of the approach and the quality of the developed process.

    Figure 2 – NDC Coffee.

    b. Action for Climate Empowerment

    The workshop was held on Thursday, July 31, 2025 at the Hotel Catalonia, Brussels Room, in Santo Domingo, and was attended by 65 people registered, of which 74% were women, 33% were young people under 35 years of age, and the presence was included of two people with disabilities. The sectors represented were mainly the public sector (64%), followed by international cooperation (19%), civil society and academia (10%), and the private sector (7%).

    The day combined technical presentations with participatory activities. Three presentations were given. introductory sessions on the NDC 3.0 process and the ACE approach, followed by a thematic working group session organized by the six elements of ACE: education, training, awareness, public access to information, public participation and international cooperation. Each table had supporting materials and defined roles (moderator, rapporteur, presenter), and They developed three main exercises: review of progress and proposals for improvement to the ACE goals, formulation of new goals articulated with key sectors, and identification of instruments, gaps and opportunities for networking.

    The workshop allowed for the validation and enrichment of the diagnosis of the NDC 2.0 ACE goals, proposing adjustments and New measures for NDC 3.0. Among the most relevant results are: strengthening education climate change as a compulsory subject at all educational levels; implement a national program of climate training certified by INFOTEP; develop awareness campaigns with goals for 2027 and 2030, including accessible media and actors such as influencers and community radio stations; create a national repository of open data on climate change; establish a An inclusive Citizen Consultation Council and a National Youth Network on Climate Change; and to strengthen the international cooperation mechanisms. The workshop was highly positive, with levels of Satisfaction between 83% and 97% in content, methodology, organization and usefulness.

    c. Gender

    The workshop was held on Thursday, August 7, 2025 at the Hotel Catalonia, Brussels Room, in Santo Domingo, and 67 people participated, of whom 87% They were women, 43% were young people under 35 years old, and at least two people were present with disability. The representation was multisectoral: 70% from the public sector, 10% from international cooperation, 13% civil society and academia, and 7% private sector.

    The workshop combined technical presentations with participatory activities. Progress on the Roundtable was discussed. National Gender and Climate Change Council and the Gender and Climate Change Action Plan (PAGCC) was presented. Subsequently, they were organized sectoral working groups to review the measures proposed in NDC 3.0, identify links with the PAGCC and formulate integrative objectives with a gender focus. The sectors addressed were: energy and waste, transport and infrastructure, health and risk management, water, agriculture and food security, tourism and coastal-marine resources. Each working group participated in three exercises: formulating objectives, reviewing existing measures and proposing new actions, considering public policy instruments, gaps and opportunities for collaboration.

    Figure 3 Gender Workshop.

    The workshop allowed for the validation and strengthening of the integration of the gender approach into sectoral measures of NDC 3.0. Specific adjustments were proposed in sectors such as energy, waste, transport, health, water, agriculture and tourism, including women's participation in projects, generation of disaggregated data by gender, and promotion of accessible and resilient infrastructure. Recommendations from [the relevant body] were also incorporated. “NDC Coffee” youth meeting, as a boost to community ecotourism and ecosystem protection coastal areas. The workshop was positively evaluated, with satisfaction levels between 79% and 96% in content, methodology, organization, and utility.

    The institutional diversity, the quality of the discussions, and the need to improve certain aspects were highlighted. logistics and extend the time for group dynamics.

    d. Private Sector

    The workshop took place on Friday, August 8, 2025 , at the Catalonia Santo Domingo Hotel, as part of the process of updating the Determined Contribution at the National Level (NDC 3.0) of the Dominican Republic. It was led by ECORED, the consortium of OCA Global – Guakía Ambiente – Fundación Plenitud and UNDP , with the participation of more than 60 representatives from the private sector, including companies, trade associations, NGOs and consultants. The main objective was to incorporate proposals and goals that strengthen adaptation measures and mitigation of NDC 3.0, recognizing the strategic role of the business sector in climate action national.

    Figure 4 Private sector workshop.

    The workshop was conducted through a combination of technical presentations and participatory activities. They presented the background of the NDC process and the financial impact of climate change on the sector. productive, and the opportunities for the private sector in the fight against climate change. Subsequently, they were organized thematic working groups (energy, transport, health, waste, biodiversity, among others), where participants reviewed the NDC 2.0 measures, proposed improvements, identified new actions and Enabling conditions were discussed. Digital forms, printed matrices, and roles were used. defined to facilitate discussion and systematize contributions.

    The workshop allowed for the validation and enhancement of existing measures, proposing 20 new actions (7 of mitigation and 13 adaptation measures) and modifications to 37 measures of NDC 2.0. Recommendations were highlighted such as: incorporating mandatory storage in renewable energy facilities, promoting electric micromobility, to implement circular economy plans and strengthen environmental management in hospitals. Furthermore, it was proposed a monitoring and accountability system with specific indicators to measure involvement business. The evaluation survey reflected high satisfaction with the content and methodology, and It identified the need for more time for discussion and institutional strengthening. The conclusions They aim to institutionalize a permanent public-private working group, improve regulatory frameworks, and financial, and ensure the integration of gender and ACE in all measures.

    e. Local Communities

    The days August 5 and 6, 2025 Two workshops were held at the National Botanical Garden of Santo Domingo and the Botanical Garden of Santiago, with the objective of strengthening the participation of rural and mountain communities in the updating of the Dominican Republic's NDC 3.0. The activity It was organized by Guakía Ambiente with the support of the UNDP, and had the participation of 80 people from 16 provinces, including 25 Community-Based Organizations (CBOs) and 11 NGOs. The approach It was aligned with Articles 6 and 7 of the Paris Agreement, promoting inclusive participation and effective.

    Each workshop was structured in three parts: presentation and introduction, group work, and plenary session. Summary. It began with a contextualization of the NDC process and its relevance to communities, followed by through the analysis of 49 measures distributed across five sectors: water resources and health, food security and resilient cities, ecosystems and tourism, energy and waste, and land use and forestry. The groups had facilitators and analysis guides, and the IPPU sector was excluded due to its low incidence community. In the plenary session, observations and recommendations were shared to strengthen inclusion territorial in the NDC.

    Local communities highlighted the importance of all measures, with an emphasis on adaptation due to their High vulnerability. Integrated watershed management and access to basic services were prioritized. inclusive rural areas, and the need for local solid waste management plans. It was also proposed include a strategic water management plan, facilities for renewable energy, and a regime of Consequences for regulatory non-compliance. The sector recommendations included proposals specific to energy, waste, tourism, resilient cities and food security, highlighting the role of the communities as overseers of the territory. It was concluded that community integration strengthens the legitimacy, viability and resilience of the NDC.

    Figure 5 Local communities workshop at the Santiago Botanical Garden.

    f. Water, Sanitation and Hygiene

    The workshop took place on August 19 at the Gran Almirante Hotel, Santiago de los Caballeros, with the objective of generating technical inputs and, recognizing the role of the Drinking Water and Sanitation (DWS) sector in adapting to climate change. Fifty-two people participated, with a composition of 61% men and 39% women, and a representation interinstitutional, comprising 67% public sector, 14% international cooperation, 18% civil society and academia, and 1% private sector. The activity was coordinated by the WASH Group, the CNCC and UNICEF, with support from UNDP and IDB.

    The day combined technical presentations on climate change, risks in the APS sector, and the Index of Safe Aqueduct, with working sessions in thematic tables on Health, Resilient Cities, Resources Water and food security. Participants reviewed sectoral matrices and formulated Recommendations based on their institutional capacities. The methodology was assessed as clear, The survey was participatory and effective, although it was suggested that more time and prior materials be available. Satisfaction showed results between 89% and 96% in content, facilitation, logistics and usefulness.

    The technical findings included specific proposals for each sector. Regarding water resources, the following recommendations were made: Define priority basins by 2030, clarify goals, and consider hydrometeorological risks. Food security was highlighted, along with water efficiency, climate-smart livestock farming, and coordination with existing projects. In health, it was proposed to apply the Safe Hospitals frameworks and WASH-FIT, ensuring safe water in emergencies and strengthening quality monitoring. In resilient cities, He emphasized climate-adapted urban drainage, the right to resilient housing, and the use of risk maps. climate displacement monitoring. The consultation was rated as highly useful and enriching, with Recommendations for replicating the model in other locations and strengthening the participation of decision-makers.

    Figure 6 Water, Sanitation and Hygiene Workshop.

    Working Groups

    The sectoral working groups were designed as technical spaces for consultation and co-construction to The update of the Dominican Republic's NDC 3.0. Representatives from various institutions participated. public, private, academic, international cooperation and civil society, organized by key sectors: energy, transport, waste, agriculture, LUTUs, IPPU and health.

    During these sessions, proposals for sectoral mitigation goals were presented and validated, many of them These are derived from previous studies, specialized technical assistance, and institutional commitments. A supplementary technical document systematizes these goals in narrative and tabular formats, including indicators, monitoring methodologies, emissions reduction estimates and requirements of financing.

    As part of the process of structuring the national portfolio of climate actions, the following were carried out Sectoral meetings with key stakeholders in the Energy, Transport, LULUCF and Agriculture sectors, IPPU and Waste. These working groups were convened by the National Steering Committee, with the objective of To review, validate, and enhance the climate policies, measures, and actions proposed in each sector. Representatives from more than 20 institutions participated. public, private and international cooperation, including MEM, CNE, INTRANT, MICM, INAPA, ADIE, FEDOMU, JICA, UNDP, CNCCMC, among others.

    The sessions were developed with a hierarchical structure that facilitated the analysis of coherence between Strategic and operational levels: policy objective, goal, measure, and action. Tools such as Evaluation matrices, action sheets, stakeholder mapping, and indicator review. The indicators are They classified them into three types: impact (emissions avoided), effect (intermediate changes) and product (deliverables) physical). The meetings were held in a hybrid format, with in-person sessions in the IDB's meeting rooms and virtual participation.

    The methodology of the roundtables was based on the review of sectoral progress, identification of gaps, Prioritization of measures and validation of preliminary goals. Technical feasibility criteria were applied. alignment with national policies, consistency with IPCC methodologies and emissions reduction potential.

    The goals were structured in technical data sheets that include: title, objective, responsible entity, type instrument (GHG, non-GHG, enabling framework), progress status, IPCC category, gases involved, estimated funding, description of the option, monitoring methodology, required activities and observations. This systematization allowed for a comparative evaluation between sectors and facilitated the Integration of goals within the framework of NDC 3.0.

    The working groups facilitated the agreement on more than 40 sectoral mitigation goals, with reduction estimates of Emissions exceeding 10 million tons of CO₂eq by 2030. Flagship measures were identified such as the incorporation of natural gas and renewables into the electricity system, public transport corridors efficient, technical closure of landfills, biodigestion in livestock farming, water management in rice paddies and mangrove conservation.

    Among the most outstanding results are:

    • Energy sector: targets for new natural gas and renewable energy generation units, improvement of electrical distribution system, public lighting efficiency and RAC standards.

    • Transportation: bus corridors, monorails, cable cars, freight fleet renewal and strategy scrapping.

    • IPPU: Decarbonization of the cement sector and reduction of HFCs in refrigeration and air conditioning.

    • Agriculture: biodigestion, composting, methane reduction in livestock, and water management in rice.

    • UTCUTS: Restoration of 4,500 km² of degraded forest and protection of mangroves in protected areas.

    • Waste: technical closure of Duquesa landfill, waste valorization, improvement of wastewater treatment in cities tourist.

    • Cross-cutting: updating technical standards (NORDOM) and strengthening evaluation schemes of the accordance.

    These goals were validated by sector stakeholders and will be integrated as part of the mitigation component. of NDC 3.0, with a focus on results, transparency and climate finance.

    Bilateral consultations

    As part of the NDC 3.0 update process, the following was carried out ad hoc bilateral consultations with key institutions and specialized technical staff to review and validate the proposed adaptation and mitigation measures. These meetings were held from shape hybrid and in-person , allowing direct interaction with strategic sectors.

    The active role of institutions linked to health, resilient cities, and biodiversity is highlighted. Water security, tourism, and especially the coastal-marine sector, given its high vulnerability and relevance for national climate resilience. These consultations complemented the sectoral working groups and They allowed for the incorporation of specific technical observations into the final definition of the adaptation goals.

    The bilateral meetings were organized ad hoc, with a technical and participatory approach. Each session was It focused on reviewing the measures previously included in the current NDC and contrasting them with new proposals. sectoral and gather comments, corrections and suggestions from institutional actors.

    The data obtained was systematized in an Excel matrix that consolidated the contributions by sector, allowing Its cross-sectional analysis and the integration of adjustments into global adaptation and mitigation goals. This matrix It served as the basis for the final validation of the measures, ensuring technical coherence, sectoral relevance and alignment with national planning frameworks.

    The bilateral consultations helped strengthen the mitigation and adaptation component of NDC 3.0, incorporating more precise and contextualized measures. Key findings include:

    • Water security: Integrated watershed management is prioritized, focusing on use planning consistent with the characteristics of the territory and the water security of the established human settlements.

    • Food Security: Efficient water management, silvopastoral systems and livestock farming are prioritized. climate smart.

    • Health: Priority is given to measures that influence the environmental determinants of health, infrastructure sustainable and resilient (co-benefits with mitigation), training and governance

    • Ecosystems, Biodiversity and Forests: The ecosystem approach is prioritized, aimed at safeguarding the functionality of natural systems and their dynamics, promoting connectivity between ecosystems and the adaptation to climate change.

    • Coastal-marine resources: Adaptive management of priority areas and conservation actions are prioritized and restoration

    • Tourism: The goals were evaluated and restructured to align them with the new tourism strategies sustainable and ecotourism sector

    Form

    As part of the NDC3.0 update process, a open inquiry form to include actors who were unable to participate in the working groups or bilateral consultations. The tool allowed for the collection of technical input, perceptions, and recommendations from representatives of institutions. public (44%), private (20%), international cooperation (22%), civil society (11%) and the financial sector (3%), broadening the participatory base of the process. In total, 36 responses were received, representing all sectors and a balanced distribution by gender and age range.

    The form included closed and open questions on key NDC topics: perception of progress, role institutional, sectoral priorities, adaptation and mitigation measures, climate finance, participation, monitoring, and recommendations to improve the effectiveness of the NDC. The responses were systematized and analyzed by sector, gender, age, and type of vulnerability. Criteria were applied qualitative and quantitative analysis, allowing the identification of patterns, concrete proposals and common barriers.

    Participants highlighted the following sectors as priorities for climate action: energy, water security, Solid waste, transportation, ecosystems, and agriculture. The main adaptation measures implemented These were: education and awareness, ecosystem restoration, water management, and climate-smart agriculture intelligent. The most frequently mentioned vulnerabilities were droughts, extreme events, loss of biodiversity and Floods. Regarding participation, mechanisms such as digital platforms and roundtables were proposed. territorial aspects, public consultations, and climate observatories. The recommendations included strengthening territorial aspects, public consultations, and climate observatories. technical capabilities, improved access to financing, integration of nature-based solutions, and ensuring inclusive and transparent governance.

    48. In the context of the Dominican Republic, green jobs will be understood as those dedicated to the productive activities that, according to the green taxonomy, do not generate significant damage to the environment environment and that are considered to be of substantial contribution, enabling or transitional oriented to contribute to the achievement of environmental and climate objectives in line with commitments national and international adaptation and mitigation measures to climate change and, in turn, promote a transition towards a fairer and more inclusive economy. This definition has been developed and agreed upon by in a participatory manner by the different actors in the world of work in the country, and constitutes a basis common for the identification, measurement and promotion of green jobs within the framework of NDC 3.0. ↩
